Derived Projectivizations of complexes
Qingyuan Jiang

Abstract
. In this paper, we study the counterpart of Grothendieck’s projectivization construction in the context of derived algebraic geometry. Our main results are as follows: First, we define the derived projectivization of a connective complex, study its fundamental properties such as finiteness properties and functorial behaviors, and provide explicit descriptions of their relative cotangent complexes. We then focus on the derived projectivizations of complexes of perfect-amplitude contained in [ 0 , 1 ] [0,1] . In this case, we prove a generalized Serre’s theorem, a derived version of Beilinson’s relations, and establish semiorthogonal decompositions for their derived categories. Finally, we show that many moduli problems fit into the framework of derived projectivizations, such as moduli spaces that arise in Hecke correspondences. We apply our results to these situations.



1.
Introduction

Grothendieck’s construction of projectivizations plays a vital role in classical algebraic geometry. For example, projectivizations and their properties are crucial for defining projective schemes and morphisms and studying their properties. In this paper, we study the counterpart of Grothendieck’s construction in the framework of derived algebraic geometry.
Our main results are as follows:

(1)

For a derived scheme
X
(or more generally, a prestack
X
) and a connective quasi-coherent sheaf
\mathscr{E}
on
X
:

(i)

we will define a (relative) derived scheme
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})
over
X
, called the
derived projectivization
of
\mathscr{E}
over
X
(§
4.2
), whose underlying classical scheme agrees with Grothendieck’s construction for the classical truncation (Proposition
4.21
).

(ii)

We study the fundamental properties of the derived projectivizations, including the finiteness properties (Proposition
4.26
) and their functorial behaviors under base change, tensoring with line bundles (Proposition
4.25
), and quotient maps (Corollary
4.32
, Proposition
4.33
). We also explicitly describe their relative cotangent complexes by proving a generalized Euler sequence (Theorem
4.27
).

(2)

In the case where
\mathscr{E}
has perfect-amplitude contained in
[0,1]
, the derived projectivization
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})
enjoys special pleasant features. In this case:

(i)

We will prove a generalized Serre’s theorem for
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})
(Theorem
5.6
).

(ii)

We show that
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})
has a natural pair of dual relative exceptional sequences which satisfies a derived version of Beilinson’s relations (Proposition
6.19
).

(iii)

We establish a semiorthogonal decomposition of the
\infty
-category
\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}))
of quasi-coherent sheaves on
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})
(Theorem
7.5
).

(3)

We apply the our results to various situations:

(i)

(Classical situations) For example, we could apply these results and obtain:

•

Semiorthogonal decompositions of the derived categories of schemes of the form
X\coprod\nolimits_{D}\mathbb{P}_{D}^{1}
, where
D\subset X
is any effective Cartier divisor, or the reducible schemes appearing as the central fiber of deformation to normal cones; see §
1.3.1
.

•

Descriptions of derived categories under stabilization maps of nodal curves (§
1.3.2
).

•

Semiorthogonal decompositions of derived categories of certain threefolds with rational double points (§
1.3.3
).

•

Examples of derived equivalences of classical schemes induced by non-trivially derived schemes (§
1.3.4
).

(ii)

(Moduli problems) Many moduli spaces have natural derived enhancements which fit into the framework of derived projectivizations, such as moduli spaces that arise in Hecke correspondences and moduli of extensions.

•

We study the situations of Hecke correspondences for schemes, and apply our construction and results to this situation (§
8
).

•

In
[
J23b
]
, we apply the results of this paper to study the derived Abel maps for integral curves to their compactified Jacobians, and provide semiorthogonal decompositions of the derived Hilbert schemes of points on integral curves.

Remark 1.1

(Conventions)
.

This paper works in the framework of derived algebraic geometry and uses
Lurie’s terminology
(§
1.5
, §
1.7
). Most results of this paper require no conditions on the base
X
: it could be arbitrarily stacky or derived (or singular). However, since the projectivization is a “relative phenomenon”, the readers might feel free to assume
X
to be a nice classical (underived) scheme and still get the essence of most of the results in this introduction. If one wishes to assume that
X
is a classical Noetherian scheme, beware the following
distinctions of conventions
between derived and classical algebraic geometry:

•

A
quasi-coherent sheaf

\mathscr{E}
on
X
in the derived context (as in this paper) corresponds to an
unbounded complex

\mathscr{E}_{*}=[\,\cdots\to\mathscr{E}_{1}\to\mathscr{E}_{0}\to\mathscr{E}_{-1}\to\cdots\,]
of quasi-coherent sheaves on
X
in the classical sense. The
i
th homotopy group

\pi_{i}(\mathscr{E})
corresponds to the sheaf (co)homology
\mathscr{H}_{i}(\mathscr{E}_{*})=\mathscr{H}^{-i}(\mathscr{E}_{*})
. We say
\mathscr{E}
is
connective
if satisfies
\pi_{i}(\mathscr{E})=\mathscr{H}_{i}(\mathscr{E}_{*})\simeq 0
for all
i<0
, and we say
\mathscr{E}
is
discrete
if
\pi_{i}(\mathscr{E})=\mathscr{H}_{i}(\mathscr{E}_{*})\simeq 0
for all
i\neq 0
. In particular,
discrete
quasi-coherent sheaves on
X
correspond to the usual quasi-coherent sheaves on
X
in the classical sense.

•

The collection of all quasi-coherent sheaves on
X
is organized into a stable
\infty
-category
\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
, whose homotopy category is the usual derived category
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(X)
of quasi-coherent complexes on
X
. The subcategory of discrete quasi-coherent sheaves, denoted by
\operatorname{QCoh}(X)^{\heartsuit}
, is equivalent to the (nerve of the) usual abelian category
{\rm qcoh}(X)
in the classical setting.

•

\mathrm{Perf}(X)
denotes the stable
\infty
-category of perfect objects of
\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
, whose homotopy category is the full triangulated subcategory
\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{perf}}(X)
of
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(X)
spanned by perfect complexes.

•

A quasi-coherent sheaf
\mathscr{E}\in\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
is said to have
perfect-amplitude (contained) in
[0,1]
if it is represented by a perfect complex
\mathscr{E}_{*}
on
X
of homological Tor-amplitude in
[0,1]
(or equivalently, of cohomological Tor-amplitude in
[-1,0]
) in the classical sense.

1.1.
Derived projectivizations

Let
X
be a classical scheme, and let
\mathscr{E}
be a discrete quasi-coherent sheaf on
X
. Then Grothendieck’s projectivization of
\mathscr{E}
over
X
, which we shall denote as
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{cl}}(\mathscr{E})=\operatorname{Proj}\operatorname{Sym}_{\mathrm{cl}}^{*}\mathscr{E}
, is the scheme over
X
which represents the following functor: for any morphism of schemes
\eta\colon T\to X
, the set of
T
-points
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{cl}}(\mathscr{E})(\eta)
over
\eta
is the set of equivalence classes of rank-one locally free quotients of
\mathscr{E}
.

Let
X
be a derived scheme (or more generally, a prestack), and let
\mathscr{E}
be a connective quasi-coherent sheaf on
X
. We define the
derived projectivization
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})
of
\mathscr{E}
over
X
via a similar manner as above, that is, it is the following functor:

•

For every map
\eta\colon T\to X
from a
derived
scheme
T
, the space of
T
-points
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})(\eta)
over
\eta
is the space of maps of quasi-coherent sheaves
\eta^{*}\mathscr{E}\to\mathscr{L}
which induce a surjection
\pi_{0}(\eta^{*}\mathscr{E})\twoheadrightarrow\pi_{0}(\mathscr{L})

, where
\mathscr{L}
is a line bundle on
T
.

(See §
4.2
for more details.) More informally, we could think of
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})
as the
derived
moduli space which parametrizes rank-one locally free quotients of
\mathscr{E}
.

The derived projectivization theory extends its classical counterpart in two ways:

(a)

We allow
X
to be any derived scheme (or more generally, any prestack).

(b)

We allow
\mathscr{E}
to be any connective quasi-coherent sheaf, that is, it needs not be discrete.

We show that the derived projectivization
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})
enjoys the following features:

(0)

(
Underlying classical schemes
) The underlying classical scheme of
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})
is canonically equivalent to Grothendieck’s projectivization construction applied to its classical truncation, that is, there is a canonical equivalence
(\mathbb{P}_{X}(\mathscr{E}))_{\mathrm{cl}}\simeq\mathbb{P}_{X_{\mathrm{cl}},\mathrm{cl}}(\pi_{0}(\mathscr{E}))
(Proposition
4.21
).

For example, suppose
X
is a classical scheme and
\mathscr{E}
is represented by a complex
\mathscr{E}_{*}
of discrete quasi-coherent sheaves on
X
. In this case, the derived projectivization
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})
has the same underlying classical scheme as Grothendieck’s construction
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{cl}}(\mathscr{H}_{0}(\mathscr{E}_{*}))
, but usually with a non-trivial derived structure induced from the “hidden higher information” of
\mathscr{E}
.

Warning 1.2
.

Let
X
be classical and let
\mathscr{E}
be represented by a complex
\mathscr{E}_{*}
. The “hidden higher information” of
\mathscr{E}
which contributes to the derived structure of
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})
includes not only all the higher homologies
\{\mathscr{H}_{i}(\mathscr{E}_{*})\}_{i>0}
of
\mathscr{E}_{*}
but also the higher homologies
\{\mathscr{H}_{i}(\operatorname{Sym}^{d}(\mathscr{E}_{*}))\}_{i>0,d>0}
of the
derived symmetric powers
of
\mathscr{E}_{*}
. For example, even in the simple case where
\mathscr{E}=\mathscr{O}_{p}^{\oplus 2}
on
\mathbb{A}^{1}
, where
p\in\mathbb{A}^{1}
is the origin, the derived projectivization
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{O}_{p}^{\oplus 2})
is not isomorphic to Grothendieck’s projectivization
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{cl}}(\mathscr{O}_{p}^{\oplus 2})\simeq\mathbb{P}^{1}
but is equipped with a non-trivial derived structure (Example
7.19
). If
X
is an integral Cohen–Macaulay scheme and
\mathscr{E}
has homological dimension one, we will provide criterion for
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})
to be classical; see Lemma
7.16
.

(1)

(
Representability
) The functor
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})
is representable by a relative derived scheme over
X
(Proposition
4.18
).

(2)

(
Functoriality
) The formation of derived projectivizations commutes with arbitrary base change and tensoring with line bundles (Proposition
4.25
).

(3)

(
Finiteness properties
) If
\mathscr{E}
is perfect to order
n
(resp. almost perfect, perfect), then the projection
\mathrm{pr}\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})\to X
is locally of finite presentation to order
n
(resp. locally almost of finite presentation, locally of ﬁnite presentation); see Proposition
4.26
.

(4)

(
Closed immersions
) For any map
\varphi\colon\mathscr{F}\to\mathscr{E}
of quasi-coherent sheaves on
X
which is surjective on
\pi_{0}
, there is a canonical closed immersion
\iota\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
which is compatible with the formation of universal line bundles and tautological quotients. Moreover, we have an explicit description of the closed immersion
\iota
and its relative cotangent complex in terms of the morphism
\varphi
(Corollary
4.32
and Proposition
4.33
).

(5)

(
Euler fiber sequences
) The projection
\mathrm{pr}\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})\to X
admits a connective relative cotangent complex
L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})/X}
which fits into a canonical exact triangle

L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})/X}\otimes\mathscr{O}(1)\to\mathrm{pr}^{*}\mathscr{E}\xrightarrow{\rho}\mathscr{O}(1)

(here
\rho
denotes the tautological morphism from
\mathrm{pr}^{*}\mathscr{E}
to
\mathscr{O}(1)
); see Theorem
4.27
. We shall refer to the above sequence as the
Euler fiber sequence
.

Remark 1.3
.

The property
(5)
seems to provide a perfect example where an object in derived algebraic geometry behaves
manifestly better
than its counterpart in classical algebraic geometry. The above Euler sequence provides an explicit description of the relative cotangent complexes for
all
derived projectivizations
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})
. On the other hand, as far as the author is aware, there seems to be no general explicit description of the relative cotangent complexes for classical projectivizations (except in the special cases where
\mathscr{E}
is a vector bundle or a “generic” discrete sheaf of homological dimension one).

The construction of derived projectivization is closely related to
affine cones

\mathbb{V}(\mathscr{E})
, which we will define and study in §
4.1
. Our proof of the properties of derived projectivizations and affine cones depends heavily on the properties of the
derived symmetric algebras
, which we will explore in detail in §
2
(the local situation) and §
3
(the global situation).

1.2.
Derived projectivizations of sheaves of perfect-amplitude in
[0,1]

The derived projectivization
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})
enjoys further pleasant properties if
\mathscr{E}
has perfect-amplitude contained in
[0,1]
. For example, in this case, (as an immediate consequence of the above properties
(3)
and
(5)
) the projection
\mathrm{pr}\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})\to X
is a quasi-smooth proper morphism.

Our main results regarding derived projectivizations of quasi-coherent sheaves of perfect-amplitude in
[0,1]
are as follows:

(1)

(
Generalized Serre’s theorem
) Let
X
be a derived scheme (or more generally, a prestack), and let
\mathscr{E}\in\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
be of perfect-amplitude contained in
[0,1]
and rank
\delta
. If
\mathscr{E}
has rank
\delta\geq 1
, then for all integers
d
, there are canonical equivalences

\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\mathscr{O}(d))\simeq\begin{cases}\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{d}\mathscr{E}&\qquad\text{if~}d\geq 0;\\
0&\qquad\text{if ~}-\delta+1\leq d\leq-1;\\
(\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{-d-\delta}\mathscr{E}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{X}}\det\mathscr{E})^{\vee}[1-\delta]&\qquad\text{if~}d\leq-\delta,\end{cases}

where
\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{d}\mathscr{E}
denotes the derived symmetric powers of
\mathscr{E}
over
X
(see §
2.2
, §
3.4
). If
\mathscr{E}
has rank
\delta\leq 0
, then there is a canonical exact triangle

\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{d}(\mathscr{E})\to\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\mathscr{O}(d))\to(\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{-d-\delta}\mathscr{E}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{X}}\det\mathscr{E})^{\vee}[1-\delta]

which generalizes the classical Eagon–Northcott complex; see Theorem
5.6
.

(2)

(
Beilinson’s relations
) Let
X
be a quasi-compact, quasi-separated derived scheme (or more generally, a perfect stack (Definition
6.3
)), and let
\mathscr{E}\in\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
be of perfect-amplitude in
[0,1]
and rank
\delta\geq 1
. Let
\mathscr{O}(1)
denote the universal line bundle on
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})
and let
\mathscr{O}(i)
denote
\mathscr{O}(1)^{\otimes i}
, where
i\geq 0
is an integer. Then the sequence of line bundles

\mathscr{O},\mathscr{O}(1),\cdots,\mathscr{O}(\delta-1)

forms a relative exceptional sequence (§
6.1
) of
\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}))
over
X
, whose left dual relative exceptional sequence is given by

\operatorname{Sym}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})}^{\delta-1}(L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})/X}(1)[1]),\cdots,L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})/X}(1)[1],\mathscr{O},

where
L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})/X}
is the relative cotangent complex for the projection
\mathrm{pr}\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})\to X
,
L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})/X}(1)
denotes
L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})/X}\otimes\mathscr{O}(1)
, and
[1]
is the degree shift; see Proposition
6.19
.
By virtue of Illusie’s result (Proposition
2.11
(
4
)), there are canonical equivalences

\operatorname{Sym}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})}^{i}\big(L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})/X}(1)[1]\big)\simeq\big(\bigwedge\nolimits_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})}^{i}L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})/X}\big)(i)[i],

where
\operatorname{Sym}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})}^{i}
and
\bigwedge_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})}^{i}
denote the
i
th derived symmetric powers and
i
th derived exterior powers over
{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})}
, respectively (see §
2.2
, §
3.4
). Therefore we could equally write the above left dual relative exceptional sequence as

\big(\bigwedge\nolimits_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})}^{\delta-1}L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})/X}\big)(\delta-1)[\delta-1],\cdots,L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})/X}(1)[1],\mathscr{O}.

Furthermore, for all integers
i,j\in[0,\delta-1]
, we have the following derived version of Beilinson’s relations (
[
Be
]
, where similar results for projective spaces are obtained):

\left\{\begin{split}&\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})/X}(\mathscr{O}(i),\mathscr{O}(j))\simeq\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{j-i}(\mathscr{E}),\\
&\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})/X}\Big(\mathscr{O}(i),\operatorname{Sym}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})}^{j}\big(L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})/X}(1)[1]\big)\Big)\simeq\delta_{i,j}\cdot\mathscr{O}_{X},\\
&\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})/X}\Big(\big(\bigwedge\nolimits_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})}^{i}L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})/X}\big)(i),\big(\bigwedge\nolimits_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})}^{j}L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})/X}\big)(j)\Big)\simeq(\bigwedge\nolimits_{X}^{i-j}\mathscr{E})^{\vee},\end{split}\right.

where
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})/X}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})\in\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
denotes the mapping object of a pair of objects
\mathscr{F}\in\mathrm{Perf}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}))
and
\mathscr{G}\in\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}))
(Construction
6.6
), and
\mathscr{H}^{\vee}
denotes the dual in
\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
of an object
\mathscr{H}\in\mathrm{Perf}(X)
;
see Corollary
6.21
.

(3)

(
Semiorthogonal decompositions
) Let
X
be a derived scheme (or more generally, a prestack) and
\mathscr{E}
a quasi-coherent sheaf on
X
with perfect-amplitude in
[0,1]
and rank
\delta\geq 0
. Then the “shifted derived dual”
\mathscr{E}^{\vee}[1]
also has perfect-amplitude contained in
[0,1]
. We let
\widehat{Z}
be the
universal incidence locus
of
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})
and
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee}[1])
over
X
, which is defined as the derived closed subscheme of the (derived) fiber product
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})\times_{X}\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee}[1])
which classifies paths between the composite map

\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})}(-1)\boxtimes\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee}[1])}(-1)[1]\to\mathscr{E}^{\vee}\boxtimes\mathscr{E}\xrightarrow{\rm ev}\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})\times_{X}\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee}[1])}

and the zero map; see Notation
7.4
. Therefore, we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee}[1])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathrm{pr}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{-}}

We will show that the functor induced by the above correspondence
\widehat{Z}
,

\Phi=r_{+\,*}\circ r_{-}^{*}\colon\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee}[1]))\to\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})),

is fully faithful, and if
\delta\geq 1
, then for all integers
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, the functors

\mathrm{pr}_{+}^{*}(\underline{\hphantom{A}})\otimes\mathscr{O}(i)\colon\operatorname{QCoh}(X)\to\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}))

are fully faithful. Furthermore, there are induced semiorthogonal decomposition

\displaystyle\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}))

\displaystyle=\big\langle\Phi\big(\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee}[1]))\big),~\mathrm{pr}_{+}^{*}(\operatorname{QCoh}(X))\otimes\mathscr{O}(1),\ldots,\mathrm{pr}_{+}^{*}(\operatorname{QCoh}(X))\otimes\mathscr{O}(\delta)\big\rangle,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Perf}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}))

\displaystyle=\big\langle\Phi\big(\mathrm{Perf}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee}[1]))\big),~\mathrm{pr}_{+}^{*}(\mathrm{Perf}(X))\otimes\mathscr{O}(1),\ldots,\mathrm{pr}_{+}^{*}(\mathrm{Perf}(X))\otimes\mathscr{O}(\delta)\big\rangle;

see Theorem
7.5
for more details.

Remark 1.4
.

Combining these semiorthogonal decompositions with the above result
(2)
, we could obtain a whole series of semiorthogonal decompositions of
\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}))
(or
\mathrm{Perf}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}))
) by mutating the relative exceptional sequence
\mathscr{O}(1),\mathscr{O}(2),\ldots,\mathscr{O}(\delta)
.

Remark 1.5
.

In the context of classical algebraic geometry, the above semiorthogonal decompositions are referred to as the
projectivization formula
in
[
JL18
, Theorem 3.4]
; see also
[
Ku07
, Theorem 5.5]
,
[
Tod19
, Theorem 4.6.11]
, and
[
J21
, Theorem 6.16]
.

1.3.
Applications to classical situations

The results of the preceding sections have many interesting applications even in the classical situation.
For this purpose, we provide in §
7.3
criteria for the derived projectivizations
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})
and
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee}[1])
, the universal incidence loci
\widehat{Z}
, and derived symmetric products
\operatorname{Sym}^{d}(\mathscr{E})
and
\operatorname{Sym}^{d}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee}[1])
to be classical (Lemmata
7.16
and
7.18
). For example, assume that
X
is an integral Cohen–Macaulay scheme, and
\mathscr{E}
is cofiber of a generically injective map between vector bundles. If
\operatorname{rank}\mathscr{E}>0
, then under the above assumption, there is a hierarchy of conditions on the codimensions of degeneracy loci of
(X,\mathscr{E})
,

\eqref{eqn:condition:PF}\impliedby\eqref{eqn:condition:irr:PF}\impliedby\eqref{eqn:condition:PK}\impliedby\eqref{eqn:condition:irr:PK}\impliedby\eqref{eqn:condition:hatZ}\impliedby\eqref{eqn:condition:irr:hatZ},

which is in one-to-one correspondence to the following sequence of conditions:

\displaystyle\text{$\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})$ is classical}\impliedby\text{$\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})$ is irreducible}\impliedby\text{$\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee}[1])$ is classical}

\displaystyle\impliedby\text{$\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee}[1])$ is irreducible}\impliedby\text{$\widehat{Z}$ is classical}\impliedby\text{$\widehat{Z}$ is irreducible}.

For example,
(

a

)
,
(

a
′

)
, and
(

b

)
are the conditions
\operatorname{codim}_{X}(X_{i})\geq i
,
\operatorname{codim}_{X}(X_{i})\geq i+1
, and
\operatorname{codim}_{X}(X_{i})\geq\operatorname{rank}\mathscr{E}+i
, for all degeneracy loci
X_{i}\subseteq X
, respectively. There is also a similar description if
\operatorname{rank}\mathscr{E}=0
; see Lemma
7.16
for more details. In particular, there are numerous counter-examples for each of the inverse of the above implications “
\impliedby
”, and the results of the preceding subsection have interesting consequences in these situations.

We will only mention some of these applications in this introduction, and refer the readers to §
7.3
in the main text for more examples.

1.3.1.
Derived categories of reducible schemes; see Examples
7.21
and
7.23

Let
X
be any scheme, let
D\subseteq X
be any effective Cartier divisor. Let
\mathscr{E}=\mathscr{O}_{X}\oplus\mathscr{O}_{D}
. Then (locally) condition (

a

) is satisfied but (

a
′

) is not. More concretely,
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})=X\coprod\nolimits_{D}\mathbb{P}_{D}^{1}
is a classical reducible scheme, which is the union of
X
and
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{D}=D\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
along
D
; and
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee}[1])=\mathbb{V}_{D}(\mathscr{O}_{D}(-D)[1])
is the total space
{\rm Tot}(\mathcal{N}_{D/X}[-1])
of “
(-1)
-shifted normal bundle of
D
in
X
” in the sense
[
ACH
,
PTVV
]
, which has the same underlying classical scheme as
D
but equipped with non-trivial derived structure everywhere. Then Theorem
7.5
implies that there are semiorthogonal decompositions

\displaystyle\operatorname{QCoh}\big(X\coprod\nolimits_{D}\mathbb{P}_{D}^{1}\big)

\displaystyle=\big\langle\operatorname{QCoh}(X),~\operatorname{QCoh}({\rm Tot}(\mathcal{N}_{D/X}[-1]))\big\rangle,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Perf}\big(X\coprod\nolimits_{D}\mathbb{P}_{D}^{1}\big)

\displaystyle=\big\langle\mathrm{Perf}(X),~\mathrm{Perf}({\rm Tot}(\mathcal{N}_{D/X}[-1]))\big\rangle.

See Examples
7.21
for more details.

On the other hand, if we let
\mathscr{E}^{\prime}=\mathscr{O}_{X}\oplus\mathscr{O}_{D}(D)
, then
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}^{\prime})=X\coprod_{D}\mathbb{P}_{D}(\mathcal{N}\oplus\mathscr{O}_{D})
, where
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}_{D/X}
is the normal bundle, and
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}^{\prime\vee}[1])=D\times\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}[\varepsilon]
. Therefore we obtain

\displaystyle\operatorname{QCoh}\big(X\coprod\nolimits_{D}\mathbb{P}_{D}(\mathcal{N}\oplus\mathscr{O}_{D})\big)

\displaystyle=\big\langle\operatorname{QCoh}(X),~\operatorname{QCoh}(D)\otimes\operatorname{QCoh}(\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}[\varepsilon])\big\rangle,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Perf}\big(X\coprod\nolimits_{D}\mathbb{P}_{D}(\mathcal{N}\oplus\mathscr{O}_{D})\big)

\displaystyle=\big\langle\mathrm{Perf}(X),~\mathrm{Perf}(D)\otimes\mathrm{Perf}(\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}[\varepsilon])\big\rangle.

Here, the reducible scheme
X\coprod_{D}\mathbb{P}_{D}(\mathcal{N}\oplus\mathscr{O}_{D})
is precisely the special fiber appearing in the construction of
deformation to normal cones
of
[
Ful
, §5.1]
; see Variant
7.23
.

We could regard the semiorthogonal decompositions of the above two examples as
blowup formulae
, where
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})
and
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}^{\prime})
are the
derived blowups
in the sense of
[
KR18
,
He21
]
of
X
along the virtual codimension two closed derived subschemes
{\rm Tot}(\mathcal{N}_{D/X}[-1])\subseteq X
and
D\times\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}[\varepsilon]\subseteq X
, respectively; see Remark
7.24
.

1.3.2.
Contracting rational tails and bridges for curves; see Examples
7.22
and
7.25

In the preceding example, if we let
X=C
be a reduced curve over a field
\kappa
, let
p\in C(\kappa)
be a non-singular
\kappa
-point, and let
\mathscr{E}=\mathscr{O}_{C}\oplus\mathscr{O}_{p}
, then we obtain semiorthogonal decompositions

\displaystyle\operatorname{QCoh}\big(C\coprod\nolimits_{p}\mathbb{P}^{1}\big)

\displaystyle=\big\langle\operatorname{QCoh}(C),~\operatorname{QCoh}(\operatorname{Spec}\kappa[\varepsilon])\big\rangle,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Perf}\big(C\coprod\nolimits_{p}\mathbb{P}^{1}\big)

\displaystyle=\big\langle\mathrm{Perf}(C),~\mathrm{Perf}(\operatorname{Spec}\kappa[\varepsilon])\big\rangle,

where
\kappa[\varepsilon]=\operatorname{Sym}^{*}_{\kappa}(\kappa[1])
is the ring of
derived
dual numbers over
\kappa
(where we might informally think of
\varepsilon
as a generator in homological degree
1
). See
7.22
for more details. This result agrees with Kuznetsov and Shinder’s in
[
Ku21
, Example 5.2, Theorem 5.12]
.

We might also repeat the above process by setting
C_{0}=C
,
p_{0}=p
, and letting
C_{i}=C_{i-1}\coprod_{p_{i-1}}\mathbb{P}^{1}
, where
p_{i-1}\in C_{i-1}(\kappa)
is any nonsingular
\kappa
-point.
Then for any
n\geq 1
, we obtain semiorthogonal decompositions:

\displaystyle\operatorname{QCoh}(C_{n})

\displaystyle=\big\langle\operatorname{QCoh}(C),~\text{$n$-copies of $\operatorname{QCoh}(\operatorname{Spec}\kappa[\varepsilon])$}\big\rangle,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Perf}(C_{n})

\displaystyle=\big\langle\mathrm{Perf}(C),~\text{$n$-copies of $\mathrm{Perf}(\operatorname{Spec}\kappa[\varepsilon])$}\big\rangle.

The morphism spaces of the latter components depend (only) on the configurations of the trees of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
’s on
C_{n}
. For example,
in the case where
C_{n}
is the union of
C
and a chain
\Gamma_{n}
of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
’s (and
\kappa
has characteristic zero), the endomorphism algebra (of a choice of tilting bundle on
\Gamma_{n}
) of the latter component is described explicitly in
[
Bur
, Theorem 2.1]
. The cases of where
C_{n}
is union of
C
and trees of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
’s can be described similarly, in view of
[
KPS21
, Theorem 4.9]
.

On the other hand, let
C=\{xy=0\}\subset\mathbb{A}_{\kappa}^{2}
, where
\kappa
is a field, and let
o=(0,0)
denote the node of
C
.
Then
\widetilde{C}:=\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{C/\kappa}^{1})
is the total transform of
C
along the usual blowup
\operatorname{Bl}_{o}(\mathbb{A}^{2})\to\mathbb{A}^{2}
, and the map
\pi\colon\widetilde{C}\to C
is the contraction of the (non-reduced) rational bridge
E=2\mathbb{P}_{\kappa}^{1}
.
We obtain from Theorem
7.5
semiorthogonal decompositions

\displaystyle\operatorname{QCoh}(\widetilde{C})

\displaystyle=\big\langle\pi^{*}\operatorname{QCoh}(C),~\operatorname{QCoh}(\operatorname{Spec}(\kappa[\varepsilon]\otimes\kappa[\delta]))\big\rangle,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Perf}(\widetilde{C})

\displaystyle=\big\langle\pi^{*}\mathrm{Perf}(C),~\mathrm{Perf}(\operatorname{Spec}(\kappa[\varepsilon]\otimes\kappa[\delta]))\big\rangle;

where
\kappa[\delta]=\kappa[\delta]/(\delta^{2})
denote the ring of usual dual numbers;
see Example
7.25
for more details.
In this example, one could replace
C
by any nodal curve
C
. Notice that a recent result
[
KS22b
, Corollary 6.8]
implies that there are no semiorthogonal decompositions for the
reduced
scheme
\widetilde{C}^{\rm red}
when
C
is projective.

These results altogether provide a comprehensive picture of the behavior of derived categories of nodal curves under stabilization maps, since the stabilization maps of nodal curves are compositions of contracting rational tails and rational bridges (
[
Sta
,
Tag 0E7Q
]
).

1.3.3.
Threefolds with rational double points; see Example
7.28

Let
X=\mathbb{A}^{2}=\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}[x,y,z]
, and let
\mathscr{F}
denote the cokernel of the map
\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{2}}\xrightarrow{(x,y,0)^{T}}\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{2}}^{\oplus 3}.
Then (

a
′

) is satisfied but (

b

) is not. In this case,
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})=Q\subseteq\mathbb{A}^{2}\times\mathbb{P}^{2}=\mathbb{A}^{2}\times\operatorname{Proj}\mathbb{Z}[X,Y,Z]
is the quadric cone defined by the equation
xX+yY=0
, which is an irreducible threefold with a unique
A_{1}
-singularity at the cone point
o=((0,0),[0,0,1])\in Q
. Theorem
7.5
implies that there are semiorthogonal decompositions:

\displaystyle\operatorname{QCoh}(Q)

\displaystyle=\big\langle\operatorname{QCoh}(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[\varepsilon])),~~\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{A}^{2})\otimes\mathscr{O}_{Q}(1),\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{A}^{2})\otimes\mathscr{O}_{Q}(2)\big\rangle,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Perf}(Q)

\displaystyle=\big\langle\mathrm{Perf}(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[\varepsilon])),~~\mathrm{Perf}(\mathbb{A}^{2})\otimes\mathscr{O}_{Q}(1),\mathrm{Perf}(\mathbb{A}^{2})\otimes\mathscr{O}_{Q}(2)\big\rangle,

where
\mathbb{Z}[\varepsilon]=\operatorname{Sym}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{*}(\mathbb{Z}[1])
is the ring of
derived
dual numbers. We may replace
X=\mathbb{A}^{2}
by any reduced surface
S
over a field
\kappa
, and
\mathscr{F}
by
\mathscr{O}_{S}\oplus\mathscr{I}_{p}
, where
p\in S(\kappa)
is a non-singular rational point. The above semiorthogonal decompositions are locally compatible with Kawamata’s results
[
Ka19
, §7.1]
on quadric cones in
\mathbb{P}^{4}
, the results of Xie
[
X21
, Theorem 4.6]
and Pavic and Shinder
[
PS21
, Theorem 3.6]
on nodal del Pezzo threefolds. We may also consider the global version of this example by letting
X
be any scheme, and
\mathscr{F}
by
\mathscr{O}_{X}\oplus\mathscr{I}_{Y}
, where
Y\subseteq X
is a regularly immersed closed subscheme of codimension two; see Example
7.29
.

1.3.4.
Derived equivalences of classical schemes induced by non-trivially derived schemes

If the condition (

b
′

) is satisfied but (

c

) is not, then the schemes in the semiorthogonal decompositions are classical, but the fully faithful embedding is induced by a derived scheme. In the case where
\mathscr{E}
has rank zero, we could obtain various examples where
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})
and
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee}[1])
are birationally equivalent classical, irreducible threefolds (or fivefolds, or even higher dimensional varieties), but the universal incidence locus
\widehat{Z}
which induces the (non-trivial) equivalences of
\infty
-categories

r_{+,*}\,r_{-}^{*}\colon\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee}[1]))\simeq\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}))\qquad r_{+,*}\,r_{-}^{*}\colon\mathrm{Perf}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee}[1]))\simeq\mathrm{Perf}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}))

is
not
classical (where
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})\xleftarrow{r_{+}}\widehat{Z}\xrightarrow{r_{-}}\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}^{\vee}[1])
is the incidence diagram). See Example
7.31
.

We refer the readers to §
7.3
for more examples, where we only list lower dimensional cases in each situation, but all these results have direct analogues in higher dimensions.

1.4.
Applications to Hecke correspondences moduli

Many moduli problems which have the nature of parametrizing extensions naturally fit into the framework of derived projectivizations. In §
8
, we will consider one such example, the Hecke correspondence moduli which parametrizes “one-point modifications of complexes”. The geometry of these moduli spaces are extensively studied in Neguţ’s works
[
Ne17
,
Ne18
,
Ne19
]
in the classical context.

Let
X\to S
be a family of smooth, separated surfaces, and let
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{perf}}_{[0,1]}
denote the prestack of perfect complexes on
X
relative to
S
of Tor-amplitude contained in
[0,1]
. Let
\mathfrak{Z}^{\mathrm{perf}}_{[0,1]}
denote the moduli of “one-point modifications of objects of
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{perf}}_{[0,1]}
”, that is,
\mathfrak{Z}^{\mathrm{perf}}_{[0,1]}
is the prestack which parametrizes exact triangles of the form

\zeta=(\mathscr{F}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{F}\to x_{*}\mathscr{L}),

where
\mathscr{F}^{\prime},\mathscr{F}\in\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{perf}}_{[0,1]}
,
\mathscr{L}
is a line bundle on
S
, and
x\colon S\to X
is a section of the projection
X\to S
. Then there are natural forgetful maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Z}^{\mathrm{perf}}_{[0,1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{perf}}_{[0,1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{perf}}_{[0,1]},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{-}}

where
p_{+}
,
p_{-}
, and
p_{X}
are the maps which carry the triangle
\zeta
to
\mathscr{F}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{perf}}_{[0,1]}
,
\mathscr{F}\in\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{perf}}_{[0,1]}
, and
x\in X
, respectively. We show that the natural projections

\pi_{\pm}=p_{\pm}\times_{S}p_{X}\colon\mathfrak{Z}^{\mathrm{perf}}_{[0,1]}\to\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{perf}}_{[0,1]}\times_{S}X

are
derived projectivizations
of perfect-complexes of Tor-amplitude in
[0,1]
(Proposition
8.7
).

Remark 1.6
.

In fact, we will show that, for any family
X\to S
of (smooth separated) derived schemes of any given dimension
d
, the Hecke correspondence moduli
\mathfrak{Z}
fits into the framework of derived projectivizations (Propositions
8.5
and
8.7
); and the results are even true for any type of flag of “one-point modifications”
\mathfrak{Z}_{\lambda}
(Propositions
8.11
and
8.13
).

In particular, we could apply all of the above-mentioned results (the generalized Serre’s Theorem
5.6
, Beilinson’s relations Proposition
6.19
, Corollary
6.21
, and Semiorthogonal Decompositions Theorem
7.5
) to this situation. For example, if we let
(\mathfrak{Z}_{2}^{\bullet})^{\mathrm{perf}}_{[0,1]}
denote the moduli of “two-step flags of one-point modifications with support on one point”, that is, the prestack which parametrizes the pair of exact triangles

\mathscr{F}_{0}\xrightarrow{\phi_{1}}\mathscr{F}_{1}\to x_{*}(\mathscr{L}_{1})\qquad\mathscr{F}_{1}\xrightarrow{\phi_{2}}\mathscr{F}_{2}\to x_{*}(\mathscr{L}_{2}),

where
\mathscr{L}_{1},\mathscr{L}_{2}
are line bundles on
S
and
x\colon S\to X
is a section of
X\to S
. We let
(\widehat{\mathfrak{Z}}_{2}^{\bullet})^{\mathrm{perf}}_{[0,1]}
denote the universal incidence locus for
\pi_{\pm}
, that is, the locus on
(\mathfrak{Z}_{2}^{\bullet})^{\mathrm{perf}}_{[0,1]}
where the composition

\mathscr{L}_{1}^{\vee}\boxtimes(\mathscr{L}_{2}\otimes\mathscr{K}_{X/S})[1]\to x^{*}\mathscr{F}_{1}\otimes x^{*}(\mathscr{F}_{1}^{\vee})\xrightarrow{\rm ev}\mathscr{O}

is nullhomotopic. Therefore, we have a correspondence diagram

\mathfrak{Z}^{\mathrm{perf}}_{[0,1]}\xleftarrow{r_{+}}(\widehat{\mathfrak{Z}}_{2}^{\bullet})^{\mathrm{perf}}_{[0,1]}\xrightarrow{r_{-}}\mathfrak{Z}^{\mathrm{perf}}_{[0,1]}.

We let
\mathfrak{Z}^{\mathrm{perf}}_{[0,1],\operatorname{rk}=\delta}
denote the subfunctor of
\mathfrak{Z}^{\mathrm{perf}}_{[0,1]}
for which
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{F}^{\prime}
have rank
\delta
, and let
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{perf}}_{[0,1],\operatorname{rk}=\delta}
be defined similarly. Theorem
7.5
implies that, if
\delta>0
, the functor

\Phi=r_{+\,*}\,r_{-}^{*}\colon\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathfrak{Z}^{\mathrm{perf}}_{[0,1],\operatorname{rk}=\delta})\to\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathfrak{Z}^{\mathrm{perf}}_{[0,1],\operatorname{rk}=\delta}),

and the functors, for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\Psi_{i}=\mathrm{pr}_{+}^{*}(\underline{\hphantom{A}})\otimes\mathscr{L}_{\rm univ}^{\otimes i}\colon\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathfrak{X})\to\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathfrak{Z}^{\mathrm{perf}}_{[0,1],\operatorname{rk}=\delta})\quad\text{where}\quad\mathfrak{X}=\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{perf}}_{[0,1],\operatorname{rk}=\delta}\times_{S}X

are fully faithful, and there are induced semiorthogonal decompositions

\displaystyle\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathfrak{Z}^{\mathrm{perf}}_{[0,1],\operatorname{rk}=\delta})

\displaystyle=\big\langle\Phi(\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathfrak{Z}^{\mathrm{perf}}_{[0,1],\operatorname{rk}=\delta})),~\Psi_{1}(\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathfrak{X})),\cdots,\Psi_{\delta}(\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathfrak{X}))\big\rangle,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Perf}(\mathfrak{Z}^{\mathrm{perf}}_{[0,1],\operatorname{rk}=\delta})

\displaystyle=\big\langle\Phi(\mathrm{Perf}(\mathfrak{Z}^{\mathrm{perf}}_{[0,1],\operatorname{rk}=\delta})),~\Psi_{1}(\mathrm{Perf}(\mathfrak{X})),\cdots,\Psi_{\delta}(\mathrm{Perf}(\mathfrak{X}))\big\rangle.

Similarly for other cases; see Corollary
8.15
for more details.

Remark 1.7
.

The general framework of §
8
not only unifies various situations such as the Hilbert schemes of points (Example
8.1
) and moduli of discrete Gieseker stable sheaves (Example
8.2
), but also allows us to consider moduli spaces under various types of stability conditions. For example, we expect the results of §
8
to be helpful for the study of the similar algebraic structures for the moduli spaces
\mathfrak{M}_{\sigma}(X)
of Bridgeland (semi-)stable objects on a surface
X
(or a family
X\to S
of surfaces), where the surface
X
(or the family
X\to S
of surfaces) is equipped with (a family of) Bridgeland stability conditions
\sigma
in the sense of

[
Bri07
,
Bri08
,
AB12
,
BM11
,
BM14
]
(the absolute cases) and
[
BLM+
]
(the family case).

1.5.
The framework of derived algebraic geometry

We work in the context of derived algebraic geometry, as developed by Lurie
[
DAG
,
HTT
,
DAG-V
,
HA
,
SAG
,
Lu-Ke
]
, Toën and Vezzosi
[
TVe05
,
TVe08
,
Toë14
]
and many others
[
BFN
,
GR
,
TVa07
,
PTVV
]
.

There are various models for the framework of derived algebraic geometry. For definiteness and completeness, we choose to work within Lurie’s framework, and we will use Lurie’s works as the only references for all results of derived algebraic geometry in the proofs of this paper. However, we expect our arguments to work in the other models of derived algebraic geometry.

From a
technical
perspective, the framework of derived algebraic geometry plays an essential role in this paper from at least the following aspects:

•

Non-abelian derived functors.
Historically, symmetric algebras of complexes are studied either using concrete Koszul models
[
Ei
,
ABW
]
or simplicial resolutions
[
DP
,
Qu
]
. However, for this paper’s goal, either approach has its advantages and certain drawbacks:

–

The definition using Koszul models is practical for computations but is a priori only defined for complexes of vector bundles (and might not be our desired definition if the characteristic is not zero). For example, even the fact that the symmetric algebras depend only on the quasi-isomorphism classes of complexes is a theorem rather than an immediate consequence of this definition.

–

On the other hand, the approach using simplicial resolutions is general and functorial, but might not be effective for concrete computations.

–

Illusie’s results in
[
Ill
]
bridge the above two approaches, but the “bridging process” is slightly complicated and lacks sufficient functoriality for this paper’s goal.

Lurie’s theory of non-abelian derived functors
[
HTT
, §5.5.8]
using the framework of
\infty
-categories in a way unifies these two perspectives, and enjoys the
best features
of both approaches. See §
2.4
, §
2.5
.

•

Functorial exact triangles (fiber sequences).
In the classical context of triangulated categories, the construction of cones is known to be non-functorial. Therefore, it is hard to show that two non-trivial exact triangles obtained from different ways are equivalent. The framework of derived algebraic geometry (see, for example,
[
HTT
,
HA
,
SAG
]
) not only solves the problem of non-functorial triangles but also explains why certain triangles should be equivalent, by “remembering their origins”. This perspective plays a vital role in many proofs of this paper; see, for example, the proofs of generalized Serre’s theorem (Theorem
5.6
) and Beilinson’s relations (Proposition
6.19
and Corollary
6.21
).

•

Translating problems of algebraic geometry to problems of algebraic topology
. The framework of derived algebraic geometry (for example, the theory of general derived higher moduli (pre)stacks, as developed by Lurie in
[
DAG
,
SAG
]
, and Toën and Vezzosi in
[
TVe05
,
TVe08
]
) allows us to reduce certain problems of algebraic geometry to that of algebraic topology, where the
\infty
-amount of homological information that is hard to manipulate in algebraic geometry is effectively encoded by topological spaces that are easier to handle in algebraic topology. This perspective plays a vital role in proving the Euler fiber sequence (Theorem
4.27
).

•

Phenomena only visible in the derived context.
Let
V
be a finite-dimensional vector space (or a vector bundle over a base scheme). The classical
universal incidence quadric

\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathbb{P}(V)\times\mathbb{P}(V^{\vee})
, defined as the zero locus of the composite map

\mathscr{O}(-1,-1)\to V^{\vee}\otimes V\xrightarrow{\rm ev}\mathscr{O},

plays a crucial role in classical and homological projective duality
[
Ku07
]
. Suppose that
V=[V_{1}\to V_{0}]
is a complex of vector spaces (or vector bundles), and
V^{\vee}
is the dual of
V
. Then the role of
\mathcal{H}
is now played by the
universal incidence locus

\widehat{Z}\subseteq\mathbb{P}(V)\times\mathbb{P}(V^{\vee}[1])
(Notation
7.4
), defined as the zero locus of the composite map

\mathscr{O}(-1,-1)[1]\to V^{\vee}\otimes V\xrightarrow{\rm ev}\mathscr{O}.

However, since the zero loci of non-zero degree shifts of line bundles only affect the derived structure and have no effects on the underlying classical schemes, the semiorthogonal decompositions of Theorem
7.5
induced by
\widehat{Z}
are best understood in the derived context, even in the cases where
\mathbb{P}(V)
and
\mathbb{P}(V^{\vee}[1])
are classical.

1.6.
Related works

The classical projectivization formula is studied in
[
Ku07
, Theorem 5.5]
,
[
Tod21
,
Tod19
]
,
[
JL18
, Theorem 3.4]
,
[
J21
, Theorem 6.16]
, and the Chow-theoretical counterpart is studied in
[
J19
]
. We expect the current work to be helpful to extend the results of
[
J19
]
to the context of bivariant and motivic theories for derived schemes.

The projectivization formula is also related to the theory of homological projective duality developed by Kuznetsov
[
Ku07
]
; see also
[
JLX17
,
P19
]
.

Our construction of affine cones in §
4.1
is closely related to the construction of Jiang and Thomas in
[
JT17
]
in the context of perfect obstruction theory
[
BF
,
LT
]
and
(-1)
-shifted symplectic structure
[
PTVV
]
. It would be interesting to explore the further connections between the results of this paper and the framework of perfect obstruction theory.

In
[
Kh21
]
, Khan defined and studied the derived symmetric algebras (§
3.5
) on derived Artin stacks and applied them to obtain an excess intersection formula for the algebraic
K
-theory.

In
[
He21
]
, Hekking constructs projective spectra for all graded simplicial commutative algebras over a derived scheme. In the case where the graded algebra
\mathcal{B}_{*}
is given by a derived symmetric algebra
\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{*}(\mathscr{E})
, our construction of
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})
in §
4.2
is equivalent to his
\operatorname{Proj}(\mathcal{B}_{*})
.

In
[
Z20
]
, Zhao also established a version of the generalized Serre’s theorem
5.6
.

The derived projectivization is closely related to the theory of categorification of wall-crossing of Donalson–Thomas moduli spaces and the d-critical birational geometry, developed by Toda in
[
Tod18
,
Tod21
,
Tod19
,
Tod21a
,
Tod21b
,
Tod21c
]
.

The applications in §
7.3
are related to the study of derived categories of nodal curves in
[
Bur
,
KPS21
]
and nodal threefolds in
[
Ku21
,
Ka19
,
X21
,
PS21
]
.

The study of Hecke correspondence in §
8
is closely related to Neguţ’s works
[
Ne17
,
Ne18
,
Ne19
]
; see also
[
KV19
,
PS19
,
PZ20
,
Z20
,
Z21
]
.

In
[
J22b
,
J23a
]
, we extend the constructions and results presented in this paper to the context of Grassmannians in characteristic zero. In this generalization, the derived symmetric and exterior products are replaced by more general derived Schur functors.

1.7.
Notations and conventions

We made an effort to keep our notations and terminologies as close to Lurie’s in
[
DAG
,
HTT
,
HA
,
SAG
,
Lu-Ke
]
as possible, so that readers of Lurie’s works will not need to make a significant extra effort to adjust to those of this paper.

For the readers’ convenience, here is a list of notations and terminologies that we will frequently use in this paper:

•

We let
\operatorname{{\bf\Delta}}
denote the
simplex category
(that is, the category of nonempty finite linearly ordered sets, with morphisms given by nondecreasing maps). We let
\operatorname{Set}_{\Delta}=\mathrm{Fun}(\operatorname{{\bf\Delta}}^{\mathrm{op}},\operatorname{Set})
denote the simplicial category of simplicial sets. For each
n\geq 0
, we let
\Delta^{n}\in\operatorname{Set}_{\Delta}
denote the simplicial set that represents the functor
\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{{\bf\Delta}}}(\underline{\hphantom{A}},[n])
.

•

We let
\mathcal{S}
denote the
\infty
-category of spaces
, that is, the homotopy-coherent nerve of simplicial category of Kan complexes.

•

We let
\mathrm{Sp}
denote the
\infty
-category of spectra
(
[
HA
, Definition 1.4.3.1]
).

•

We let
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
denote the
\infty
-category of small
\infty
-categories, and
\widehat{\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}}_{\infty}
the
\infty
-category of not necessarily small
\infty
-categories (
[
HTT
, Chapter 3]
). For an
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
and objects
C,D\in\mathcal{C}
,
\mathrm{Map}_{\mathcal{C}}(C,D)\in\mathcal{S}
denotes the
mapping space
. For a pair of
\infty
-categories
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
, we let
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
denote the
\infty
-category of functors from
\mathcal{C}
to
\mathcal{D}
.

•

For an
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
, we let
\mathcal{C}^{\simeq}
denote the largest Kan complex contained in
\mathcal{C}
, that is, the
\infty
-category obtained from
\mathcal{C}
by discarding all non-invertible morphisms.

•

If
\mathcal{C}
is a pointed
\infty
-category which admits cofibers (resp. fibers), we let
\Sigma=\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}
(resp.
\Omega=\Omega_{\mathcal{C}}
) denote the
suspension
(resp.
loop
) functor. If a pointed
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
admits both cofibers and fibers, we will define a translation functor
[i]
for each
i\in\mathbb{Z}
by the following rule: let
[i]=\Sigma^{\circ i}
if
i>0
,
[i]=\Omega^{\circ-i}
if
i<0
, and
[i]=\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{C}}
if
i=0
.

•

We let
\operatorname{CAlg}^{\Delta}
denote the
\infty
-category of simplicial commutative rings (
[
SAG
, Definition 25.1.1.1]
), and we will refer to the elements of
\operatorname{CAlg}^{\Delta}
as
simplicial commutative rings

1
1

1

These are also referred to as
animated commutative rings
; see
[
CS19
, §5.1]
,
[
BL22
, Appendix A]
.
.
The
\infty
-category
\operatorname{CAlg}^{\Delta}
can be obtained from ordinary category
{\rm Poly}
of finite type polynomial rings by freely adjoining sifted colimits (that is,
\operatorname{CAlg}^{\Delta}=\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}({\rm Poly})
), and we will review this construction in §
2.2
. For a simplicial commutative ring
R\in\operatorname{CAlg}^{\Delta}
, we let
\operatorname{CAlg}^{\Delta}_{R}
denote the coslice category
(\operatorname{CAlg}^{\Delta})_{/R}
(
[
SAG
, Notation 25.1.4.4]
).

•

Let
\mathcal{C}^{\otimes}\to{\rm Comm}^{\otimes}
be a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category (
[
HA
, Definition 2.0.0.7]
) with underlying
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
. We let
\operatorname{CAlg}(\mathcal{C})=\mathrm{Alg}_{/\mathrm{Comm}}(\mathcal{C})
denote
\infty
-category of commutative algebra objects
of
\mathcal{C}
(
[
HA
, Definition 2.1.3.1]
). For
A\in\operatorname{CAlg}(\mathcal{C})
, we let
\operatorname{Mod}_{A}(\mathcal{C})
denote the
\infty
-category of
A
-module objects of
\mathcal{C}
(
[
HA
, Definition 3.3.3.8]
).

•

We let
\operatorname{CAlg}=\operatorname{CAlg}(\mathrm{Sp})
denote the
\infty
-category of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-rings (
[
HA
, Definition 7.1.0.1]
). There is a forgetful functor
\Theta\colon\operatorname{CAlg}^{\Delta}\to\operatorname{CAlg}
. For a simplicial commutative ring
A
, we will denote the image
\Theta(A)
as
A^{\circ}
and refer to it as the
underlying
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-ring
of
A
(
[
SAG
, Construction 25.1.2.1]
).

•

For any
A\in\operatorname{CAlg}^{\Delta}
, we will let
\operatorname{Mod}_{A}
denote the
\infty
-category
\operatorname{Mod}_{A^{\circ}}(\mathrm{Sp})
, and refer to the elements of
\operatorname{Mod}_{A}
as
A
-modules
. For a simplicial commutative ring
A
and an
A
-module
M
, we let
\pi_{i}(A)=\pi_{i}(A^{\circ})
(resp.
\pi_{i}(M)
) denotes the
i
th homotopy group of the underlying spectrum of
A
(resp.
M
). The action map
R\otimes M\to M
endows
\pi_{*}(M)=\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\pi_{m}(M)
with the structure of a graded
\pi_{*}(R)
-module. In particular,
\pi_{0}(M)
is a module over the ordinary commutative ring
\pi_{0}(R)
. We will say that
M
is
connective
if
\pi_{i}(M)=0
for all
i<0
. We let
\operatorname{Mod}_{A}^{\mathrm{cn}}
denote the full subcategory of
\operatorname{Mod}_{A}
spanned by connective
A
-modules. See
[
HA
, §7.1.1]
for more details. We say a simplicial commutative ring
A
(resp. an
A
-module
M
) is
discrete
if
\pi_{i}(A)=0
for
i\neq 0
(resp.
\pi_{i}(M)=0
for
i\neq 0
). We let
\operatorname{CAlg}^{\heartsuit}
and
\operatorname{Mod}_{A}^{\heartsuit}
denote the full subcategory of
\operatorname{CAlg}^{\Delta}
and
\operatorname{Mod}_{A}
spanned by discrete objects, respectively.

•

By a
derived scheme
we mean a pair
X=(|X|,\mathscr{O}_{X})
where
|X|
is a topological space, and
\mathscr{O}_{X}
is an
\operatorname{CAlg}^{\Delta}
-valued sheaf on
|X|
, such that the following conditions are satisfied: (i) The underlying ringed space
X_{\mathrm{cl}}=(|X|,\pi_{0}\mathscr{O}_{X})
is a (classical) scheme, which is called the
underlying classical scheme of
X
. (ii) Each of the sheaves
\pi_{n}(\mathscr{O}_{X})
is a quasi-coherent
\pi_{0}(\mathscr{O}_{X})
-module on
X_{\mathrm{cl}}
. (iii) The structure sheaf
\mathscr{O}_{X}
is hypercomplete when regarded as an object of the
\infty
-topos
\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)
in the sense of
[
HTT
, §6.5.2]
.

•

By a
prestack
we mean an element of the
\infty
-category
\mathrm{Fun}(\operatorname{CAlg}^{\Delta},\mathcal{S})
, that is, a functor
X\colon\operatorname{CAlg}^{\Delta}\to\mathcal{S}
. A map
f\colon X\to Y
between prestacks is a natural transformation of the functors
X,Y\colon\operatorname{CAlg}^{\Delta}\to\mathcal{S}
. For a prestack
X
, we let
\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
denote the
\infty
-category of quasi-coherent sheaves on
X
(which we will review in §
3.2
), and let
\mathrm{Perf}(X)
denote the full subcategory of
\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
spanned by perfect objects.

Here is a list of notations and terminologies that either we introduce in this paper or for which our usage is slightly different from (some of) Lurie’s works:

•

We use
\operatorname{Sym}_{A}^{n}(M)
to denote the
derived
n
th symmetric product
of a connective
A
-module
M
over
A
(§
2.3
), and use
\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{n}(\mathscr{M})
to denote the global version, that is, the
derived
n
th symmetric power
of a connective quasi-coherent sheaf
\mathscr{M}
over a prestack
X
(§
3.4
). This notation agrees with Lurie’s in
[
DAG
]
, but is
different
from his in
[
HA
,
SAG
]
.
2
2

2

In
[
HA
,
SAG
]
, Lurie uses
\operatorname{Sym}_{A}^{n}(M)
to denote the
A
-module obtained by taking homotopy coinvariants for the action of the symmetric group
\Sigma_{n}
on the
n
-fold relative tensor product
M\otimes_{A}\cdots\otimes_{A}M
, which is generally different from the derived
n
th symmetric power unless
A
has characteristic zero; We will not use this construction in this paper. Our notation
\operatorname{Sym}_{A}^{n}(M)
corresponds to Lurie’s notation
{\rm LSym}_{A}^{n}(M)
in
[
SAG
, §25.2]
.

•

For an ordinary commutative ring
R
and a discrete
R
-module
M
,
S_{R}^{d}(M)=\operatorname{Sym}_{\mathrm{cl},R}^{d}(M)
denote the
classical symmetric products
, reviewed in §
2.1
. Let
\rho\colon M\to M^{\prime}
be a map of discrete
R
-modules,
\mathbf{S}^{d}(R,\rho)
denotes the Koszul complex defined in Construction
2.19
.

•

\operatorname{QCAlg}^{\Delta}(X)
denotes the
\infty
-category of quasi-coherent algebras
on a prestack
X
; see §
3.3
. For a morphism of prestacks
f\colon X\to Y
, we let
f_{\rm alg}^{*}\colon\operatorname{QCAlg}^{\Delta}(Y)\to\operatorname{QCAlg}^{\Delta}(X)
denote the pullback functor, and
f_{*}^{\rm alg}
the right adjoint of
f_{\rm alg}^{*}
(if exists).

•

\operatorname{Spec}_{X}
denotes the
relative spectrum
functor over a prestack
X
; see Definition
3.13
.

•

\mathbb{V}(\mathscr{E})
or
\mathbb{V}_{X}(\mathscr{E})
denotes the
affine cone
of a connective quasi-coherent sheaf
\mathscr{E}
over a prestack
X
(§
4.1
);
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})
or
\mathbb{P}_{X}(\mathscr{E})
denotes the
derived projectivization
of a connective quasi-coherent sheaf
\mathscr{E}
over a prestack
X
(§
4.2
).

•

The Eagon–Northcott complexes
\mathbf{EN}_{d}(X,\sigma)
are defined in Remark
5.9
.

•

For an perfect stack
X
(Definition
6.3
), a
X
-linear stable
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
,
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(C,D)=\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathcal{C}/X}(C,D)\in\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
denotes the mapping object defined in Construction
6.6
. For a relative exceptional object
E\in\mathcal{C}
over
X
(Definition
6.8
),
\mathbb{L}_{E}
and
\mathbb{R}_{E}
denote the left and right mutation functors, respectively, defined in Definition
6.12
.

•

The universal incidence locus
\widehat{Z}
and the incidence diagram is defined in Notation

.

•

The notations
\mathfrak{M}
,
\mathfrak{Z}
,

, and their variants, denote the moduli functors that appear in Hecke correspondences, which we will define and only use in §

.

2.
Derived symmetric algebras

This section reviews Lurie’s construction of derived symmetric algebras and studies their basic properties. The constructions and results of this section play an essential role in our later study of affine cones and derived projectivizations in this paper.

In §
2.1
, we briefly review the classical constructions of the symmetric, exterior, and divided powers. §
2.2
reviews Lurie’s general theory of non-abelian derived functors, and §
2.3
reviews the constructions and properties of the
derived
symmetric, exterior, and divided powers. In §
2.4
, we define
derived
symmetric algebras and study their basic properties; see Propositions
2.16
and
2.17
. Finally, in §
2.5
, we apply Lurie’s machinery §
2.2
to construct two canonical series of functorial fiber sequences associated to symmetric powers.

Remark 2.1
.

The material of this section has a direct generalization to a theory of derived Schur functors, which we will explore in a forthcoming paper.

2.1.
Classical symmetric, exterior and divided power algebras

For the readers’ convenience, we will briefly review the classical definitions of symmetric, exterior and divided power algebras. In this subsection, we make the following assumption:

•

We let
R
be a(n ordinary) commutative ring, and
M
a discrete
R
-module.

Definition 2.2

(Classical tensor algebras; see
[
Bou
, Chapter III, §5]
)
.

For a pair of discrete
R
-modules
M
and
N
, we let
M\otimes_{R}^{\mathrm{cl}}N
denote the classical tensor product
\operatorname{Tor}_{0}^{R}(M,N)
over
R
. For an integer
n\geq 0
, we let
\operatorname{T}^{n}(M)
denote the
n
-fold classical tensor product
M\otimes_{R}^{\mathrm{cl}}M\otimes_{R}^{\mathrm{cl}}\cdots\otimes_{R}^{\mathrm{cl}}M
. By convention, we set
\operatorname{T}^{0}(M)=R
,
\operatorname{T}^{1}(M)=M
. Then the
classical tensor algebra of
M
over
R
, denoted by
\operatorname{T}^{*}(M)
, or
\operatorname{T}_{R}^{*}(M)
, is the (ordinary) graded associative
R
-algebra
\operatorname{T}^{*}(M)=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\operatorname{T}^{n}(M)
, with the multiplication map

m_{p,q}\colon\operatorname{T}^{p}(M)\times\operatorname{T}^{q}(M)\to\operatorname{T}^{p+q}(M),

where
p,q\geq 0
, given by the formula:

m_{p,q}(x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{p},x_{p+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{p+q})=x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{p}\otimes x_{p+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{p+q}.

Definition 2.3

(Classical symmetric algebras; see
[
Bou
, Chapter III, §6]
)
.

The
clasical symmetric algebra of
M
over
R
, denoted by
S^{*}(M)
,
S_{R}^{*}(M)
, or
\operatorname{Sym}_{\mathrm{cl}}^{*}(M)
, is the (ordinary) commutative
R
-algebra obtained as the quotient of the tensor algebra
\operatorname{T}_{R}(M)
by the two-sided ideal
\mathfrak{C}=\mathfrak{C}_{M}
, where the ideal
\mathfrak{C}
is generated by the elements of the form
x\otimes y-y\otimes x
for
x,y\in M
. Since
\mathfrak{C}
is a graded ideal generated by homogeneous elements of degree
2
, the algebra
S_{R}^{*}(M)
is graded and equipped with a direct sum decomposition
S_{R}^{*}(M)=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}S_{R}^{n}(M)
, where
S_{R}^{n}(M)=\operatorname{T}^{n}(M)/(\mathfrak{C}\cap\operatorname{T}^{n}(M))
. We will refer to the
R
-module
S_{R}^{n}(M)
as the
classical
n
th symmetric power of
M
over
R
. From
\mathfrak{C}^{0}=\mathfrak{C}^{1}=0
, we obtain canonical identifications
S_{R}^{0}(M)=R
and
S_{R}^{1}(M)=M
. The canonical map
M=S_{R}^{1}(M)\hookrightarrow S_{R}^{*}(M)
is referred to as the canonical injection.

Definition 2.4

(Classical exterior algebras, see
[
Bou
, Chapter III, §7]
)
.

The
exterior algebra of
M
over
R
, denoted by
\bigwedge_{\mathrm{cl}}^{*}(M)
, or
\bigwedge_{\mathrm{cl},R}^{*}(M)
, is the ordinary
R
-algebra defined as the quotient of the tensor algebra
\operatorname{T}_{R}(M)
by the two-sided ideal
\mathfrak{A}=\mathfrak{A}_{M}
, where
\mathfrak{A}
is generated by elements of the form
x\otimes x
for
x\in M
. Since
\mathfrak{A}
is a graded ideal generated by homogeneous elements of degree
2
, the algebra
\bigwedge_{\mathrm{cl}}^{*}(M)
is graded, and we have a direct sum decomposition
\bigwedge_{\mathrm{cl}}^{*}(M)=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\bigwedge_{\mathrm{cl}}^{n}(M)
, where
\bigwedge_{\mathrm{cl}}^{n}(M)=\operatorname{T}^{n}(M)/(\mathfrak{A}\cap\operatorname{T}^{n}(M))
. We will refer to
\bigwedge_{\mathrm{cl}}^{n}(M)
as the
classical
n
th exterior power of
M
over
R
. From
\mathfrak{A}^{0}=\mathfrak{A}^{1}=0
, we obtain canonical identifications
\bigwedge_{\mathrm{cl}}^{0}(M)=R
and
\bigwedge_{\mathrm{cl}}^{1}(M)=M
. We will refer to the canonical map
M=\bigwedge_{\mathrm{cl}}^{1}(M)\hookrightarrow\bigwedge_{\mathrm{cl}}^{*}(M)
as the canonical injection.

Definition 2.5

(Classical divided power algebras,
[
Ro
, Chapter III, §1, p. 248]
)
.

The
classical divided power (or P.D., puissances divisées) algebra of
M
over
R
, denoted by
\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}^{*}(M)
,
\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl},R}^{*}(M)
, or
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{cl}}^{*}(M)
, is the (ordinary) commutative
R
-algebra obtained as the quotient algebra of the free polynomial algebra
R[\{\gamma^{n}(x)\}_{n\geq 0,x\in M}]
on symbols
\{\gamma^{n}(x)\}_{n\geq 0,x\in M}
by the ideal generated by following relations:

\displaystyle\gamma^{0}(x)=1,

\displaystyle\forall x\in M;

\displaystyle\gamma^{n}(rx)=r^{n}\gamma^{n}(x)

\displaystyle\forall r\in R,x\in M^{\prime},n\geq 0;

\displaystyle\gamma^{m}(x)\cdot\gamma^{n}(x)=\binom{m+n}{m}\gamma^{m+n}(x)

\displaystyle\forall x\in M,n\geq 0;

\displaystyle\gamma^{n}(x+y)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}\gamma^{i}(x)\gamma^{n-i}(y),

\displaystyle\forall x,y\in M,n\geq 0.

Since the ideal generated by above relations are homogeneous, the algebra
\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}^{*}(M)
is graded, and we have a direct sum decomposition
\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}^{*}(M)=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}^{n}(M)
, where
\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}^{0}(M)=R
and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}^{1}(M)=M
. We will refer to the
R
-module
\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}^{n}(M)=\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl},R}^{n}(M)
as the
classical
n
th divided power of
M
over
R
. The assignment
x\mapsto\gamma^{n}(x)
defines a map of
R
-modules
\gamma^{n}\colon M\to\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}^{n}(M)
. Notice that
\gamma^{0}\equiv 1\colon M\to R
is the constant map, and
\gamma^{1}=\operatorname{id}\colon M\to M
is the identity map on
M
. For an element
x\in M
, we will also use the notation
x^{(n)}:=\gamma^{n}(x)\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}^{n}(M)
; we will refer to
x^{(n)}=\gamma^{n}(x)
as the
n
th divided power
of
x
.

We refer the readers to
[
Bou
, Chapter III, §6, §7]
and
[
Ro
]
for the basic properties of the above classical constructions. Here, we will list some of them so that the reader can easily compare them with the corresponding properties of their derived counterparts:

(1)

(Base change) Let
R\to R^{\prime}
be a homomorphism of (ordinary) commutative rings, and let
M
be a discrete
R
-module. Then there are canonical isomorphisms

\displaystyle R^{\prime}\otimes_{R}^{\mathrm{cl}}S_{R}^{*}(M)

\displaystyle\simeq S_{R^{\prime}}^{*}(R^{\prime}\otimes_{R}^{\mathrm{cl}}M),

\displaystyle R^{\prime}\otimes_{R}^{\mathrm{cl}}\bigwedge\nolimits_{\mathrm{cl},R}^{*}(M)

\displaystyle\simeq\bigwedge\nolimits_{\mathrm{cl},R^{\prime}}^{*}(R^{\prime}\otimes_{R}^{\mathrm{cl}}M),

\displaystyle R^{\prime}\otimes_{R}^{\mathrm{cl}}\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl},R}^{*}(M)

\displaystyle\simeq\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl},R^{\prime}}^{*}(R^{\prime}\otimes_{R}^{\mathrm{cl}}M)

of graded
R^{\prime}
-algebras; see
[
Bou
, Chapter III, §6.4, Proposition 7, §7.5, Proposition 8]
and
[
Ro
, Theorem III.3, p. 262]
.

(2)

(Freeness) If
M
is a (locally) free
R
-module of rank
m
, then for any integer
n\geq 0
,
S^{n}(M)
,
\bigwedge_{\mathrm{cl}}^{n}M
, and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}^{n}(M)
are (locally) free
R
-modules of ranks
\binom{n+m-1}{n}
,
{m\choose n}
, and
\binom{n+m-1}{n}
, respectively. In this case, we will drop the subscript
\mathrm{cl}
and denote them by
S^{n}(M)
,
\bigwedge^{n}M
, and
\Gamma^{n}(M)
; these notations will be justified in the next section.

(3)

(Duality) If
M
is a locally free
R
-module, then for any integer
n\geq 0
, there are canonical isomorphisms of
R
-modules
(\bigwedge^{n}M)^{\vee}\simeq\bigwedge^{n}(M^{\vee})
,
(S^{n}(M))^{\vee}\simeq\Gamma(M^{\vee})
. (Here,
N^{\vee}
denote the
R
-linear dual of a locally free
R
-module
N
.)

(4)

(Right exactness) The formations of the graded algebras
S^{*}(M)
,
\bigwedge_{\mathrm{cl}}^{*}M
and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}^{*}(M)
are right exact in the variable
M
in the following sense: suppose we are given a short exact sequence of discrete
R
-modules:

0\to P\to M\xrightarrow{u}N\to 0.

Then there are induced short exact sequences

\displaystyle 0\to I_{1}\to S^{*}(M)\xrightarrow{S^{*}(u)}S^{*}(N)\to 0,

\displaystyle 0\to I_{2}\to\bigwedge\nolimits_{\mathrm{cl}}^{*}M\xrightarrow{\bigwedge\nolimits_{\mathrm{cl}}^{*}(u)}\bigwedge\nolimits_{\mathrm{cl}}^{*}N\to 0,

\displaystyle 0\to I_{3}\to\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}^{*}(M)\xrightarrow{\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}^{*}(u)}\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}^{*}(N)\to 0,

where
I_{1}
is the ideal of the commutative algebra
S^{*}(M)
generated by
P
(
[
Bou
, Chapter III, §6.2, Proposition 4]
),
I_{2}
is the two-sided ideal of the algebra
\bigwedge_{\mathrm{cl}}^{*}(M)
generated by
P
(
[
Bou
, Chapter III, §7.2, Proposition 3]
), and
I_{3}
is the ideal of the commutative algebra
\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}^{*}(M)
generated by elements of the form
x^{(i)}
for
x\in P
,
i\geq 1
(
[
Ro
, Proposition IV.8, p. 284]
), respectively.

Remark 2.6
.

A word of
caution
: if
n\geq 2
, the functors
M\mapsto S^{n}(M),\bigwedge_{\mathrm{cl}}^{n}(M),\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl}}^{n}(M)
are
not
right exact in the usual sense as they are
not additive
. However, they are “right exact” in a “non-abelian sense” that they commute with reflexive coequalizers, which is one of the underlying reasons they have a well-behaved non-abelian derived theory. We will explore their derived functors in the following subsections.

2.2.
Non-abelian derived functors

To define and study the derived functors of symmetric, exterior, and divided powers, we will use Lurie’s general theory of non-abelian derived functors as developed in
[
HTT
, §5.5.8]
, which we review in this subsection.

To keep our exposition brief, we introduce the following notations:

•

We let
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}^{\amalg}
(resp.
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}^{\pi}
) denote the subcategory of
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
spanned by those
\infty
-categories which admit finite coproducts (resp. finite products) and those functors which preserve finite coproducts (resp. finite products). For
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}\in\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}^{\amalg}
(resp.
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}^{\pi}
), we let
\mathrm{Fun}^{\amalg}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
(resp.
\mathrm{Fun}^{\pi}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
) denote the full subcategory of
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
spanned by those functors which preserve finite coproducts (resp. finite products).

•

We let
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}^{\Sigma}
denote the subcategory of
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
spanned by those
\infty
-categories which admit small sifted colimits and those functors which preserve small sifted colimits. For
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}\in\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}^{\Sigma}
, we let
\mathrm{Fun}_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
denote the full subcategory of
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
spanned by those functors which preserve small sifted colimits.

•

We let
\widehat{\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}}_{\infty}
denote the “very large version” of
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
, that is, it is the
\infty
-category of not necessarily small
\infty
-categories. We let
\widehat{\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}}_{\infty}^{\rm L}
denote the full subcategory of
\widehat{\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}}_{\infty}
spanned by those
\infty
-categories which admit all small colimits and those functors which preserve all small colimits. For
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}\in\widehat{\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}}_{\infty}^{\rm L}
, we let
\mathrm{Fun}^{\rm L}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
denote the full subcategory of
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
spanned by those functors which preserve all small colimits.

•

Similarly, we let
\widehat{\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}}_{\infty}^{?}
denote the “very large version” of the
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}^{?}
, where
?=\amalg,\pi,\Sigma
(that is,
\widehat{\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}}_{\infty}^{?}\subseteq\widehat{\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}}_{\infty}
is defined in a same way as the above-defined
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}^{?}
with the only exception that its objects are not assumed to be small).

Let
\mathcal{C}\in\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}^{\amalg}
be an
\infty
-category that admits finite coproducts, and let
\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{C})=\mathrm{Fun}^{\pi}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathcal{S})
denote the
\infty
-category spanned by those functors which preserve finite products. We can think of
\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{C})
as obtained from
\mathcal{C}
by formally adjoining sifted colimit. More precisely:

Proposition 2.7

(Lurie
[
HTT
, §5.5.8]
)
.

For any
\mathcal{C}\in\widehat{\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}}_{\infty}^{\amalg}
, there exists a Yoneda functor
j\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{C})
, such that the natural transform
\{j\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{C})\}_{\mathcal{C}}
exhibits the functor

(\mathcal{C}\in\widehat{\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}}_{\infty}^{\amalg})\mapsto(\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{C})\in\widehat{\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}}_{\infty}^{\rm L})

as a left adjoint to the inclusion
\widehat{\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}}_{\infty}^{\rm L}\subseteq\widehat{\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}}_{\infty}^{\amalg}
. Moreover, given any
\mathcal{C}\in\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}^{\amalg}
:

(1)

The Yoneda functor
j\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{C})
is fully faithful and preserves finite coproducts.

(2)

For any
\mathcal{D}\in\widehat{\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}}_{\infty}^{\Sigma}
, composition with
j
induces an equivalence of
\infty
-categories

\mathrm{Fun}_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{C}),\mathcal{D})\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}).

Moreover, a functor
F\colon\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{D}
belongs to
\mathrm{Fun}_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{C}),\mathcal{D})
if and only if
F
is a left Kan extension of
f=F\circ j
along
j
.

(3)

For any
\mathcal{D}\in\widehat{\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}}_{\infty}^{\rm L}
, composition with
j
restricts to an equivalence of
\infty
-categories

\mathrm{Fun^{L}}(\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{C}),\mathcal{D})\to\mathrm{Fun}^{\amalg}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}).

Combining with (
2
), we obtain that for any given functor
F\in\mathrm{Fun}_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{C}),\mathcal{D})
,
F
preserves all small colimits if and only if
f=F\circ j
preserves finite coproducts.

Proof.

This is a reformulation of
[
HTT
, Propositions 5.5.8.10, 5.5.8.15]
in view of the adjunction provided by
[
HTT
, Corollary 5.3.6.10]
.
∎

Definition 2.8
.

In the situation of Proposition
2.7
(
2
) or (
3
), we will refer to the functor
F
as the
derived functor
of
f=F|\mathcal{C}
. By virtue of Proposition
2.7
, the functor
F
is essentially uniquely determined by
f
and preserves sifted colimits.

2.3.
Derived symmetric, exterior, and divided powers

In this subsection, we apply Lurie’s machinery of §
2.2
to the classical functors of §
2.1
.

Notation 2.9

(
[
SAG
, §25.2.1]
)
.

Let
A
be a simplicial commutative ring and let
A^{\circ}
be its underlying
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-ring. We let
\operatorname{SCRMod}^{\rm cn}:=\operatorname{CAlg}^{\Delta}\times_{\operatorname{CAlg}}\operatorname{Mod}^{\mathrm{cn}}
denote the
\infty
-category of pairs
(A,M)
, where
A
is a simplicial commutative ring and
M
is a connective
A^{\circ}
-module. Then the forgetful functor
q\colon\operatorname{SCRMod}^{\rm cn}\to\operatorname{CAlg}^{\Delta}
is a coCartesian ﬁbration which classifies the functor
(A\in\operatorname{CAlg}^{\Delta})\mapsto(\operatorname{Mod}^{\mathrm{cn}}_{A}\in\widehat{\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}}_{\infty})
. Let
{\rm PolyMod}^{\rm ff}\subseteq\operatorname{SCRMod}^{\rm cn}
denote the full subcategory spanned by elements of the form
(A=\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}],M=A^{n})
for
m,n\geq 0
. Then the inclusion
{\rm PolyMod}^{\rm ff}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{SCRMod}^{\rm cn}
extends to an equivalence of
\infty
-categories
\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}({\rm PolyMod}^{\rm ff})\simeq\operatorname{SCRMod}^{\rm cn}
(
[
SAG
, Proposition 25.2.1.2]
).

Construction 2.10

(Derived symmetric, exterior and divided powers; Lurie
[
SAG
, §25.2.2]
)
.

In the situation of Proposition
2.7
, we let
\mathcal{C}={\rm PolyMod}^{\rm ff}
,
\mathcal{D}=\operatorname{SCRMod}^{\rm cn}
(Notation
2.9
) and let
f\colon{\rm PolyMod}^{\rm ff}\to{\rm PolyMod}^{\rm ff}\subseteq\operatorname{SCRMod}^{\rm cn}
be one of classical functors considered in §
2.1
. Then we obtain the
derived
functor of
f
. More concretely, let
n\geq 0
be an integer:

(1)

Let
f
be the functor
(R,M)\mapsto(R,S_{R}^{n}(M))
, where
S_{R}^{n}(M)
is the classical
n
th symmetric power of
M
over
R
(Definition
2.3
). Then the derived functor of
f
has the form
F(A,M)=(A,\operatorname{Sym}_{A}^{n}M)
, where
A\in\operatorname{CAlg}^{\Delta}
and
\operatorname{Sym}_{A}^{n}M\in\operatorname{Mod}_{A}^{\mathrm{cn}}
. The connective
A
-module
\operatorname{Sym}_{A}^{n}M
is called the
derived
n
th symmetric power of
M
over
A
.

(2)

Let
f
be the functor
(R,M)\mapsto(R,\bigwedge_{\mathrm{cl},R}^{n}(M))
, where
\bigwedge_{\mathrm{cl},R}^{n}(M))
is the classical
n
th exterior power of
M
over
R
(Definition
2.4
). Then the derived functor of
f
has the form
F(A,M)=(A,\bigwedge_{A}^{n}M)
, where
A\in\operatorname{CAlg}^{\Delta}
and
\bigwedge_{A}^{n}M\in\operatorname{Mod}_{A}^{\mathrm{cn}}
. We will refer to the connective
A
-module
\bigwedge_{A}^{n}M
as the
derived
n
th exterior power of
M
over
A
.

(3)

Let
f
be the functor
(R,M)\mapsto(R,\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl},R}^{n}(M))
, where
\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl},R}^{n}(M))
is the classical
n
th divided power of
M
over
R
(Definition
2.5
). Then the derived functor of
f
has the form
F(A,M)=(A,\Gamma_{A}^{n}M)
, where
A\in\operatorname{CAlg}^{\Delta}
and
\Gamma_{A}^{n}M\in\operatorname{Mod}_{A}^{\mathrm{cn}}
. We will refer to the connective
A
-module
\Gamma_{A}^{n}M
as the
derived
n
th divdied power of
M
over
A
.

For readers’ convenience, we list some of the basic properties of these functors that are frequently used in this paper; all these results in our context are due to Lurie:

Proposition 2.11

(Lurie
[
SAG
, §25.2]
)
.

The derived symmetric, exterior, and divided power functors defined in Construction
2.10
satisfy the following properties:

(1)

(Base change) Let
A\to B
be a morphism of simplicial commutative rings. Then for any connective
A
-module
M
and an integer
n\geq 0
, there are canonical equivalences:

\displaystyle B\otimes_{A}\operatorname{Sym}_{A}^{n}(M)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Sym}_{B}^{n}(B\otimes_{A}M);

\displaystyle B\otimes_{A}\bigwedge\nolimits_{A}^{n}(M)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\sim}\bigwedge\nolimits_{B}^{n}(B\otimes_{A}M);

\displaystyle B\otimes_{A}\Gamma_{A}^{n}(M)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\sim}\Gamma_{B}^{n}(B\otimes_{A}M).

(2)

(Freeness) For any simplicial commutative ring
A\in\operatorname{CAlg}^{\Delta}
and an integer
n\geq 0
, if the
A
-module
M
is (locally) free of ﬁnite rank
r
, then
\operatorname{Sym}_{A}^{n}M
,
\bigwedge_{A}^{n}M
and
\Gamma_{A}^{n}M
are (locally) free of ﬁnite ranks
{n+r-1\choose n}
,
{r\choose n}
, and respectively
{n+r-1\choose n}
. If
M
is flat, then
\operatorname{Sym}_{A}^{n}M
,
\bigwedge_{A}^{n}M
and
\Gamma_{A}^{n}M
are flat.

(3)

(Flat modules over discrete rings) If
A=R
is an ordinary commutative ring, and
M
is a flat
A
-module, then there are canonical isomorphisms

\operatorname{Sym}_{R}^{n}M\simeq\operatorname{Sym}_{\mathrm{cl},R}^{n}M,\qquad\bigwedge\nolimits_{R}^{n}M\simeq\bigwedge\nolimits_{\mathrm{cl},R}^{n}M,\qquad\Gamma_{R}^{n}(M)\simeq\Gamma_{\mathrm{cl},R}^{n}(M).

(In this situation, we will often abuse notation and use
\operatorname{Sym}_{R}^{n}M
,
\bigwedge_{A}^{n}M
and
\Gamma_{A}^{n}M
to denote the corresponding classical constructions.)

(4)

(Illusie’s proposition) Let
A
be an simplicial commutative ring, then for any connective
A
-module
M
and an integer
n\geq 0
, there are canonical equivalences of connective
A
-modules
\operatorname{Sym}_{A}^{n}(\Sigma M)\simeq\Sigma^{n}\bigwedge_{A}^{n}(M)
and
\bigwedge_{A}^{n}(\Sigma M)\simeq\Sigma^{n}\Gamma_{A}^{n}(M)
.

Proof.

The assertion (
1
) is
[
SAG
, Proposition 25.2.3.1]
, the assertion (
2
) is
[
SAG
, Corollary 25.2.3.2, Corollary 25.2.3.3]
, the assertion (
3
) is
[
SAG
, Proposition 25.2.3.4]
, and the assertion (
4
) is
[
SAG
, Proposition 25.2.4.2]
.
∎

2.4.
Derived symmetric algebras

In this subsection, we review the construction of derived symmetric algebras and study their basic properties.

Definition 2.12

(Derived symmetric algebras;
[
SAG
, Construction 25.2.2.6]
)
.

Let
\operatorname{SCRMod}^{\rm cn}
be defined as in Notation
2.9
, and let
\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{1},\operatorname{CAlg}^{\Delta})
denote the functor
\infty
-category whose objects are morphisms of simplicial commutative rings
A\to B
. Then the forgetful functor

{\rm forg}\colon\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{1},\operatorname{CAlg}^{\Delta})\to\operatorname{SCRMod}^{\rm cn},\quad(A\to B)\mapsto(A,B^{\circ})

admits a left adjoint functor of the following form:

\operatorname{SCRMod}^{\rm cn}\to\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{1},\operatorname{CAlg}^{\Delta}),\quad(A,M)\mapsto(A,\operatorname{Sym}_{A}^{*}(M)).

The
A
-algebra
\operatorname{Sym}_{A}^{*}(M)\in\operatorname{CAlg}^{\Delta}_{A}
is called
derived symmetric algebra of
M
over
A
. By virtue of
[
SAG
, Construction 25.2.2.6]
, there is a canonical equivalence of
A
-modules

\operatorname{Sym}_{A}^{*}(M)\simeq\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\operatorname{Sym}_{A}^{n}(M).

Remark 2.13

(The underlying commutative algebra objects of derived symmetric algebras)
.

Let
A
be a simplicial commutative ring, and let
\Theta\colon\operatorname{CAlg}^{\Delta}\to\operatorname{CAlg}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\mathrm{cn}}
denote the functor of taking underlying
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-rings (
[
SAG
, Construction 25.1.2.1]
). Then
\Theta
induces a forgetful functor

\Theta_{A}\colon\operatorname{CAlg}^{\Delta}_{A}\to\operatorname{CAlg}^{\mathrm{cn}}_{\Theta(A)/}\simeq\operatorname{CAlg}(\operatorname{Mod}_{A}^{\mathrm{cn}}),

where the last equivalence is given by
[
HA
, Corollary 3.4.1.7]
. For an object
B\in\operatorname{CAlg}^{\Delta}_{A}
, we will refer to its image
\Theta_{A}(B)\in\operatorname{CAlg}(\operatorname{Mod}_{A}^{\mathrm{cn}})
as the
underlying commutative algebra object in
\operatorname{Mod}_{A}^{\mathrm{cn}}
of
B
. For any connective
A
-module
M
, we will generally abuse notations by using the notation
\operatorname{Sym}_{A}^{*}(M)
to denote the derived symmetric algebra of
M
over
A
, its underlying commutative algebra object in
\operatorname{Mod}_{A}^{\mathrm{cn}}
, and its underlying connective
A
-module. In particular, the underlying connective
A
-module
\operatorname{Sym}_{A}^{*}(M)\simeq\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\operatorname{Sym}_{A}^{n}(M)
is equipped with a multiplication map

m\colon\operatorname{Sym}_{A}^{*}(M)\otimes_{A}\operatorname{Sym}_{A}^{*}(M)\to\operatorname{Sym}_{A}^{*}(M)

which is unital, commutative, and associative up to homotopy. Furthermore, the multiplication is
graded
, that is,
m
restricts to a map of connective
A
-modules

\operatorname{Sym}_{A}^{n}(M)\otimes_{A}\operatorname{Sym}_{A}^{n^{\prime}}(M)\to\operatorname{Sym}_{A}^{n+n^{\prime}}(M).

(To prove this assertion, observe that since the relative tensor product
\otimes_{A}\colon\operatorname{Mod}_{A}^{\mathrm{cn}}\times\operatorname{Mod}_{A}^{\mathrm{cn}}\to\operatorname{Mod}_{A}^{\mathrm{cn}}
preserves sifted colimits separately in each variable, we might regard the multiplication
m
as a functor from
\operatorname{SCRMod}^{\rm cn}
to
\mathcal{E}
, where
\mathcal{E}
is defined as in Notation
2.18
. Then since
m
preserves sifted colimits, it is determined by its restrictions to
{\rm PolyMod}^{\rm ff}
, for which the desired statements are straightforward.)

Remark 2.14

(Coproducts in
\operatorname{SCRMod}^{\rm cn}
and
