. This defines a natural transform
\iota\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})\to\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
such that
\iota^{*}
preserves the tautological quotients. To show
\iota
is a closed immersion, we may reduce to the case where
X=\operatorname{Spec}R
. Let
\{t_{i}\colon\mathscr{O}_{X}\to\mathscr{F}\}_{i\in I}
be a family of sections that is jointly surjective on
\pi_{0}
, then by our assumption on
\varphi
,
\{\varphi\circ t_{i}\colon\mathscr{O}_{X}\to\mathscr{E}\}_{i\in I}
is also jointly surjective on
\pi_{0}
. As in the proof of Proposition
4.18
, we let
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
and
\{V_{i}\}_{i\in I}
denote the Zariski open covers of
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
and
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})
associated to
\{t_{i}\colon\mathscr{O}_{X}\to\mathscr{F}\}_{i\in I}
and
\{\varphi\circ t_{i}\colon\mathscr{O}_{X}\to\mathscr{E}\}_{i\in I}
, respectively. Then by construction,
V_{i}
is the inverse image of
U_{i}
from the map
\iota\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})\to\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
, and it suffices to show each restriction
\iota|_{V_{i}}\colon V_{i}\to U_{i}
is a closed immersion. The argument of Remark
4.20
shows that
\iota|_{V_{i}}\colon V_{i}\to U_{i}
is the base change of the map
\mathbb{V}(\varphi^{*})\colon\mathbb{V}(\mathscr{E})\to\mathbb{V}(\mathscr{F})
along the section map
s_{X}\colon X\to\mathbb{V}(\mathscr{O}_{X})
. By virtue of Proposition
4.10
(
6
),
\mathbb{V}(\varphi^{*})
is a closed immersion, whence so is
\iota|_{V_{i}}\colon V_{i}\to U_{i}
. This proves assertion (
1
).

We now prove (
2
). By virtue of Theorem
4.27
, we have a diagram in
\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}))
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{*}L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})/X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})/X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})/\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{pr}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})}^{*}\mathscr{F})\otimes\mathscr{O}(-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{pr}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})}^{*}\mathscr{E})\otimes\mathscr{O}(-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm cofib}(\mathrm{pr}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})}^{*}\varphi)\otimes\mathscr{O}(-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{*}\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})}^{*}\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{*}\rho_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}\otimes\mathscr{O}(-1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})}\otimes\mathscr{O}(-1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

In the above diagram: the first row is the fiber sequence of relative cotangent complexes obtained from the composition
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})\to\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})\to X
(see
[
DAG
, Proposition 3.2.12]
, which furthermore implies the existence of
L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})/\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}
). The first and second columns are the fiber sequences of Theorem
4.27
which also implies the two solid squares are commutative. Therefore by virtue of the property (
TR4
) of the stable
\infty
-category
\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}))
(see
[
HA
, Theorem 1.1.2.14]
), there exists essentially a unique vertical dashed arrow rending the above diagram commutative and inducing an equivalence between
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})/\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})}
and
{\rm cofib}(\mathrm{pr}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})}^{*}\varphi)\otimes\mathscr{O}(-1)\simeq\mathrm{pr}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})}^{*}({\rm cofib}(\varphi))\otimes\mathscr{O}(-1)
.
∎

Proposition 4.33
.

Let
X
be a prestack and
\mathscr{F}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\varphi^{\prime}}\mathscr{F}\xrightarrow{\varphi^{\prime\prime}}\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime}
a fiber sequence of connective quasi-coherent sheaves on
X
. Then there is a pullback diagram of prestacks:

(4.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}(\mathrm{pr}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}^{*}\mathscr{F}^{\prime}\otimes\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}(-1)),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\varphi^{\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\varphi^{\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\varphi^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\mathbf{0}}}

where
\iota_{\varphi^{\prime\prime}}\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime})\to\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
is the closed immersion induced by
\varphi^{\prime\prime}\colon\mathscr{F}\to\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime}
(see Corollary
4.32
(
1
));
i_{\mathbf{0}}
and
i_{\varphi^{\prime}}
are sections of the projection
\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}(\mathrm{pr}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}^{*}\mathscr{F}^{\prime}\otimes\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}(-1))\to\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
that classify the zero map
\mathrm{pr}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}^{*}\mathscr{F}^{\prime}\otimes\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}(-1)\to\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}
and the map
\rho_{\varphi^{\prime}}
obtained by tensoring the composition
\mathrm{pr}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}^{*}\mathscr{F}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{pr}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}^{*}\varphi^{\prime}}\mathrm{pr}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}^{*}\mathscr{F}\xrightarrow{\rho_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}}\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}(1)
with
\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}(-1)
, respectively. In other words,
\iota_{\varphi^{\prime\prime}}
identifies
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime})
as the derived zero locus of the canonical cosection
\rho_{\varphi^{\prime}}\colon\mathrm{pr}^{*}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}\mathscr{F}^{\prime}\otimes\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}(-1)\to\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}
determined by
\varphi^{\prime}
in the sense of Definition
4.13
.

Proof.

Since the composition map
\mathrm{pr}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime})}^{*}\mathscr{F}^{\prime}\to\mathrm{pr}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime})}^{*}\mathscr{F}\to\mathrm{pr}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime})}^{*}\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime}\to\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime})}(1)
is nullhomotopic, we see that the maps
i_{\varphi^{\prime}}\circ\iota_{\varphi^{\prime\prime}}
and
i_{\mathbf{0}}\circ\iota_{\varphi^{\prime\prime}}
both classify the zero map
\mathrm{pr}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime})}^{*}\mathscr{F}^{\prime}\otimes\iota_{\varphi^{\prime\prime}}^{*}\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}(-1)\to\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime})}
, hence the diagram (
4.4
) commutes.

To show that (
4.4
) is a pullback diagram, it suffices to prove the assertion in the case where
X=\operatorname{Spec}R
,
R\in\operatorname{CAlg}^{\Delta}
. Let
\{U_{i}\subseteq\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})\}_{i\in I}
and
\{V_{i}\subseteq\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime})\}_{i\in I}
be the Zariski open covers as in the proof of Corollary
4.32
, that is, they are Zariski open covers of
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
and
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime})
associated to some generating family of sections
\{t_{i}\colon\mathscr{O}_{X}\to\mathscr{F}\}_{i\in I}
and
\{\varphi^{\prime\prime}\circ t_{i}\colon\mathscr{O}_{X}\to\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime}\}_{i\in I}
, respectively. Since the assertion is local on
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
with respect to Zariski topology, it suffices to show for each
i\in I
, the restriction of the diagram (
4.4
) to each
U_{i}
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{V}_{U_{i}}(\mathrm{pr}_{U_{i}}^{*}\mathscr{F}^{\prime}\otimes\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}(-1)|_{U_{i}}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\varphi^{\prime\prime}}|V_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\varphi^{\prime\prime}}|V_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\varphi^{\prime}}|U_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\mathbf{0}}|U_{i}}

is a pullback square. By virtue of Remark
4.20
,
\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}(-1)|_{U_{i}}
is trivial, and the above diagram is a base change of the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{V}(\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{V}(\mathscr{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{V}(\mathscr{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{V}(\mathscr{F}^{\prime})\times_{X}\mathbb{V}(\mathscr{F}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{V}(\rho^{\prime\prime*})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{V}(\rho^{\prime\prime*})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\varphi^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\mathbf{0}}}

which is a pullback square by virtue of Remark
4.12
. This proves the desired assertion.
∎

Example 4.34
.

Let
X
be a prestack, and let
\mathscr{F}
be a connective quasi-coherent sheaf on
X
, and let
\rho\colon\mathscr{F}\to\mathscr{O}_{X}
be a cosection. Then the closed immerison
\mathbb{P}(\operatorname{cofib}(\rho))\to\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{O}_{X})\simeq X
identifies
\mathbb{P}(\operatorname{cofib}(\rho))
as the derived zero locus
Z(\rho)
of the cosection
\rho
(Definition
4.13
).

Example 4.35
.

Let
X
be a prestack, let
\mathscr{V}
be a vector bundle on
X
, and let
\mathscr{F}=\operatorname{cofib}(\mathscr{V}\xrightarrow{0}\mathscr{O}_{X})
denote the cofiber of the zero cosection map. Then Proposition
4.33
implies that there is a canonical closed immersion
\iota\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})\hookrightarrow X
which fits into a pullback diagram of prestacks:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{V}(\mathscr{V}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\mathbf{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\mathbf{0}}}

Combining with Proposition
4.11
, we obtain a canonical equivalence
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})\simeq\mathbb{V}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{V})
. More generally, let
\mathscr{E}
be any connective quasi-coherent sheaf on
X
, and let
\mathscr{F}=\Sigma\mathscr{V}\oplus\mathscr{E}
, where
\mathscr{V}
is a vector bundle on
X
. Then by virtue of Proposition
4.33
, the canonical map
\mathscr{E}\to\mathscr{F}
induces a closed immersion
\iota\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})
which fits into a pullback diagram of prestacks:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})}(\mathrm{pr}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})}^{*}\mathscr{V}\otimes\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})}(-1)).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\mathbf{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\mathbf{0}}}

Combining with Proposition
4.11
, we obtain a canonical equivalence

\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})\simeq\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{V}\boxtimes\mathscr{O}(-1))

where
\mathscr{V}\boxtimes\mathscr{O}(-1)
denote the vector bundle
\mathrm{pr}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})}^{*}\mathscr{V}\otimes\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})}(-1)
on
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E})
.

5.
Generalized Serre’s theorem

Let
\kappa
be a commutative ring, and
V=\kappa^{\oplus n+1}
a free
\kappa
-module of rank
(n+1)
, where
n\geq 0
is an integer. We let
\mathbb{P}_{\kappa}^{n}=\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{Spec}\kappa}(V)
denote the projective space over
\kappa
of dimension
n
. Let
\mathscr{O}(1)
denote the universal line bundle on
\mathbb{P}_{\kappa}^{n}
, let
\mathscr{O}(d)=\mathscr{O}(1)^{\otimes d}
if
d\geq 0
and
\mathscr{O}(d)=(\mathscr{O}(1)^{\vee})^{\otimes-d}
if
d<0
. A theorem of Serre explicitly computes the cohomologies of line bundles
\mathscr{O}(d)
over
\mathbb{P}_{\kappa}^{n}
. More concretely:

Theorem 5.1

(Serre)
.

(1)

If
d\geq 0
, then there is a canonical equivalence
\operatorname{H}^{*}(\mathbb{P}_{\kappa}^{n};\mathscr{O}(d))\simeq S^{d}_{\kappa}(V)
. In particular,
\operatorname{H}^{*}(\mathbb{P}_{\kappa}^{n};\mathscr{O}(d))
is a free
\kappa
-module of rank
\binom{n+d}{n}
concentrated in degree
0
. (Here,
\operatorname{H}^{*}(\mathbb{P}_{\kappa}^{n};\mathscr{O}(d))\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\kappa}
denotes the total cohomology of
\mathscr{O}(d)
over
\mathbb{P}_{\kappa}^{n}
.)

(2)

If
d<0
, then there are canonical equivalences

\operatorname{H}^{*}(\mathbb{P}_{\kappa}^{n};\mathscr{O}(d))\simeq\Sigma^{-n}(S_{\kappa}^{-d-n-1}V)^{\vee}\simeq\Sigma^{-n}\Gamma_{\kappa}^{-d-n-1}(V^{\vee})

(where
\Gamma_{\kappa}^{m}(V^{\vee})
is the divided power). In particular, if
-n\leq d\leq-1
, then
\operatorname{H}^{*}(\mathbb{P}_{\kappa}^{n};\mathscr{O}(d))
vanishes; if
d\leq-n-1
, then
\operatorname{H}^{*}(\mathbb{P}_{\kappa}^{n};\mathscr{O}(d))
is a degree shift of a free
\kappa
-module of rank
\binom{-d-1}{n}
placed in cohomological degree
n
.

In
[
SAG
, Theorem 5.4.2.6]
, Lurie extends the above Serre’s result in the framework of spectral algebraic geometry to projective spaces over all
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-rings
\kappa
, which, in particular, implies the classical Serre’s theorem
5.1
in the form as we formulate above.

In this section, we generalize the above Serre’s result in a different direction, that is, we show it holds
beyond
the cases of projective bundles. Let
X
be a prestack and
\mathscr{F}
a quasi-coherent sheaf over
X
of perfect-amplitude contained in
[0,1]
. We show that Serre’s theorem holds for the derived projectivization
\mathrm{pr}\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})\to X
(Theorem
5.6
). Our version of Serre’s theorem also includes the cases where the sheaves
\mathscr{F}
have non-positive ranks; in this case, the formula for the “global sections” of
\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}(d)
reproduces (a derived version of) the classical Eagon–Northcott complexes (Remark
5.9
).

5.1.
Serre’s theorem for projective bundles

In this subsection, we present a global version of Serre’s Theorem
5.1
for projectivizations of vector bundles; Our formulation closely follows Grothendieck’s in the classical setting
[
EGA
, III, 2.1.15 & 2.1.16]
.

Theorem 5.2

(Serre, Grothendieck)
.

Let
X
be a prestack, let
\mathscr{F}
be a vector bundle of rank
r\geq 1
over
X
, and let
\det\mathscr{F}=\bigwedge\nolimits^{r}\mathscr{F}\in\operatorname{Pic}(X)
denote the determinant line bundle of
\mathscr{F}
over
X
. Let
\mathrm{pr}\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})\to X
denote the derived projectivization of
\mathscr{F}
over
X
, and let
\mathscr{O}(1)
denote the universal line bundle on
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
. Let
d
be an integer, and we set
\mathscr{O}(d)=\mathscr{O}(1)^{\otimes d}
if
d\geq 0
and
\mathscr{O}(d)=(\mathscr{O}(1)^{\vee})^{\otimes-d}
if
d<0
as usual. Then:

(1)

If
d\geq 0
, then there is a canonical equivalence

\varphi_{d}\colon\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{d}(\mathscr{F})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\mathscr{O}(d)),

where
\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{d}(\mathscr{F})
denotes the
d
th symmetric power of
\mathscr{F}
over
X
(and is locally free).

(2)

If
d<0
, then the following holds:

(i)

If
-r+1\leq d\leq-1
, then
\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\mathscr{O}(d))\simeq 0
.

(ii)

If
d=-r
, then there is a canonical equivalence

\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\Sigma^{r-1}\mathrm{pr}^{*}(\det\mathscr{F})\otimes\mathscr{O}(-r))\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathscr{O}_{X}

which exhibits
\Sigma^{r-1}\mathrm{pr}^{*}(\det\mathscr{F})\otimes\mathscr{O}(-r)
as (canonically equivalent to) the relative dualizing sheaf
\omega_{\mathrm{pr}}
of the projection
\mathrm{pr}\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})\to X
(Theorem
3.7
(
22g
)).

(iii)

More generally, for every integer
d\leq-r
, there is a canonical equivalence

\psi_{d}\colon\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\mathscr{O}(d))\xrightarrow{\sim}\Sigma^{1-r}(\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{-d-r}\mathscr{F})^{\vee}\otimes(\det\mathscr{F})^{\vee}\simeq\Sigma^{1-r}(\Gamma_{X}^{-d-r}(\mathscr{F}^{\vee}))\otimes(\det\mathscr{F})^{\vee}.

Proof.

Let
\rho\colon\mathrm{pr}^{*}\mathscr{F}\to\mathscr{O}(1)
denote the tautological quotient morphism, then the fiber of
\rho
is locally free of rank
r-1
. If
d\geq 0
, then the map

\mathrm{pr}^{*}\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{d}(\mathscr{F})\simeq\operatorname{Sym}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}^{d}(\mathrm{pr}^{*}\mathscr{F})\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Sym}^{d}(\rho)}\operatorname{Sym}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}^{d}(\mathscr{O}(1))\simeq\mathscr{O}(d)

induces a canonical map

\varphi_{d}\colon\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{d}(\mathscr{F})\to\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\mathscr{O}(d)).

We wish to show that the cofiber of
\varphi_{d}
is equivalent to zero. Since the formation of
\varphi_{d}
commutes with base change (§
3.4
and Theorem
3.7
(
1
)), we may reduce to the case where
X=\operatorname{Spec}R
,
R\in\operatorname{CAlg}^{\Delta}
. Since the condition “the cofiber of
\varphi_{d}
is equivalent zero” is local on
X
with respect to Zariski topology, we may further reduce to the case where
\mathscr{F}=R^{\oplus r}
. Using the fact that the formation of
\varphi_{d}
commutes with base change again, we could further reduce to the case where
X=\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}
; in this case, the assertion follows from Theorem
5.1
. This proves the assertion (
1
). The assertion (
2i
) can be proved in the same way.

To prove assertion (
2ii
), we apply Construction
3.18
to the twisted tautological quotient

\rho(-1)=\rho\otimes\mathscr{O}(-1)\colon\mathrm{pr}^{*}\mathscr{F}\otimes\mathscr{O}(-1)\to\mathscr{O}

in the case where
n=r
. Then for all
0\leq i\leq r
, there are canonically defined quasi-coherent sheaves
\mathscr{G}_{i,r}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}),\rho(-1))
which fit into canonical fiber sequences
\beta_{i,r}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}),\rho(-1))
:

\Sigma^{i}\,\bigwedge\nolimits^{i+1}(\mathrm{pr}^{*}\mathscr{F})\otimes\mathscr{O}(-i-1)\to\mathscr{G}_{i,r}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}),\rho(-1))\to\mathscr{G}_{i+1,r}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}),\rho(-1)),

for which there are canonical equivalences
\mathscr{G}_{0,r}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}),\rho(-1))\simeq\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}
and

\mathscr{G}_{r,r}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}),\rho(-1))\simeq\operatorname{Sym}^{r}(\operatorname{cofib}(\rho(-1)))\simeq\Sigma^{r}\bigwedge\nolimits^{r}\operatorname{fib}(\rho(-1))\simeq 0.

In particular, combining with assertion (
2i
), the application of the exact functor
\mathrm{pr}_{*}
to the fiber sequences
\{\beta_{i,r}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}),\rho(-1))\}_{0\leq i\leq r-2}
induces canonical equivalences

\mathscr{O}_{X}\simeq\mathrm{pr}_{*}\mathscr{G}_{0,r}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}),\rho(-1))\simeq\mathrm{pr}_{*}\mathscr{G}_{1,r}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}),\rho(-1))\simeq\cdots\simeq\mathrm{pr}_{*}\mathscr{G}_{r-1,r}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}),\rho(-1));

and the application of
\mathrm{pr}_{*}
to
\beta_{r-1,r}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}),\rho(-1))
induces a canonical equivalence

\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\Sigma^{r-1}(\bigwedge\nolimits^{r}\mathrm{pr}^{*}\mathscr{F})\otimes\mathscr{O}(-r))\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{pr}_{*}\mathscr{G}_{r-1,r}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}),\rho(-1))\simeq\mathscr{O}_{X}.

This proves assertion (
2ii
). Finally, for any
d\leq-r
, we let
e=-r-d\geq 0
. By applying (
1
) to
\mathscr{O}(e)
, we obtain a canonical equivalence:

\varphi_{e}^{\vee}\colon(\mathrm{pr}_{*}\mathscr{O}(e))^{\vee}\xrightarrow{\sim}(\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{e}(\mathscr{F}))^{\vee}.

By virtue of Theorem
3.7
(
22d
,
22g
), there is a canonical equivalence

(\mathrm{pr}_{*}\mathscr{O}(e))^{\vee}\simeq\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\mathscr{O}(-e)\otimes\omega_{\mathrm{pr}}).

Composing with the equivalence of (
2ii
), we obtain a canonical equivalence

\displaystyle\psi_{d}\colon\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\mathscr{O}(d))

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\mathscr{O}(-e)\otimes\omega_{\mathrm{pr}})\otimes\Sigma^{1-r}(\det\mathscr{F})^{\vee}

\displaystyle\simeq(\mathrm{pr}_{*}\mathscr{O}(e))^{\vee}\otimes\Sigma^{1-r}(\det\mathscr{F})^{\vee}\xrightarrow{\varphi_{e}^{\vee}}(\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{e}(\mathscr{F}))^{\vee}\otimes\Sigma^{1-r}(\det\mathscr{F})^{\vee}.

This proves assertion (
2iii
).
∎

Remark 5.3

(Grothendieck–Serre duality for projective bundles)
.

The above proof of assertion Theorem
5.2
(
2iii
) implies that, for any
d\geq 0
, the composite map

\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\mathscr{O}(d))\otimes\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\Sigma^{r-1}\mathscr{O}(-d-r)\otimes\mathrm{pr}^{*}(\det\mathscr{F}))\to\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\Sigma^{r-1}\mathrm{pr}^{*}(\det\mathscr{F})\otimes\mathscr{O}(-r))\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathscr{O}_{X}

is a duality datum in the symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
\operatorname{QCoh}(X)^{\otimes}
(in the sense of
[
HA
, Definition 4.6.1.7]
). This statement is usually referred to as the
Grothendieck–Serre duality
for the projective bundle
\mathrm{pr}\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})\to X
.

5.2.
Generalized Serre’s theorem

In this subsection, we generalize the above Theorem
5.2
of Serre and Grothendieck beyond the cases of projective bundles.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
X
be a prestack, let
\sigma\colon\mathscr{W}\to\mathscr{V}
be a map between vector bundles on
X
, where
\mathscr{W}
and
\mathscr{V}
are vector bundles of ranks
m
and
n
, respectively. Let
\mathscr{F}
denote the cofiber of
\sigma
and set
r=n-m
. Let
\mathrm{pr}\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})\to X
and
q\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})\to X
denote the derived projectivizations. Let
\varphi\colon q^{*}\mathscr{W}\to\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(1)
denote the composite map of
q^{*}\sigma
with the tautological quotient
\rho_{\mathscr{V}}\colon q^{*}\mathscr{V}\to\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(1)
, and let
\iota\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})\to\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})
denote the closed immersion induced by the surjection
\mathscr{V}\to\mathscr{F}
(Corollary
4.32
). Then:

(1)

The projection
\mathrm{pr}\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})\to X
is proper, quasi-smooth of relative dimension
r
.

(2)

For any integer
d
, we let
\mathscr{G}_{i}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V});d)
denote the quasi-coherent sheaf

\mathscr{G}_{i,m}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}),q^{*}\mathscr{W}\xrightarrow{\varphi}\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(1))\otimes\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(d-m)

for all
0\leq i\leq m
, where
\mathscr{G}_{i,m}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}),\varphi)
is defined as in Construction
3.18
. Then there are canonical equivalences
\mathscr{G}_{0}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V});d)\simeq\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(d)
and
\mathscr{G}_{m}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V});d)\simeq\iota_{*}(\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}(d))
. Furthermore, there are canonical fiber sequences
\beta_{i}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V});d)
:

\mathscr{G}_{i-1}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V});d)\to\mathscr{G}_{i}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V});d)\to\Sigma^{i}(\bigwedge\nolimits^{i}q^{*}\mathscr{W})\otimes\mathscr{O}(d-i).

(3)

Let
\omega_{\mathrm{pr}}
denote the relative dualizing sheaf of
\mathrm{pr}
. There is a canonical equivalence

\Sigma^{r-1}{\mathrm{pr}}^{*}((\bigwedge\nolimits^{m}\mathscr{W})^{\vee}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n}\mathscr{V})\otimes\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}(-r)\xrightarrow{\sim}\omega_{\mathrm{pr}}.

Remark 5.5
.

We could informally think of assertion (
2
) as the heuristic statement that “
\iota_{*}(\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}(d))
is canonically resolved by the Koszul complex
\mathbf{S}^{m}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}),\varphi)\otimes\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(m-d)
:

\bigwedge\nolimits^{m}q^{*}\mathscr{W}\otimes\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(-m+d)\xrightarrow{d_{m}^{\prime}}\cdots\to q^{*}\mathscr{W}\otimes\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(d-1)\xrightarrow{d_{1}^{\prime}}\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(d)."

This heuristic statement is rigirous whenever
X
is classical and the cosection
\varphi\colon q^{*}\mathscr{W}\to\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(1)
is Koszul-regular. In general, we could think of the canonical fiber sequences of (
2
) as “remembering” the extra information of how the complex is “assembled” from
d_{i}^{\prime}
.

Proof of Lemma
5.4
.

Assertion (
1
) is a consequence of Theorem
4.27
. Assertion (
2
) is a consequence of Proposition
4.33
, Lemma

4.16
and Remark
4.17
. Finally, the fiber sequence
\beta_{m}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V});-r)
induces a canonical map

\iota_{*}\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}(-r)\simeq\mathscr{G}_{m}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V});-r)\to\Sigma^{m}\bigwedge\nolimits^{m}(q^{*}\mathscr{W})\otimes\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(-n).

Applying the exact functor
q_{*}
and using Theorem
5.2
(
2ii
), we obtain a canonical map

\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\Sigma^{r-1}\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}(-r))\to\bigwedge\nolimits^{m}\mathscr{W}\otimes q_{*}(\Sigma^{n-1}\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(-n))\xrightarrow{\sim}\bigwedge\nolimits^{m}\mathscr{W}\otimes(\bigwedge\nolimits^{n}\mathscr{V})^{\vee}

which determines a canonical map

\theta\colon\Sigma^{r-1}{\mathrm{pr}}^{*}((\bigwedge\nolimits^{m}\mathscr{W})^{\vee}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n}\mathscr{V})\otimes\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}(-r)\to\omega_{\mathrm{pr}}.

As the formation of
\theta
commutes with base change, arguing as the proof of Theorem
5.2
(
1
), we may reduce to case where
X
is classical,
\mathscr{V}=\mathscr{O}_{X}^{\oplus n}
,
\mathscr{W}=\mathscr{O}_{X}^{\oplus m}
, and
\varphi\colon q^{*}\mathscr{W}\to\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(1)
is Koszul-regular. Then the desired equivalence follows from
\omega_{\mathrm{pr}}\simeq\omega_{\iota}\otimes\omega_{q}
(
[
SAG
, Corollary 6.4.2.8]
), Theorem
5.2
(
2ii
), and the formula for relative dualizing sheaves of Koszul-regular closed immersions (see
[
Sta
,
Tag 0BR0
]
or
[
SAG
, Example 6.4.2.9]
).
∎

The following theorem
4
4

4

Zhao has also obtained a version of the generalized Serre’s theorem
5.6
in
[
Z20
, Lemma 3.9]
(in the form the equivalence
[\mathbf{EN}_{d}(X,\sigma)]\simeq\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\mathscr{O}(d))
of Remark
5.9
).
generalize Serre’s Theorem
5.2
to the case of derived projectivizations of perfect complexes of Tor-amplitude
[0,1]
:

Theorem 5.6

(Generalized Serre’s theorem)
.

Let
X
be a prestack, let
\mathscr{F}
be a quasi-coherent sheaf on
X
of perfect-amplitude contained in
[0,1]
and constant rank
r
. Let
\mathrm{pr}\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})\to X
denote the derived projectivization of
\mathscr{F}
over
X
, let
\mathscr{O}(1)
denote the universal line bundle on
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
, and let
\rho\colon\mathrm{pr}^{*}\mathscr{F}\to\mathscr{O}(1)
denote the tautological quotient map. Then the projection
\mathrm{pr}\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})\to X
is proper, quasi-smooth of relative virtual dimension
r-1
. Furthermore, let
d
be an integer, and we set
\mathscr{O}(d)=\mathscr{O}(1)^{\otimes d}
if
d\geq 0
and
\mathscr{O}(d)=(\mathscr{O}(1)^{\vee})^{\otimes-d}
if
d<0
. Then

(1)

If
r\geq 1
, then the following holds:

(i)

If
d\geq 0
, then there is a canonical equivalence

\varphi_{d}\colon\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{d}(\mathscr{F})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\mathscr{O}(d)),

where
\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{d}(\mathscr{F})
denotes the
d
th derived symmetric power of
\mathscr{F}
over
X
.

(ii)

If
-r+1\leq d\leq-1
, then
\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\mathscr{O}(d))\simeq 0
.

(iii)

If
d=-r
, then

\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\Sigma^{r-1}\mathscr{O}(-r))

is a line bundle on
X
. We
define
the determinant line bundle of
\mathscr{F}
over
X
by the formula

\det\mathscr{F}=(\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\Sigma^{r-1}\mathscr{O}(-r)))^{\vee}\in\operatorname{Pic}(X).

Then the canonical equivalence

\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\Sigma^{r-1}\mathrm{pr}^{*}(\det\mathscr{F})\otimes\mathscr{O}(-r))\simeq\mathscr{O}_{X}

exhibits
\Sigma^{r-1}\mathrm{pr}^{*}(\det\mathscr{F})\otimes\mathscr{O}(-r)
as canonically equivalent to the relative dualizing sheaf
\omega_{\mathrm{pr}}
of the projection
\mathrm{pr}\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})\to X
(Theorem
3.7
(
22g
)).

(iv)

More generally, for every integer
d\leq-r
, there are canonical equivalences

\psi_{d}\colon\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\mathscr{O}(d))\xrightarrow{\sim}\Sigma^{1-r}(\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{-d-r}\mathscr{F})^{\vee}\otimes(\det\mathscr{F})^{\vee}.

(2)

If
r\leq 0
, the following holds:

(i)

If
d\geq-r+1
, then there is a canonical equivalence

\varphi_{d}\colon\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{d}(\mathscr{F})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\mathscr{O}(d)).

(ii)

if
d=-r
, then the cofiber of the canonical map
\Sigma^{r-1}\varphi_{-r}
is a line bundle on
X
, where

\varphi_{-r}
is the canonical map
\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{-r}\mathscr{F}\to\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\mathscr{O}(-r))
. We
define
the determinant line bundle of
\mathscr{F}
over
X
by the formula

\det\mathscr{F}=\Sigma^{1-r}\operatorname{cofib}\big(\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{-r}\mathscr{F}\xrightarrow{\varphi_{-r}}\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\mathscr{O}(-r))\big)^{\vee}\in\operatorname{Pic}(X).

Then the canonical map

\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\Sigma^{r-1}\mathrm{pr}^{*}(\det\mathscr{F})\otimes\mathscr{O}(-r))\to\mathscr{O}_{X}

exhibits
\Sigma^{r-1}\mathrm{pr}^{*}(\det\mathscr{F})\otimes\mathscr{O}(-r)
as canonically equivalent to the relative dualizing sheaf
\omega_{\mathrm{pr}}
of the projection
\mathrm{pr}\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})\to X
(Theorem
3.7
(
22g
)).

(iii)

In general, if
0\leq d\leq-r
, then there is a canonical fiber sequence

\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{d}(\mathscr{F})\xrightarrow{\varphi_{d}}\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\mathscr{O}(d))\xrightarrow{\psi_{d}}\Sigma^{1-r}(\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{-r-d}\mathscr{F})^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{X}}(\det\mathscr{F})^{\vee}.

(iv)

If
d\leq-1
, then there is a canonical equivalence

\psi_{d}\colon\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\mathscr{O}(d))\xrightarrow{\sim}\Sigma^{1-r}(\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{-r-d}\mathscr{F})^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{X}}(\det\mathscr{F})^{\vee}.

Remark 5.7

(Determinant line bundles)
.

Let
X
be a prestack, and
\mathscr{F}
a quasi-coherent sheaf on
X
of perfect-amplitude contained in
[0,1]
. It is well known that
\mathscr{F}
has a well-defined line bundle
\det\mathscr{F}
. However, the usual methods of defining
\det\mathscr{F}
are either by an
ad hoc
gluing process or by deducing its existence from the theory of determinant of vector bundles via the equivalence of the
K
-theories of
\operatorname{Vect}(X)
and
\mathrm{Perf}(X)
. We could regard our definition of
\det{\mathscr{F}}
in (
1iii
) and (
2ii
) as providing an
explicit
and
definite
model for the determinant line bundle of
\mathscr{F}
. Our definition is justified by the following fact: by virtue of Lemma
5.4
(
3
), for any morphism
f\colon Y\to X
of prestacks such that
f^{*}\mathscr{F}
is represented by the cofiber of a map
\sigma\colon\mathscr{W}\to\mathscr{V}
between vector bundles over
Y
, there is a canonical equivalence

(\det\mathscr{W})^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{Y}}(\det\mathscr{V})\to f^{*}(\det\mathscr{F})\simeq\det(f^{*}\mathscr{F}).

Proof of Theorem
5.6
.

Let
\rho\colon\mathrm{pr}^{*}\mathscr{F}\to\mathscr{O}(1)
denote the tautological quotient morphism, then for all
d\geq 0
, the map

\mathrm{pr}^{*}\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{d}(\mathscr{F})\simeq\operatorname{Sym}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}^{d}(\mathrm{pr}^{*}\mathscr{F})\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Sym}^{d}(\rho)}\operatorname{Sym}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}^{d}(\mathscr{O}(1))\simeq\mathscr{O}(d)

induces a canonical map

\varphi_{d}\colon\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{d}(\mathscr{F})\to\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\mathscr{O}(d)).

We first prove assertion (
1i
). By the same argument of the proof of Theorem
5.2
(
1
), we may reduced to the case where
X=\operatorname{Spec}R
, where
R\in\operatorname{CAlg}^{\Delta}
, and
\mathscr{F}
is represented by the cofiber of a map
\sigma\colon\mathscr{W}\to\mathscr{V}
, where
\mathscr{W}=R\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{\oplus m}
and
\mathscr{V}=R\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{\oplus n}
. By virtue of the fact that the formation of
\varphi_{d}
commutes with base change, and Example
4.8
, we may reduce to the case where
X=|\mathscr{H}\kern-5.0pt\mathcalligra{om}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{Z}^{\oplus m},\mathbb{Z}^{\oplus n})|\simeq\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{mn}
, and
\mathscr{F}
is represented by the cokernel of the tautological map
\sigma\colon\mathscr{O}_{X}^{\oplus m}\to\mathscr{O}_{X}^{\oplus n}
. Let
q\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})\to X
denote the projection and let
\iota\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})\to\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})
denote the closed immersion induced by
\mathscr{V}\twoheadrightarrow\mathscr{F}
as in Lemma
5.4
. In particular, the composite map
\varphi\colon q^{*}\mathscr{W}\to q^{*}\mathscr{V}\to\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(1)
is Koszul-regular, and for any
d\in\mathbb{Z}
, the discrete sheaf
\iota_{*}(\mathscr{O}(d))
is resolved by the Koszul complex
\mathbf{S}^{m}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}),\varphi)\otimes\mathscr{O}(m-d)
, which, in this case, is a genuine acyclic complex of vector bundles (Remark
5.5
):

(5.1)

(\bigwedge\nolimits^{m}q^{*}\mathscr{W})\otimes\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(-m+d)\xrightarrow{d_{m}^{\prime}}\cdots\to(q^{*}\mathscr{W})\otimes\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(d-1)\xrightarrow{d_{1}^{\prime}}\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(d).

To prove assertion (
1i
), we observe that the canonical map
\rho\colon\mathrm{pr}^{*}\mathscr{F}\to\mathscr{O}(1)
induces a composite map
q^{*}\mathscr{F}\to\iota_{*}\mathrm{pr}^{*}\mathscr{F}\to\iota_{*}\mathscr{O}(1)
which fits into a commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{*}\mathscr{W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{*}\mathscr{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{*}\mathscr{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{*}\mathscr{W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{*}\mathscr{O}(1).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{*}\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

Therefore, the composite map
q^{*}\operatorname{Sym}^{d}_{X}(\mathscr{F})\to\iota_{*}(\operatorname{Sym}^{d}(\mathrm{pr}^{*}\mathscr{F}))\to\iota_{*}\mathscr{O}(d)
induced from the canonical map
\operatorname{Sym}^{d}(\mathrm{pr}^{*}\mathscr{F})\to\mathscr{O}(d)
is compatible with the map of complexes

\mathbf{S}^{d}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}),q^{*}\sigma)\to\mathbf{S}^{d}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}),\varphi)\to\mathbf{S}^{m}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}),\varphi)\otimes\mathscr{O}(m-d).

If we assume
d\geq 0
and
r=n-m\geq 1
, then
d-m\geq-m\geq-n+1
. If we pushforward along
q
, then by virtue of Theorem
5.2
(
1
), (
2i
) we obtain an equivalence

\mathbf{S}^{d}(X,\sigma\colon\mathscr{W}\to\mathscr{V})\simeq q_{*}\left((\mathbf{S}^{m}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}),q^{*}\mathscr{W}\to\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(1)))\otimes\mathscr{O}(m-d)\right)

which is compatible with the map
\varphi_{d}\colon\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{d}(\mathscr{F})\to q_{*}\iota_{*}(\mathscr{O}(d))=\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\mathscr{O}(d))
. Since
X
is classical, the natural map
\mathbf{S}^{d}(X,\sigma)\to\operatorname{Sym}^{d}_{X}(\mathscr{F})
induces an equivalence
[\mathbf{S}^{d}(X,\sigma)]\simeq\operatorname{Sym}^{d}_{X}(\mathscr{F})
(Corollary
2.30
), we obtain that
\varphi_{d}
is an equivalence. This proves assertion (
1i
); the same argument proves (
1ii
) and (
2i
).

We next prove assertion (
1iii
). Since the condition that “
\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\Sigma^{r-1}\mathscr{O}(-r))
is a line bundle” is local on
X
, and the formation of
\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\Sigma^{r-1}\mathscr{O}(-r))
commutes with base change, we may reduce to the above case where
X=\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{mn}
and
\mathscr{F}
is represented by the cofiber of the tautological map between vector bundles
\sigma\colon\mathscr{W}=\mathscr{O}_{X}^{\oplus m}\to\mathscr{V}=\mathscr{O}_{X}^{\oplus n}
. Setting
d=-r
in (
5.1
) and using Theorem
5.2
(
2i
), (
2ii
), we obtain a canonical equivalence

\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\Sigma^{r-1}\mathscr{O}(-r))\xrightarrow{\sim}(\bigwedge\nolimits^{m}\mathscr{W})\otimes q_{*}(\Sigma^{n-1}\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(-n))\xrightarrow{\sim}(\bigwedge\nolimits^{m}\mathscr{W})\otimes(\bigwedge\nolimits^{n}\mathscr{V})^{\vee}

This proves that
\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\Sigma^{r-1}\mathscr{O}(-r))
is a line bundle on
X
. The rest of assertion (
1iii
) follows from Lemma
5.4
(
3
).

Similarly, to prove assertion (
2ii
), we may also reduce to the above case where
X=\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{mn}
and
\mathscr{F}
is the cofiber of the tautological map
\sigma\colon\mathscr{O}_{X}^{m}\to\mathscr{O}_{X}^{n}
, and we set
d=-r
in (
5.1
). Let
\mathscr{G}_{i}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V});-r)
denote the sheaf constructed in Lemma
5.4
(
2
) (which in this case canonically represents the
i
th brutal truncation of (
5.1
)), then the proof of assertion (
1i
) implies that there is a canonical equivalence

q_{*}(\mathscr{G}_{-r}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V});-r))\simeq\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{-r}(\mathscr{F}).

By virtue of Theorem
5.2
(
2i
) and Lemma
5.4
(
2
), there are canonical equivalences

q_{*}(\mathscr{G}_{m-1}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V});-r))\simeq\cdots q_{*}(\mathscr{G}_{-r}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V});-r))\qquad q_{*}(\mathscr{G}_{m}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V});-r))\simeq\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\mathscr{O}(-r)),

and a canonical fiber sequence

q_{*}(\mathscr{G}_{m-1}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V});-r))\to q_{*}(\mathscr{G}_{m}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V});-r))\to q_{*}(\Sigma^{m}(\bigwedge\nolimits^{m}q^{*}\mathscr{W})\otimes\mathscr{O}(-n))

for which the first map is canonically equivalent to
\varphi_{-r}
. Therefore, invoking Theorem
5.2
(
2ii
), we obtain a canonical equivalence

\operatorname{cofib}\big(\varphi_{-r}\colon\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{-r}(\mathscr{F})\to\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\mathscr{O}(-r))\big)\simeq\Sigma^{1-r}(\bigwedge\nolimits^{m}\mathscr{W})\otimes(\bigwedge\nolimits^{n}\mathscr{V})^{\vee}.

The rest of assertion (
2ii
) now follows from Lemma
5.4
(
3
).

The assertions (
1iv
) and (
2iv
) can be proved in the same way as the proof of Theorem
5.2
(
2iii
), that is, by setting
e=-r-d\geq 0
, we let
\psi_{d}
denote the composite map:

\displaystyle\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\mathscr{O}(d))

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\mathscr{O}(-e)\otimes\omega_{\mathrm{pr}})\otimes\Sigma^{1-r}(\det\mathscr{F})^{\vee}

\displaystyle\simeq(\mathrm{pr}_{*}\mathscr{O}(e))^{\vee}\otimes\Sigma^{1-r}(\det\mathscr{F})^{\vee}\xrightarrow{\varphi_{e}^{\vee}}(\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{e}(\mathscr{F}))^{\vee}\otimes\Sigma^{1-r}(\det\mathscr{F})^{\vee}

where the first equivalence follows from the expressions of
\omega_{\mathrm{pr}}
of (
1ii
) and (
2iii
), and the second equivalence is the Grothendieck duality (Theorem
3.7
(
22d
)(
22g
)). Then
\psi_{d}
is equivalence since
\varphi_{e}^{\vee}
is an equivalence, which follow from assertions (
1i
) and (
2i
), respectively.

Finally, it only remains to prove assertion (
2iii
). By setting
e=-r-d\geq 0
, we let
\psi_{d}
be defined by the same formula as above. Then we have a composable pair of maps

\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{d}(\mathscr{F})\xrightarrow{\varphi_{d}}\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\mathscr{O}(d))\xrightarrow{\psi_{d}}\Sigma^{1-r}(\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{-r-d}\mathscr{F})^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{X}}(\det\mathscr{F})^{\vee}

whose formation commutes with base change. To prove that the above sequence forms a fiber sequence, we may again reduce to the above case where
X=\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{mn}
,
\mathscr{F}
is the cofiber of the tautological map
\sigma\colon\mathscr{O}_{X}^{m}\to\mathscr{O}_{X}^{n}
, and
\iota_{*}(\mathscr{O}(d))
is resolved by the Koszul complex (
5.1
). We consider the following (shifted) truncations of the Koszul complex (
5.1
):

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{d}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}),\varphi)\colon

\displaystyle(\bigwedge\nolimits^{d}q^{*}\mathscr{W})\ \xrightarrow{d_{d}^{\prime}}\cdots\xrightarrow{d_{2}^{\prime}}(q^{*}\mathscr{W})\otimes\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(d-1)\xrightarrow{d_{1}^{\prime}}\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(d),

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{d}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}),\varphi)\colon

\displaystyle(\bigwedge\nolimits^{d+n-1}q^{*}\mathscr{W})\otimes\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(-n+1)\xrightarrow{d_{d+n-1}^{\prime}}\cdots\xrightarrow{d_{d+1}^{\prime}}(\bigwedge\nolimits^{d+1}q^{*}\mathscr{W})\otimes\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(-1)

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{e}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}),\varphi)\colon

\displaystyle(\bigwedge\nolimits^{m}q^{*}\mathscr{W})\otimes\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(d-m)\xrightarrow{d_{m}^{\prime}}\cdots\xrightarrow{d_{d+n+1}^{\prime}}(\bigwedge\nolimits^{d+n}q^{*}\mathscr{W})\otimes\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(-n).

(Here, the first terms of all three complexes are placed in degree
0
.) Then there are canonical isomoprhisms of complexes of vector bundles:

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{d}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}),\varphi)\simeq\mathbf{S}^{d}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}),\varphi)\qquad(\mathbf{H}_{e}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}),\varphi))^{\vee}\simeq\Sigma^{-e}\,(q^{*}\det\mathscr{W})^{\vee}\otimes\mathbf{S}^{e}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}),\varphi)\otimes\mathscr{O}(n)

(where
\mathbf{S}^{d}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}),\varphi)
denotes the Koszul complex defined by formula (
2.2
) as usual, and we let
(\mathbf{H}_{e}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}),\varphi))^{\vee}
denote the term-wise dual of the complex
\mathbf{H}_{e}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}),\varphi)
). Moreover, there are canonical fiber sequences determined by the original complex (
5.1
):

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{d}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}),\varphi)\to\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}_{d}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}),\varphi)\to\Sigma^{d+1}\,\mathbf{E}_{d}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}),\varphi),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}_{d}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}),\varphi)\to\iota_{*}(\mathscr{O}(d))\to\Sigma^{d+n}\,\mathbf{H}_{e}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}),\varphi).

By virtue of Theorem
5.2
(
2i
), we obtain a canonical equivalence

q_{*}[\mathbf{G}_{d}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}),\varphi)]\simeq q_{*}[\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}_{d}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}),\varphi)]

and hence a canonical fiber sequence

\displaystyle q_{*}\left[\mathbf{G}_{d}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}),\varphi)\right]\xrightarrow{\varphi_{d}^{\prime}}\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\mathscr{O}(d))\xrightarrow{\psi_{d}^{\prime}}q_{*}\left[\Sigma^{n+d}\,\mathbf{H}_{e}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}),\varphi)\right].

The proof of assertion (
1i
) implies that there is a canonical equivalence
q_{*}[\mathbf{G}_{d}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}),\varphi)]\simeq\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{d}(\mathscr{F})
such that the map
\varphi_{d}^{\prime}
is canonically equivalent to the map
\varphi_{d}\colon\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{d}(\mathscr{F})\to\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\mathscr{O}(d))
. Similarly, combing with Grothendieck duality (Theorem
3.7
(
22d
)(
22g
))

(q_{*}\left[\Sigma^{n+d}\,\mathbf{H}_{e}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}),\varphi)\right])^{\vee}\simeq q_{*}(\Sigma^{e-m}[\mathbf{H}_{e}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}),\varphi)]^{\vee}\otimes\omega_{q})\simeq\Sigma^{r-1}\det\mathscr{F}\otimes q_{*}[\mathbf{S}^{e}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}),\varphi)],

there is a canonical equivalence

(q_{*}\left[\Sigma^{n+d}\,\mathbf{H}_{e}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}),\varphi)\right])^{\vee}\simeq\Sigma^{r-1}\det\mathscr{F}\otimes\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{e}(\mathscr{F})

for which the composite map

\displaystyle\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\mathscr{O}(d))

\displaystyle\simeq(\mathrm{pr}_{*}\mathscr{O}(e))^{\vee}\otimes\Sigma^{1-r}(\det\mathscr{F})^{\vee}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\varphi_{e}^{\vee}}\Sigma^{1-r}(\det\mathscr{F})^{\vee}\otimes(\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{e}(\mathscr{F}))^{\vee}\simeq q_{*}\left[\Sigma^{n+d}\,\mathbf{H}_{e}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}),\varphi)\right]

is canonically equivalent to
\psi_{d}^{\prime}
. This proves assertion (
2iii
). ∎

Remark 5.8

(Grothendieck–Serre duality)
.

Let
\mathscr{F}
be a quasi-coherent sheaf on a prestack
X
of perfect-amplitude in
[0,1]
and rank
r
, and let
\mathrm{pr}\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})\to X
denote the derived projectivization. Then the above proof of assertions (
1iv
) and (
2iv
) of Theorem
5.6
shows that, for any
d\in\mathbb{Z}
, the composite map

\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\mathscr{O}(d))\otimes\mathrm{pr}_{*}\big(\Sigma^{r-1}\mathscr{O}(-d-r)\otimes\mathrm{pr}^{*}(\det\mathscr{F})\big)\to\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\Sigma^{r-1}\mathrm{pr}^{*}(\det\mathscr{F})\otimes\mathscr{O}(-r))\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathscr{O}_{X}

is a duality datum in the symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
\operatorname{QCoh}(X)^{\otimes}
(in the sense of
[
HA
, Definition 4.6.1.7]
; notice this statement implies that if one of the two tensor factors is zero then the other is zero). We could regard this statement as the generalization of the Grothendieck–Serre duality for the projective bundles (Remark
5.3
).

Remark 5.9

(Eagon–Northcott complexes)
.

Let
X
be a prestack, and let
\mathscr{F}
be a quasi-coherent sheaf on
X
of perfect-amplitude contained in
[0,1]
. If
\mathscr{F}
has rank
r\leq 0
, then we could regard the fiber sequences of Theorem
5.6
(
2iii
) as the
derived version
of the classical Eagon–Northcott complexes.

More concretely, if
X
is a classical scheme, and
\mathscr{F}
is represented by the cofiber of a map
\sigma\colon\mathscr{W}\to\mathscr{V}
between vector bundles on
X
, where
\operatorname{rk}\mathscr{W}=m
,
\operatorname{rk}\mathscr{V}=n
, and
r=n-m
. Then for any integer
d
, we define a complex
\mathbf{EN}_{d}(X,\sigma\colon\mathscr{W}\to\mathscr{V})
of vector bundles on
X
by the following formula: if
r=n-m>0
, then we define

\mathbf{EN}_{d}(X,\sigma)=\begin{cases}\mathbf{S}^{-r-d}(X,\sigma)^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{X}}(\det\mathscr{F})^{\vee}[1-r]&\text{if}\quad d\leq-1;\\
\mathbf{S}^{d}(X,\sigma)&\text{if}\quad d\geq 0.\end{cases}

(Here,
\mathbf{S}^{d}(X,\sigma)
denotes the complex defined by formula (
2.2
) as usual,
[\underline{\hphantom{A}}]
denotes the degree shift, and
(\underline{\hphantom{A}})^{\vee}
denotes the term-wise dual of a complex.) If
r=n-m\leq 0
, then we define

\mathbf{EN}_{d}(X,\sigma)=\begin{cases}\mathbf{S}^{-r-d}(X,\sigma)^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{X}}(\det\mathscr{F})^{\vee}[1-r]&\text{if}\quad d\leq-1;\\
\mathbf{S}^{-r-d}(X,\sigma)^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{X}}(\det\mathscr{F})^{\vee}[-r]\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}\mathbf{S}^{d}(X,\sigma)&\text{if}\quad 0\leq d\leq-r;\\
\mathbf{S}^{d}(X,\sigma)&\text{if}\quad d\geq-r+1,\end{cases}

where in the second formula, the rightmost term of the complex

\mathbf{S}^{-r-d}(X,\sigma)^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{X}}(\det\mathscr{F})^{\vee}[-r]
,

\bigwedge\nolimits^{-r-d}\mathscr{W}^{\vee}\otimes\det\mathscr{W}\otimes(\det\mathscr{V})^{\vee}\simeq\bigwedge\nolimits^{n+d}\mathscr{W}\otimes(\det\mathscr{V})^{\vee},

is placed at homological degree
d+1
, and
\varepsilon
is the composite map

\bigwedge\nolimits^{n+d}\mathscr{W}\otimes(\det\mathscr{V})^{\vee}\xrightarrow{\wedge^{n}(\sigma^{\vee})}\bigwedge\nolimits^{n+d}\mathscr{W}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n}(\mathscr{W}^{\vee})\xrightarrow{\llcorner}\bigwedge\nolimits^{d}\mathscr{W},

where the last map is the inner product of exterior algebras (
[
Bou
, Chapter III, §11.7]
). The complexes
\{\mathbf{EN}^{d}(X,\sigma)\}_{d\in\mathbb{Z}}
are usually referred to as the
Eagon–Northcott complexes
. By our construction, the sequence of complexes
\{\mathbf{EN}_{d}(X,\sigma)\}_{d\in\mathbb{Z}}
satisfies the following duality:

\mathbf{EN}_{d}(X,\sigma)\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{X}}\det\mathscr{F}\simeq\left(\mathbf{EN}_{-r-d}(X,\sigma)\right)^{\vee}.

By virtue of the equivalences
[\mathbf{S}^{d}(X,\sigma)]\simeq\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{d}(\mathscr{F})
and
[\mathbf{S}^{-r-d}(X,\sigma)]\simeq\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{-r-d}(\mathscr{F})
of Corollary
2.30
, and our proof of Theorem
5.6
(
2iii
),
it is not hard to see that the complex
\mathbf{EN}_{d}(X,\sigma)
canonically represents the cofiber of the map

\Sigma^{-r}(\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{-r-d}\mathscr{F})^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{X}}(\det\mathscr{F})^{\vee}\to\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{d}(\mathscr{F})

induced by the fiber sequence of Theorem
5.6
(
2iii
). In particular, Theorem
5.6
(
2iii
) implies that (in the case where
X
is a classical scheme) there are canonical equivalences

[\mathbf{EN}_{d}(X,\sigma)]\simeq\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\mathscr{O}(d))

for all
d\in\mathbb{Z}
. Historically, in Kempf’s approach (reviewed in
[
Laz
, §B.2]
), the above equivalence is taken as the
definition
of the Eagon–Northcott complex. We refer the readers to
[
Ei
, §A.2.6.1]
and
[
Laz
, §B.2]
for more about Eagon–Northcott complexes.

The Generalized Serre’s theorem is
not
true in general for classical projectivizations, even in the case where
\mathscr{F}
has positive rank and homological dimension one, and in degree
d=1
, as is shown in the following example:

Example 5.10

(
[
Sta
,
Tag 01OC
]
)
.

Let
X=\operatorname{Spec}A
,
A=\kappa[u,v,s_{1},s_{2},t_{1},t_{2}]/I
, where
\kappa
is a field and
I=(-us_{1}+vt_{1}+ut_{2},vs_{1}+us_{2}-vt_{2},vs_{2},ut_{1})
. Let
\overline{u}
and
\overline{v}
denote the class of
u
and
v
in
A
, respectively. Let
\mathscr{F}
be the cofiber of the map

\mathscr{O}_{X}\xrightarrow{(\overline{u},\overline{v})}\mathscr{O}_{X}^{\oplus 2}.

(Equivalently,
\mathscr{F}
is the sheaf corresponds to the module
M=(Ax\oplus Ay)/A(\overline{u}x+\overline{v}y)
.) Then the computation of
[
Sta
,
Tag 01OC
]
shows that the classical projectivization
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{cl}}(\mathscr{F})
over
X
violates the Serre’s theorem. More concretely,
loc. cit.
shows that the canonical map

\mathscr{H}^{0}(\mathscr{F})\to\mathbb{R}^{0}\mathrm{pr}_{\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{cl}}(\mathscr{F})\,*}(\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{cl}}(\mathscr{F})}(1))

is not injective, which, in particular implies that the canonical map

\mathscr{F}\to\mathrm{pr}_{\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{cl}}(\mathscr{F})\,*}(\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{cl}}(\mathscr{F})}(1))

is not an equivalence. On the other hand, we know from Theorem
5.6
(
1i
) that for the derived projectivization
\mathrm{pr}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})\to X
, the canonical map

\varphi_{d}\colon\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{d}(\mathscr{F})\to\mathrm{pr}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})\,*}(\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}(d))

is an equivalence for all
d\geq 0
.

The next example shows that, the Generalized Serre’s theorem does not hold for “three-term complexes of vector bundles”, even in very nice cases such as Koszul complexes:

Example 5.11
.

Let
X=\mathbb{A}^{2}=\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}[x,y]
, let
p
be the zero locus of defined by the maximal ideal
(x,y)
, and let
\mathscr{F}=\mathscr{O}_{p}
denote the structure sheaf of
p
. Then
\mathscr{F}
can be resolved by the Koszul complex on
(x,y)
. The classical projectivization
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{cl}}(\mathscr{F})\simeq\{p\}
, however,
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
is equipped with a non-trivial derived structure. Therefore
\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}(1))
is not isomorphic to
\mathscr{F}\simeq\mathscr{O}_{p}
but is equipped with non-trivial higher homological information.

The above two examples seem to suggest that the generalized Serre’s Theorem as we formulate in Theorem
5.6
for sheaves of perfect amplitude contained in
[0,1]
might be the most general form we could expect. However, there are possible ways to generalizing these results to sheaves of higher Tor-amplitude, if we consider variants of the derived projectivization constructions. For example, in the case of three-term complexes, if a given complex possesses certain symmetries, then we might consider the
reduced
derived structure on the projectivization (similar to the reduced obstruction theory in Donaldson–Thomas theory); it is then reasonable to expect that (variants of ) Serre’s theorem to hold in these cases.

6.
Beilinson’s relations

Let
\kappa
be a field and
V=\kappa^{\oplus n+1}
a
\kappa
-vector space of rank
(n+1)
, where
n\geq 0
is an integer. We let
\mathbb{P}_{\kappa}^{n}=\mathbb{P}_{\operatorname{Spec}\kappa}(V)
denote the projective space over
\kappa
of dimension
n
. Let
\mathscr{O}(1)
denote the universal line bundle on
\mathbb{P}_{\kappa}^{n}
, and for an integer
d
, we let set
\mathscr{O}(d)=\mathscr{O}(1)^{\otimes d}
if
d\geq 0
and
\mathscr{O}(d)=(\mathscr{O}(1)^{\vee})^{\otimes-d}
if
d<0
as usual. We let
\Omega^{1}\simeq L_{\mathbb{P}_{\kappa}^{n}/\kappa}
denote algebraic cotangent bundle of
\mathbb{P}_{\kappa}^{n}
, let
\Omega^{i}=\bigwedge\nolimits^{i}\Omega^{1}
for
i\geq 0
, and let
\Omega^{i}(j)=\Omega^{i}\otimes\mathscr{O}(j)
for
i\geq 0
and
j\in\mathbb{Z}
.

We may formulate Beilinson’s computations in
[
Be
]
in the following way:

Beilinson’s relations:

The two sequences
\{\mathscr{O},\mathscr{O}(1),\ldots,\mathscr{O}(n)\}
and
\{\Sigma^{n}\Omega^{n}(n),\ldots,\Sigma\,\Omega^{1}(1),\mathscr{O}\}
form a pair of (full) dual exceptional collections over
\kappa
. Moreover, for all integers
0\leq i,j\leq n
, the following “Beilinson’s relations” hold:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{\kappa}^{*}(\mathscr{O}(i),\mathscr{O}(j))=S_{\kappa}^{j-i}(V)\qquad\operatorname{Hom}_{\kappa}^{*}(\Omega^{i}(i),\Omega^{j}(j))=\big(\bigwedge\nolimits^{i-j}_{\kappa}V\big)^{\vee}

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{\kappa}^{*}(\mathscr{O}(i),\Sigma^{j}\,\Omega^{j}(j))=\delta_{i,j}\cdot\kappa,

where
\operatorname{Hom}_{\kappa}^{*}(\underline{\hphantom{A}},\underline{\hphantom{A}})
denotes the
\operatorname{Mod}_{\kappa}
-valued
\operatorname{Hom}
objects for quasi-coherent sheaves on
\mathbb{P}_{\kappa}^{n}
, and
\delta_{i,j}
is Kronecker delta function, that is,
\delta_{i,j}=1
if
i=j
and
\delta_{i,j}=0
if
i\neq j
.

Remark 6.1
.

A generalization of Beilinson’s result to the cases of all projectivizations of vector bundles over quasi-compact, quasi-separated schemes (together with a generalized version of Orlov’s Theorem) can be found in
[
J21
, Theorem B.3]
.

In this section, we prove a derived version of Beilinson’s relations for all derived projectivizations of quasi-coherent sheaves of perfect-amplitude contained in
[0,1]
over a perfect stack
X
(Proposition
6.19
, Corollary
6.21
). The results and proofs of §
6.2
are most suitably formulated using the framework of relative exceptional sequences and mutation theory, as developed in the classical absolute cases in
[
Bo
,
BK
,
Go
]
(see also
[
BLM+
, §3.1]
and
[
J21
, §3.11]
for the classical relative cases), which we discuss in §
6.1
.

6.1.
Exceptional sequences and mutation functors

In this subsection, we will study the theory of relative exceptional sequences and mutations for linear
\infty
-categories.

Definition 6.2
.

Let
X
be a prestack. We will refer to the
\infty
-category
\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{QCoh}(X)}(\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{St}})
as the
\infty
-category of linear stable
\infty
-categories over
X
(where
\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{St}}
is the
\infty
-category of presentable stable
\infty
-categories).
We will refer to the objects of
\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{QCoh}(X)}(\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{St}})
as
X
-linear stable
\infty
-categories
, and morphisms in
\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{QCoh}(X)}(\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{St}})
as
X
-linear functors
. The
\infty
-category
\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{QCoh}(X)}(\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{St}})
is equipped with a symmetric monoidal structure given by the relative tensor product
\otimes_{\operatorname{QCoh}(X)}
over
\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
, where
\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
is regarded as a commutative algebra object of
\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{St}}
. For any
\mathcal{C}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{QCoh}(X)}(\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{St}})
, we denote the action functor of
\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
on
\mathcal{C}
by
\otimes\colon\operatorname{QCoh}(X)\times\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
,
(\mathscr{F},C)\mapsto\mathscr{F}\otimes C
.

For technical reason, it is helpful to restrict our attention to
perfect stacks

X
, which guarantees that we have a well behaved theory of
X
-linear categories.

(\star)

Throughout this subsection, we will assume that
X
is a
perfect stack
.

Definition 6.3

(Compare with
[
SAG
, Definition 9.4.4.1]
)
.

We say a prestack
X\colon\operatorname{CAlg}^{\Delta}\to\mathcal{S}
is a
perfect stack
if it satisfies the following conditions:

(1)

X
is a quasi-geometric stack in the sense of (the derived version) of
[
SAG
, Definition 9.1.0.1]
(that is,
X
satisfies descent with respect to the fpqc topology, the diagonal map
\delta\colon X\to X\times X
is quasi-affine, and there exists a simplicial commutative ring
A\in\operatorname{CAlg}^{\Delta}
and a faithful flat morphism
u\colon\operatorname{Spec}A\to X
).

(2)

The
\infty
-category
\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
is compactly generated and the structure sheaf
\mathscr{O}_{X}
is a compact object of
\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
.

Notice that given condition
(1)
, condition
(2)
is equivalent to:

(2^{\prime})

The inclusion
\mathrm{Perf}(X)\subseteq\operatorname{QCoh}(X)

extends to an equivalence of
\infty
-categories

\operatorname{Ind}(\mathrm{Perf}(X))\simeq\operatorname{QCoh}(X).

Remark 6.4
.

Our Definition
6.3
is slightly different from the definition of a perfect stack in
[
BFN
,
GR
]
. All these notions of perfect stacks work equally fine for all the discussions of this section; we choose the above Definition
6.3
so that the following is true:

•

Any quasi-compact, quasi-separated derived scheme
X
is a perfect stack.

The above assertion can be deduced from
[
SAG
, Proposition 9.6.1.1]
, since the underlying spectral scheme
X^{\circ}
of
X
is a quasi-compact, quasi-separated spectral algebraic space. On the other hand,
[
BFN
]
requires semi-separateness as part of the definition of a perfect stack.

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
X
be a perfect stack (Definition
6.3
). Then
\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
is locally rigid (in the sense of
[
SAG
, Definition D.7.4.1]
). In particular, the following holds:

(1)

The functor
\lambda=\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\operatorname{QCoh}(X)}(\mathscr{O}_{X},\underline{\hphantom{A}})\colon\operatorname{QCoh}(X)\to\mathrm{Sp}
exhibits
\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
as a Frobenius algebra object of
\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{St}}
(in the sense of
[
HA
, Definition 4.6.5.1]
, that is, the composition
\operatorname{QCoh}(X)\otimes\operatorname{QCoh}(X)\to\operatorname{QCoh}(X)\xrightarrow{\lambda}\mathrm{Sp}
is a duality datum in the symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{St}}
).

(2)

\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
is smooth when regarded as an algebra object of
\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{St}}
(in the sense of
[
HA
, Definition 4.6.4.13]
, that is,
\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
is dualizable when regarded as a module over
\operatorname{QCoh}(X)^{\mathrm{op}}\otimes\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
).

(3)

The forgetful functor
\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{QCoh}(X)}(\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{St}})\to\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{St}}
is compatible with duality. In other words, suppose we are given an object
\mathcal{C}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{QCoh}(X)}(\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{St}})
. Then any duality datum
e\colon\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\operatorname{QCoh}(X)}\mathcal{D}\to\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
in
\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{QCoh}(X)}(\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{St}})
induces a duality datum in
\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{St}}
through the composition

\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\operatorname{QCoh}(X)}\mathcal{D}\xrightarrow{e}\operatorname{QCoh}(X)\xrightarrow{\lambda}\mathrm{Sp};

On the other hand, for any duality datum
\overline{e}\colon\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{D}\to\mathrm{Sp}
in
\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{St}}
, the
\infty
-category
\mathcal{D}
admits an action of
\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
for which
\overline{e}
factorizes as a composition

\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\operatorname{QCoh}(X)}\mathcal{D}\xrightarrow{e}\operatorname{QCoh}(X)\xrightarrow{\lambda}\mathrm{Sp},

where
e
is a duality datum in
\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{QCoh}(X)}(\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{St}})
.

Proof.

It follows from our definition of a perfect stack that
\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
is locally rigid. Then the assertions (
1
) and (
2
) are formal consequences of the locally rigidity of
\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
(
[
SAG
, Propositions D.7.5.1, D.7.7.1]
). The assertion (
3
) is a consequence of (
1
) and (
2
) (
[
HA
, Remark 4.6.5.15]
). Notice that the assertions (
1
) through (
3
) are true even under the a priori weaker assumption that
X
is weakly perfect: see
[
SAG
, Proposition 9.4.2.1, Corollaries 9.4.3.4, 9.4.3.5, 9.4.3.6]
.
∎

Construction 6.6
.

Let
X
be a perfect stack, and let
\mathcal{C}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{QCoh}(X)}(\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{St}})
be an
X
-linear stable
\infty
-category. Assume that
\mathcal{C}
is compactly generated. Therefore by virtue of
[
SAG
, Proposition D.7.2.3]
,
\mathcal{C}
is dualizable as an object of
\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{St}}
, and we can identify its dual with the
\infty
-category
\operatorname{Ind}(\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\mathrm{op}})
, where
\mathcal{C}_{c}
is the full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
spanned by compact objects. Then Proposition
6.5
(
3
) implies that
\mathcal{C}
is also dualizable as a
\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
-module; We let

e\colon\operatorname{Ind}(\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\mathrm{op}})\otimes_{\operatorname{QCoh}(X)}\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{QCoh}(X)

denote the duality datum in
\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{QCoh}(X)}(\mathcal{P}\mathrm{r}^{\mathrm{St}})
obtained from Proposition
6.5
(
3
). In particular, the restriction of
e
induces a functor

\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathcal{C}/X}\colon\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{QCoh}(X)\qquad(C,D)\mapsto\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathcal{C}/X}(C,D)

which is characterized by the following universal property: there is a canonical
evaluation
map
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathcal{C}/X}(C,D)\otimes C\to D
such that, for any
\mathscr{F}\in\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
, the composition

\mathrm{Map}_{\operatorname{QCoh}(X)}(\mathscr{F},\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathcal{C}/X}(C,D))\to\mathrm{Map}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathscr{F}\otimes C,\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathcal{C}/X}(C,D)\otimes C)\to\mathrm{Map}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathscr{F}\otimes C,D)

is a homotopy equivalence. For any pair of object
(C,D)\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}
, we will refer to
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathcal{C}/X}(C,D)
as the
mapping object
of
C
and
D
in
\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
. We will sometimes simplify the notation by denoting
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(C,D)=\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathcal{C}/X}(C,D)
. Notice that from our construction, given any compact object
C\in\mathcal{C}_{c}
, the induced functor

\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathcal{C}/X}(C,\underline{\hphantom{A}})\colon\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{QCoh}(X)

is
\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
-linear and preserves all small colimits; For any
D\in\mathcal{C}
, the induced functor

\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathcal{C}/X}(\underline{\hphantom{A}},D)\colon\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\operatorname{QCoh}(X)

is
\mathrm{Perf}(X)
-linear and preserves all finite colimits (hence is an exact functor). Moreover, for any triple of objects
\mathscr{F}\in\mathrm{Perf}(X)
,
C\in\mathcal{C}_{c}
and
D\in\mathcal{C}
, there is a canonical equivalence

\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathcal{C}/X}(\mathscr{F}\otimes C,D)\simeq\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathcal{C}/X}(C,\mathscr{F}^{\vee}\otimes D)

for which the application of the functor
\mathrm{Map}_{\operatorname{QCoh}(X)}(\mathscr{O}_{X},\underline{\hphantom{A}})
induces a homotopy equivalence

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathscr{F}\otimes C,D)\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathcal{C}}(C,\mathscr{F}^{\vee}\otimes D)

which exhibits
\mathscr{F}^{\vee}\otimes D
as an exponential object of
D
by
\mathscr{F}
(see
[
HA
, Lemma 4.6.1.5]
).

Example 6.7
.

Let
f\colon Y\to X
be a map between perfect stacks which is a relative quasi-compact, quasi-separated derived scheme. Then the pullback functor
f^{*}\colon\operatorname{QCoh}(X)\to\operatorname{QCoh}(Y)
is symmetric monoidal and exhibits
\operatorname{QCoh}(Y)
as an
X
-linear stable
\infty
-category. We let
f_{*}\colon\operatorname{QCoh}(Y)\to\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
denote the right adjoint of
f^{*}
. For any perfect object
\mathscr{F}\in\mathrm{Perf}(Y)
, we let
\mathscr{F}^{\vee}
denote the dual of
\mathscr{F}
in
\operatorname{QCoh}(Y)
. Then, for all triple of objects
\mathscr{F}\in\mathrm{Perf}(Y)
,
\mathscr{G}\in\operatorname{QCoh}(Y)
and
\mathscr{E}\in\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
, the composite map

f^{*}f_{*}(\mathscr{F}^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{Y}}\mathscr{G})\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{Y}}\mathscr{F}\to\mathscr{F}^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{Y}}\mathscr{G}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{Y}}\mathscr{F}\simeq\mathscr{F}^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{Y}}\mathscr{F}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{Y}}\mathscr{G}\to\mathscr{G}

together with the canonical homotopy equivalences

\mathrm{Map}_{\operatorname{QCoh}(X)}(\mathscr{E},f_{*}(\mathscr{F}^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{Y}}\mathscr{G}))\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\operatorname{QCoh}(Y)}(f^{*}\mathscr{E},\mathscr{F}^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{Y}}\mathscr{G})\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\operatorname{QCoh}(Y)}(f^{*}\mathscr{E}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{Y}}\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})

exhibit
f_{*}(\mathscr{F}^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{Y}}\mathscr{G})
as a mapping object
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\operatorname{QCoh}(Y)/X}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})\in\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
.

Definition 6.8

(Relative exceptional objects)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
X
-linear stable
\infty
-category which is compactly generated. Let
E
be a compact object of
\mathcal{C}
, and let
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathcal{C}/X}(E,E)
be the mapping object of
E
with itself (Construction
6.6
), and let
\mathscr{O}_{X}\to\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathcal{C}/X}(E,E)
be the canonical map in
\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
which classifies the identity map
\operatorname{id}_{E}\colon E\to E
. We say an object
E\in\mathcal{C}
is a
relative exceptional object (over
X
)
if
E
is compact and the canonical map
\mathscr{O}_{X}\to\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathcal{C}/X}(E,E)
is an equivalence. We say a sequence of objects
(E_{1},E_{2},\ldots,E_{n})
in
\mathcal{C}
is a
relative exceptional sequence (of length
n
over
X
)
if each
E_{i}\in\mathcal{C}
is a relative exceptional object, and
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathcal{C}/X}(E_{i},E_{j})\simeq 0
for all
i>j
. We will also refer to a relative exceptional sequence
(E_{1},E_{2})
of
\mathcal{C}
of length
2
as a
relative exceptional pair (over
X
)
.

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a
X
-linear stable
\infty
-category which is compactly generated, and let
E\in\mathcal{C}
be a relative exceptional object over
X
. Then the functor
\alpha_{E}\colon(\mathscr{F}\in\operatorname{QCoh}(X))\mapsto(\mathscr{F}\otimes E\in\mathcal{C})
is fully faithful and induces fully faithful embeddings of
\infty
-categories

\alpha_{E}|{\mathrm{Perf}(X)}\colon\mathrm{Perf}(X)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{c}\qquad\alpha_{E}\colon\operatorname{QCoh}(X)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}.

Moreover,
\alpha_{E}
admits a right adjoint given by the functor
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathcal{C}/X}(E,\underline{\hphantom{A}})\colon\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
.

Proof.

For any pair of perfect objects
\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G}\in\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
, by applying the functor
\mathrm{Map}_{\mathcal{C}/X}(\mathscr{O}_{X},\underline{\hphantom{A}})
to the canonical equivalences

\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathcal{C}/X}(\mathscr{F}\otimes E,\mathscr{G}\otimes E)\simeq\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\operatorname{QCoh}(X)}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{X}}\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathcal{C}/X}(E,E)\simeq\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\operatorname{QCoh}(X)}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G}),

we see that the exact functor
\alpha_{E}|\mathrm{Perf}(X)
is fully faithful. Moreover, by virtue of the equivalence
\mathrm{Map}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathscr{F}\otimes E,\underline{\hphantom{A}})\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathcal{C}}(E,\mathscr{F}^{\vee}\otimes\underline{\hphantom{A}})
, we see that
\alpha_{E}
preserves compact objects. Therefore, by virtue of
[
HTT
, Proposition 5.3.5.11 (1)]
, we deduce that
\alpha_{E}\colon\operatorname{QCoh}(X)\to\mathcal{C}
is fully faithful. Furthermore, for any pair of objects
C\in\mathcal{C}
and
\mathscr{F}\in\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
, the evaluation map
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathcal{C}/X}(E,C)\otimes E\to C
together with the induced homotopy equivalence

\mathrm{Map}_{\operatorname{QCoh}(X)}(\mathscr{F},\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathcal{C}/X}(E,C))\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathscr{F}\otimes E,C)

exhibits
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathcal{C}/X}(E,\underline{\hphantom{A}})
as a right adjoint functor of
\alpha_{E}
.
∎

Definition 6.10

(Proper linear
\infty
-categories)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
X
-linear stable
\infty
-category (where
X
is a fixed perfect stack). We say that
\mathcal{C}
is
proper over
X
(or
\mathcal{C}
is a
proper
X
-linear
\infty
-category
), if
\mathcal{C}
is compactly generated and, for each pair of compact objects
C,D\in\mathcal{C}
, the mapping object
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathcal{C}/X}(C,D)
(Construction
6.6
) is a perfect object of
\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
.

By virtue of
[
SAG
, Proposition 11.1.3.3]
, (by applying the global section functor) the above definition is equivalent to Lurie’s definition of a proper quasi-coherent stack in the case where
X
is a quasi-compact, quasi-separated derived algebraic space.

Example 6.11
.

In the situation of Example
6.7
, if we furthermore assume that
f
is proper, locally almost of ﬁnite presentation and locally of finite Tor-amplitude. Then
f_{*}
preserves perfect objects (see
[
SAG
, Theorem 6.1.3.2]
). In particular, we deduce that
\operatorname{QCoh}(Y)
is a
proper

X
-linear stable
\infty
-category. Furthermore, by virtue of Theorem
3.7
(
22a
)(
22c
)(
22g
),

f_{*}
admits a right adjoint
f^{!}
which satisfies
f^{!}(\underline{\hphantom{A}})\simeq f^{*}(\underline{\hphantom{A}})\otimes\omega_{f}
, where
\omega_{f}=f^{!}(\mathscr{O}_{X})\in\operatorname{QCoh}(Y)
is the relative dualizing sheaf, and the relative Serre–Grothendieck duality implies that for all perfect objects
\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G}\in\mathrm{Perf}(Y)
, there is a canonical equivalence

\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\operatorname{QCoh}(Y)/X}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G}\otimes\omega_{f})\simeq\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\operatorname{QCoh}(Y)/X}(\mathscr{G},\mathscr{F})^{\vee}

of quasi-coherent sheaves on
X
(see, for example,
[
SAG
, Remark 11.1.5.4]
).

Definition 6.12

(Mutation functors)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a
X
-linear stable
\infty
-category which is compactly generated, and let
E\in\mathcal{C}
be a relative exceptional object over
X
. The
left mutation functor (passing through
E
)
, denoted by
\mathbb{L}_{E}\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
, is the colimit-preserving functor which carries each object
A\in\mathcal{C}
to the cofiber of the evaluation map
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathcal{C}/E}(E,A)\otimes E\to A
. In other words, for each
A\in\mathcal{C}
, there is a canonical fiber sequence

(6.1)

\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathcal{C}/X}(E,A)\otimes E\to A\to\mathbb{L}_{E}(A).

If we furthermore assume that
\mathcal{C}
is proper over
X
, then
\mathbb{L}_{E}
restricts to an exact functor
\mathcal{C}_{c}\to\mathcal{C}_{c}
, which we shall also denote by
\mathbb{L}_{E}
. Moreover, for each compact object
A\in\mathcal{C}
, the evaluation map
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathcal{C}/X}(A,E)\otimes A\to E
classifies a coevaluation map
A\to\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathcal{C}/X}(A,E)^{\vee}\otimes E
. The
right mutation functor (passing through
E
)
, denoted by
\mathbb{R}_{E}\colon\mathcal{C}_{c}\to\mathcal{C}_{c}
, is the exact functor which carries each compact object
A\in\mathcal{C}
to the fiber of the coevaluation map
A\to\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathcal{C}/X}(A,E)^{\vee}\otimes E
. In other words, there is a canonical fiber sequence

(6.2)

\mathbb{R}_{E}(A)\to A\to\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathcal{C}/X}(A,E)^{\vee}\otimes E.

The following is a list of fundamental properties about mutation functors (compare with the classical results in
[
Bo
,
BK
,
Go
]
and our exposition in
[
J21
, §3.11]
):

Lemma 6.13
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a proper
X
-linear stable
\infty
-category, let
E\in\mathcal{C}
be a relative exceptional object over
X
, and let
A
be any compact object of
\mathcal{C}
. We let
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}=\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathcal{C}/X}
denote the mapping object functor of Construction
6.6
. Then:

(1)

There are canonical equivalences

\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(E,\mathbb{L}_{E}(A))\simeq 0\qquad\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\mathbb{R}_{E}(A),E)\simeq 0.

(2)

If
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(A,E)\simeq 0
, then there is a canonical equivalence

\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\mathbb{L}_{E}(A),E)\simeq\Sigma^{-1}\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(E,A)^{\vee}.

Furthermore, for any compact object
B\in\mathcal{C}
, there are bi-functorial equivalences

\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(A,B)\xrightarrow{\sim}\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(A,\mathbb{L}_{E}(B))\xleftarrow{\sim}\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\mathbb{L}_{E}(A),\mathbb{L}_{E}(B)).

(3)

If
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(E,A)\simeq 0
, then there is a canonical equivalence

\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(E,\mathbb{R}_{E}(A))\simeq\Sigma^{-1}\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(A,E)^{\vee}.

Furthermore, for any compact object
B\in\mathcal{C}
, there are bi-functorial equivalences

\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(B,A)\xrightarrow{\sim}\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\mathbb{R}_{E}(B),A)\xleftarrow{\sim}\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\mathbb{R}_{E}(B),\mathbb{R}_{E}(A)).

(4)

If
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(A,E)\simeq 0
, then there is a canonical equivalence
\mathbb{R}_{E}\circ\mathbb{L}_{E}(A)\simeq A
. Similarly, if
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(E,A)\simeq 0
, then there is a canonical equivalence
\mathbb{L}_{E}\circ\mathbb{R}_{E}(A)\simeq A
.

Proof.

The first equivalence of (
1
) follows from applying the exact functor
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(E,\underline{\hphantom{A}})
to the fiber sequence (
6.1
), and the second equivalence of (
1
) follows from applying the exact functor
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\underline{\hphantom{A}},E)
to the fiber sequence (
6.2
). We now prove (
2
); the proof of (
3
) is similar. Since
\mathcal{C}
is a stable
\infty
-category, (
6.1
) induces a canonical fiber sequence

(6.3)

A\to\mathbb{L}_{E}(A)\to\Sigma\,\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(E,A)\otimes E

(which is determined up to a contractible space of choices) for which the application of the exact functor
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\underline{\hphantom{A}},E)
induces an equivalence
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\mathbb{L}_{E}(A),E)\simeq\Sigma^{-1}\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(E,A)^{\vee}
. Applying the exact functor
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(A,\underline{\hphantom{A}})
to the fiber sequence

\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(E,B)\otimes E\to B\to\mathbb{L}_{E}(B),

we obtain a canonical equivalence
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(A,B)\simeq\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(A,\mathbb{L}_{E}(B))
; By applying the exact functor
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\underline{\hphantom{A}},\mathbb{L}_{E}(B))
to (
6.1
) and using the equivalence
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(E,\mathbb{L}_{E}(B))\simeq 0
of (
1
), we obtain a canonical equivalence
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(A,\mathbb{L}_{E}(B))\simeq\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\mathbb{L}_{E}(A),\mathbb{L}_{E}(B))
.

Finally, we prove (
4
). We will prove in the case where
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(A,E)\simeq 0
; The other case is similar. From the way how we obtain the equivalence
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\mathbb{L}_{E}(A),E)\simeq\Sigma^{-1}\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(E,A)^{\vee}
of (
2
), we see that the coevaluation map
\mathbb{L}_{E}(A)\to\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\mathbb{L}_{E}(A),E)^{\vee}\otimes E
is equivalent to the morphism
\mathbb{L}_{E}(A)\to\Sigma\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(E,A)\otimes E
in the fiber sequence (
6.3
). Therefore, the fiber sequence (
6.3
) exhibits
A
as the right mutation of
\mathbb{L}_{E}(A)
passing through
E
.
∎

Remark 6.14
.

In the situation of Lemma
6.13
, if we let
{}^{\perp}\langle E\rangle\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{c}
(resp.,
\langle E\rangle^{\perp}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{c}
) denote the full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}_{c}
spanned by those objects
C
for which
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(C,E)\simeq 0
(resp.,
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(E,C)\simeq 0
). Then
{}^{\perp}\langle E\rangle
and
\langle E\rangle^{\perp}
are
\mathrm{Perf}(X)
-linear stable subcategories of
\mathcal{C}_{c}
. Therefore Lemma
6.13
implies that the restrictions of the mutation functors
\mathbb{L}_{E}
and
\mathbb{R}_{E}
induces mutually inverse
\mathrm{Perf}(X)
-linear equivalences of stable
\infty
-categories

\mathbb{L}_{E}\,|_{{}^{\perp}\langle E\rangle}\colon{}^{\perp}\langle E\rangle\xrightarrow{\sim}\langle E\rangle^{\perp}\qquad\mathbb{R}_{E}\,|_{\langle E\rangle^{\perp}}\colon\langle E\rangle^{\perp}\xrightarrow{\simeq}{}^{\perp}\langle E\rangle.

Lemma 6.15
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a proper
X
-linear stable
\infty
-category, let
(E,F)
be a relative exceptional pair of objects of
\mathcal{C}
over
X
. We let
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}=\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathcal{C}/X}
denote the mapping object functor of Construction
6.6
. Then:

(1)

Both
(\mathbb{L}_{E}(F),E)
and
(F,\mathbb{R}_{F}(E))
are relative exceptional pairs over
X
.

(2)

There are canonical equivalences

\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\mathbb{L}_{E}(F),E)\simeq\Sigma^{-1}\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(E,F)^{\vee}\simeq\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(F,\mathbb{R}_{F}(E)).

(3)

There are canonical equivalences

\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(F,\mathbb{L}_{E}(F))\simeq\mathscr{O}_{X}\qquad\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\mathbb{R}_{F}(E),E)\simeq\mathscr{O}_{X}.

(4)

There are canonical natural isomorphisms of functors from
\mathcal{C}_{c}
to itself:

\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{L}_{E}(F)}\circ\mathbb{L}_{E}\simeq\mathbb{L}_{E}\circ\mathbb{L}_{F}\qquad\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{R}_{F}(E)}\circ\mathbb{R}_{F}\simeq\mathbb{R}_{F}\circ\mathbb{R}_{E}.

Proof.

We first prove assertion (
1
). It follows from the canonical equivalence

\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\mathbb{L}_{E}(F),\mathbb{L}_{E}(F))\simeq\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(F,F)\simeq\mathscr{O}_{X}

of Lemma
6.13
(
2
) that
\mathbb{L}_{E}(F)
is a relative exceptional object; similarly, Lemma
6.13
(
3
) implies that
\mathbb{R}_{F}(E)
is a relative exceptional object. Moreover, Lemma
6.13
(
1
) implies
(\mathbb{L}_{E}(F),E)
and
(F,\mathbb{R}_{F}(E))
are relative exceptional pairs.
The assertion (
2
) follows from Lemma
6.13
(
2
) and (
3
).
To prove assertion (
3
), if we apply the second equation of Lemma
6.13
(
2
) to the case
A=B=F
, then we obtain canonical equivalences

\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(F,\mathbb{L}_{E}(F))\xleftarrow{\sim}\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(F,F)\xleftarrow{\sim}\mathscr{O}_{X};

Similarly, by applying Lemma
6.13
(
3
),
we obtain canonical equivalences

\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\mathbb{R}_{F}(E),E)\xleftarrow{\sim}\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(E,E)\xleftarrow{\sim}\mathscr{O}_{X}.

We now prove the equivalence
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{L}_{E}(F)}\circ\mathbb{L}_{E}\simeq\mathbb{L}_{E}\circ\mathbb{L}_{F}
of assertion (
4
); the other case is similar. For any
A\in\mathcal{C}_{c}
, consider the following commutative diagram of evaluation maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(F,A)\otimes\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(E,F)\otimes E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(F,A)\otimes F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(E,A)\otimes E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A.}

By virtue of
(TR2)
of the stable
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}_{c}
(see
[
HA
, Theorem 1.1.2.14]
), the above commutative diagram extends to a commutative diagram of fiber sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(F,A)\otimes\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(E,F)\otimes E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(F,A)\otimes F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(F,A)\otimes\mathbb{L}_{E}(F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(E,A)\otimes E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{E}(A)}

By virtue of the canonical equivalence

\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(E,\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(F,A)\otimes F)\simeq\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(F,A)\otimes\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(E,F),
