we can identify the first vertical arrow of the above diagram with the composition of the functor
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(E,\underline{\hphantom{A}})
and the evaluation map
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(F,A)\otimes F\to A
. By virtue of the equivalence
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\mathbb{L}_{E}(F),\mathbb{L}_{E}(A))\simeq\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(F,A)
of Lemma
6.13
(
2
), we can identify the last vertical arrow of the above diagram with the evaluation map

\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\mathbb{L}_{E}(F),\mathbb{L}_{E}(A))\otimes\mathbb{L}_{E}(F)\to\mathbb{L}_{E}(A).

By virtue of
(TR4)
of the stable
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}_{c}
(see
[
HA
, Theorem 1.1.2.14]
), we obtain that the above commutative diagram induces a fiber sequence

\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(E,\mathbb{L}_{F}(A))\otimes E\to\mathbb{L}_{F}(A)\to\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{L}_{E}(F)}(\mathbb{L}_{E}(A))

which exhibits
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{L}_{E}(F)}(\mathbb{L}_{E}(A))
as the left mutation of
\mathbb{L}_{F}(A)
passing through
E
. ∎

Definition 6.16
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a proper
X
-linear stable
\infty
-category, and let
E_{\bullet}=(E_{1},E_{2},\ldots,E_{n})
be a relative exceptional sequence of
\mathcal{C}
over
X
. We define the
left dual (relative exceptional) sequence
D_{\bullet}
(over
X
)
of
E_{\bullet}
by the following iterated left mutation process:

D_{1}:=E_{1},\qquad D_{i}:=\mathbb{L}_{E_{1}}\circ\ldots\circ\mathbb{L}_{E_{i-1}}(E_{i})\quad\text{for $2\leq i\leq n$}.

Similarly, we define the
right dual (relative exceptional) sequence
D_{\bullet}
(over
X
)
of
E_{\bullet}
by the following iterated right mutation process:

F_{n}:=E_{n},\qquad F_{i}:=\mathbb{R}_{E_{n}}\circ\ldots\circ\mathbb{R}_{E_{i+1}}(E_{i})\quad\text{for $1\leq i\leq n-1$}.

Corollary 6.17
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a proper
X
-linear stable
\infty
-category, and let
E_{\bullet}=(E_{1},E_{2},\ldots,E_{n})
be a relative exceptional sequence of
\mathcal{C}
over
X
. Then both the left dual sequence
D_{\bullet}=(D_{n},\ldots,D_{1})
and the right dual sequence
F_{\bullet}=(F_{1},\ldots,F_{n})
of
E_{\bullet}
are relative exceptional sequences over
X
, for which there are canonical equivalences (“relations of dual basis”):

\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathcal{C}/X}(E_{i},D_{j})\simeq\begin{cases}\mathscr{O}_{X}&\text{if $i=j$}.\\
0&\text{ if $i\neq j$}.\end{cases}\qquad\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathcal{C}/X}(F_{i},E_{j})\simeq\begin{cases}\mathscr{O}_{X}&\text{if $i=j$}.\\
0&\text{if $i=j$}.\end{cases}

Proof.

This follows from an iterated application of Lemmas
6.13
and
6.15
.
∎

Remark 6.18
.

In the situation of Corollary
6.17
, we let
\langle\mathrm{Perf}(X)\otimes E_{\bullet}\rangle
denote the smallest stable subcategory of
\mathcal{C}_{c}
which contains all elements of the form
\mathscr{F}\otimes E_{i}
, where
\mathscr{F}\in\mathrm{Perf}(X)
and
1\leq i\leq n
, and let
\langle\mathrm{Perf}(X)\otimes D_{\bullet}\rangle
and
\langle\mathrm{Perf}(X)\otimes F_{\bullet}\rangle
be defined similarly. Then there are canonical identifications of subcategories

\langle\mathrm{Perf}(X)\otimes E_{\bullet}\rangle=\langle\mathrm{Perf}(X)\otimes D_{\bullet}\rangle=\langle\mathrm{Perf}(X)\otimes F_{\bullet}\rangle\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{c}.

This follows from an iterated application of the fiber sequences (
6.1
) and (
6.2
).

6.2.
Beilinson’s relations

In this subsection, we present the derived version of Beilinson’s relations for projectivizations of sheaves of perfect-amplitude contained in
[0,1]
.

Proposition 6.19

(Dual exceptional sequences)
.

Let
X
be a perfect stack in the sense of Definition
6.3
, and let
\mathscr{F}
be a quasi-coherent sheaf on
X
of perfect-amplitude contained in
[0,1]
and rank
\delta\geq 1
. We let
\mathrm{pr}\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})\to X
denote the derived projectivization of
\mathscr{F}
and let
\mathscr{O}(1)
denote the universal line bundle. Then the sequence of line bundles on
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
:

\mathscr{O},\mathscr{O}(1),\cdots,\mathscr{O}(\delta-1),

forms a relative exceptional sequence on
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
over
X
(in the sense of Definition
6.8
), whose left dual relative exceptional sequence (Definition
6.16
) is given by

\operatorname{Sym}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}^{\delta-1}(\Sigma\,L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})/X}(1)),\cdots,\operatorname{Sym}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}^{i}(\Sigma\,L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})/X}(1)),\cdots,\Sigma\,L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})/X}(1),\mathscr{O},

where
L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})/X}
is the relative cotangent complex and
L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})/X}(1)=L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})/X}\otimes\mathscr{O}(1)
.

Remark 6.20
.

From the canonical equivalences

\operatorname{Sym}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}^{i}(\Sigma\,L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})/X}(1))\simeq\Sigma^{i}\bigwedge\nolimits_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}^{i}(L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})/X}(1))

for all
i\geq 0
, we obtain that the following sequence

\bigwedge\nolimits_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}^{\delta-1}(L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})/X}(1)),\cdots,\bigwedge\nolimits_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}^{i}(L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})/X}(1)),\cdots,L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})/X}(1),\mathscr{O}

also forms a relative exceptional sequence on
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
over
X
.

Proof.

It follows directly from Theorem
5.6
(
1i
) and (
1ii
) that the sequence
\mathscr{O},\mathscr{O}(1),\cdots,\mathscr{O}(\delta-1)
forms a relative exceptional sequence of
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
over
X
; It only remains to compute its left dual relative exceptional sequence. Let
\rho
denote the tautological quotient
\mathrm{pr}^{*}\mathscr{F}\to\mathscr{O}(1)
. Then by virtue of Theorem
4.27
, there is a canonical equivalence
\operatorname{cofib}(\rho)\simeq\Sigma\,L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})/X}(1)
. If we apply Construction
3.18
to the map
\rho
, then we obtain quasi-coherent sheaves
\mathscr{G}_{i,n}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}),\rho)
for all integers
n\geq 0
and
0\leq i\leq n
which fit into canonical fiber sequences

\beta_{i,n}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}),\rho)\colon\qquad\Sigma^{i}\,\bigwedge\nolimits^{i+1}(\mathrm{pr}^{*}\mathscr{F})\otimes\mathscr{O}(n-i-1)\to\mathscr{G}_{i,n}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}),\rho)\to\mathscr{G}_{i+1,n}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}),\rho),

and for which there are canonical equivalences:

\mathscr{G}_{0,n}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}),\rho)\simeq\mathscr{O}(n),\qquad\mathscr{G}_{n,n}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}),\rho)\simeq\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(\Sigma\,L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})/X}(1)).

Moreover, for any integer
n^{\prime}\geq 0
, since the functor
\otimes\mathscr{O}(n^{\prime})
is an exact equivalence, the canonical morphisms from Construction
3.18
,

(6.4)

\displaystyle\beta_{i,n}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}),\rho)\otimes\mathscr{O}(n^{\prime})\to\beta_{i,n+n^{\prime}}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}),\rho),

are equivalences of fiber sequences. Now for each
n\geq 1
, the canonical natural transformation
\operatorname{id}_{\mathrm{pr}^{*}\mathscr{F}}\to\rho
, which can be depicted as a commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}^{*}\mathscr{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}^{*}\mathscr{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}^{*}\mathscr{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{O}(1),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

induces canonical morphisms of fiber sequences

\beta_{i,n}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}),\mathrm{pr}^{*}\operatorname{id}_{\mathscr{F}})\to\beta_{i,n}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}),\rho).

By virtue of Theorem
5.6
(
1i
) and the functoriality of the fiber sequences
\beta_{i,n}
, by applying the exact functor
\mathrm{pr}_{*}
we obtain canonical equivalences of fiber sequences:

(6.5)

\displaystyle\beta_{i,n}(X,\mathscr{F}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{id}_{\mathscr{F}}}\mathscr{F})\simeq\mathrm{pr}_{*}\left(\beta_{i,n}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}),\mathrm{pr}^{*}\xrightarrow{\rho}\mathscr{O}(1))\right).

To finish the proof of the proposition, we only need to show the following assertion:

(*)

For for every pair of integers
n\geq 1
and
0\leq i\leq n-1
, the above fiber sequence
\beta_{i,n}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}),\rho)
is canonically equivalent to the mutation fiber sequence (
6.1
) for the left mutation of
\mathscr{G}_{i,n}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}_{\bullet}),\rho)
passing through
\mathscr{O}(n-i-1)
.

We prove
(*)
by induction on
n
. Assume
n=1
,
i=0
, then by construction the fiber sequence
\beta_{0,1}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}_{\bullet}),\rho)
is canonically equivalent with the shifted Euler fiber sequence

\mathrm{pr}^{*}\mathscr{F}\xrightarrow{\rho}\mathscr{O}(1)\to\Sigma\,L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})/X}(1).

By virtue of Theorem
5.6
(
1i
), there is a canonical equivalence of fiber sequences

\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\beta_{0,1}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}_{\bullet}),\rho))\simeq(\mathscr{F}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{id}}\mathscr{F}\to 0)

which canonically identifies
\beta_{0,1}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}_{\bullet}),\rho)
with the fiber sequence (
6.1
) for the left mutation of
\mathscr{O}(1)
through
\mathscr{O}
.

Assume that the assertion
(*)
holds for an integer
n\geq 1
and all integers
0\leq i\leq n-1
. We wish to show that the assertion
(*)
holds for
n+1
and all integers
0\leq i\leq n
. From the equivalences (
6.4
) for the case
n^{\prime}=1
, we deduce from induction hypothesis that the assertion
(*)
holds for all
0\leq i\leq n-1
; In particular, there are canonical equivalences

\mathscr{G}_{1,n+1}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}),\rho)\simeq\mathscr{G}_{0,n}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}),\rho)\otimes\mathscr{O}(1)\simeq\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(\Sigma\,L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})/X}(1))\otimes\mathscr{O}(1).

It remains to prove the case where
i=n
, that is, to show that the fiber sequence

\beta_{n,n+1}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}),\rho)\colon\quad\Sigma^{n}\bigwedge\nolimits^{n+1}(\mathrm{pr}^{*}\mathscr{F})\to\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(\Sigma\,L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})/X}(1))\otimes\mathscr{O}(1)\to\operatorname{Sym}^{n+1}(\Sigma\,L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})/X}(1))

can be canonically identified with the mutation fiber sequence (
6.1
) for the left mutation of
\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(\Sigma\,L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})/X}(1))\otimes\mathscr{O}(1)
passing through
\mathscr{O}
. Invoking the equivalence (
6.5
), we obtain that the fiber sequence
\mathrm{pr}_{*}(\beta_{n,n+1}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}),\rho))
is canonically equivalent to

\beta_{n,n+1}(X,\operatorname{id}_{\mathscr{F}})\colon\qquad\Sigma^{n}\bigwedge\nolimits^{n+1}(\mathscr{F})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathscr{G}_{n,n+1}(X,\operatorname{id}_{\mathscr{F}})\to 0.

From the adjunction between
\mathrm{pr}^{*}
and
\mathrm{pr}_{*}
, we obtain from the above equivalence that the fiber sequence
\beta_{n,n+1}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}),\rho)
exhibits
\operatorname{Sym}^{n+1}(\Sigma\,L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})/X}(1))
as the left mutation of
\mathscr{G}_{1,n+1}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}),\rho)
through
\mathscr{O}
. By induction, the assertion
(*)
holds for all
n\geq 1
,
0\leq i\leq n-1
.
∎

Corollary 6.21

(Beilinson’s relations)
.

Assume we are in the same situation as Proposition
6.19
, then for all integers
0\leq i,j\leq\delta-1
, there are canonical equivalences:

(6.6)

\left\{\begin{split}&\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}\big(\mathscr{O}(i),\mathscr{O}(j)\big)\simeq\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{j-i}(\mathscr{F}),\\
&\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}\big(\mathscr{O}(i),\Sigma^{j}\bigwedge\nolimits_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}^{j}(L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})/X}(1))\big)\simeq\delta_{i,j}\cdot\mathscr{O}_{X},\\
&\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}\big(\bigwedge\nolimits_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}^{i}(L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})/X}(1)),\bigwedge\nolimits_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}^{j}(L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})/X}(1))\big)\simeq(\bigwedge\nolimits_{X}^{i-j}\mathscr{F})^{\vee}.\end{split}\right.

Here,
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}=\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})/X}
denotes the mapping object functor on
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
over
X
of Construction
6.6
,
(\underline{\hphantom{A}})^{\vee}
denotes the dual of a perfect object in
\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
, the quasi-coherent sheaves
\operatorname{Sym}^{d}(\mathscr{E})
and
\bigwedge^{d}(\mathscr{E})
are understood as zero whenever
d<0
, and
\delta_{i,j}
denotes the Kronecker delta function, that is,
\delta_{i,j}=1
if
i=j
and
\delta_{i,j}=0
if
i\neq j
.

Proof.

For simplicity of notations, in this proof, we set

\mathscr{R}=L_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})/X}\otimes\mathscr{O}(1)

and omit the subscript
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
in all the expressions of the form
\operatorname{Sym}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}^{i}(\mathscr{E})
.

The first relation of (
6.6
) follows from Theorem
5.6
(
1i
). The second relation of (
6.6
) is a consequence of Proposition
6.19
and Corollary
6.17
. To prove the third relation of (
6.6
), we only need to show that, for all
0\leq i,j\leq\delta-1
, there are canonical equivalences

(6.7)

\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\operatorname{Sym}^{i}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{R}),\operatorname{Sym}^{j}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{R}))\simeq(\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{i-j}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{F}))^{\vee}

We prove (
6.7
) by induction on
i
. The base case
i=0
follows from Proposition
6.19
and Corollary
6.17
. Now assume that (
6.7
) holds for the case
(i-1)
, where
i\geq 1
(thus we may assume
\delta\geq 2
). We wish to prove (
6.7
) for the case
i
. By virtue of Remark
6.18
and Proposition
6.19
,
\operatorname{Sym}^{i-1}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{R})
belongs to the full subcategory

\big\langle\mathrm{pr}^{*}(\mathrm{Perf}(X))\otimes\mathscr{O},\cdots,\mathrm{pr}^{*}(\mathrm{Perf}(X))\otimes\mathscr{O}(i-1)\big\rangle.

From the equivalences
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\mathscr{O},\mathscr{O}(-1))\simeq\cdots\simeq\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\mathscr{O}(i-1),\mathscr{O}(-1))\simeq 0
, we obtain

\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\operatorname{Sym}^{i-1}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{R})\otimes\mathscr{O}(1),\mathscr{O})\simeq\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\operatorname{Sym}^{i-1}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{R}),\mathscr{O}(-1))\simeq 0.

The fiber sequence
\beta_{i-1,i}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}),\rho)
of the proof of Proposition
6.19
induces a fiber sequence

(6.8)

\operatorname{Sym}^{i-1}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{R})\otimes\mathscr{O}(1)\to\operatorname{Sym}^{i}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{R})\to\mathrm{pr}^{*}(\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{i}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{F})).

By applying the exact functor
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\underline{\hphantom{A}},\mathscr{O})
to (
6.8
), we obtain a canonical equivalence

\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\operatorname{Sym}^{i}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{R}),\mathscr{O})\simeq(\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{i}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{F}))^{\vee}.

This proves (
6.7
) in the case where
j=0
. Assume
j\geq 1
, then
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\mathscr{O},\operatorname{Sym}^{j}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{R}))\simeq 0
. By applying
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\underline{\hphantom{A}},\operatorname{Sym}^{j}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{R}))
to (
6.8
), we obtain a canonical equivalence

\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\operatorname{Sym}^{i}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{R}),\operatorname{Sym}^{j}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{R}))\simeq\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\operatorname{Sym}^{i-1}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{R})\otimes\mathscr{O}(1),\operatorname{Sym}^{j}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{R})).

By virtue of the vanishing

\displaystyle\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\operatorname{Sym}^{i-1}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{R})\otimes\mathscr{O}(1),\mathrm{pr}^{*}(\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{j}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{F})))

\displaystyle\simeq\,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{j}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{F})\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{X}}\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\operatorname{Sym}^{i-1}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{R})\otimes\mathscr{O}(1),\mathscr{O})\simeq 0,

if we apply the exact functor
\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\operatorname{Sym}^{i-1}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{R})\otimes\mathscr{O}(1),\underline{\hphantom{A}})
to the fiber sequence

\operatorname{Sym}^{j-1}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{R})\otimes\mathscr{O}(1)\to\operatorname{Sym}^{j}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{R})\to\mathrm{pr}^{*}(\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{j}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{F}))

(which is (
6.8
) but with
i
replaced by
j
), we obtain canonical equivalences

\displaystyle\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\operatorname{Sym}^{i-1}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{R})\otimes\mathscr{O}(1),\operatorname{Sym}^{j}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{R}))

\displaystyle\simeq\,

\displaystyle\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\operatorname{Sym}^{i-1}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{R})\otimes\mathscr{O}(1),\operatorname{Sym}^{j-1}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{R})\otimes\mathscr{O}(1))

\displaystyle\simeq\,

\displaystyle\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\operatorname{Sym}^{i-1}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{R}),\operatorname{Sym}^{j-1}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{R})).

By induction, we obtain that (
6.7
) holds for
i
and all
1\leq j\leq\delta-1
.
∎

7.
Semiorthogonal decompositions

For a prestack
Y
, we let
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(Y)
denote the homotopy category of the
\infty
-category
\operatorname{QCoh}(Y)
. Since
\operatorname{QCoh}(Y)
is a stable
\infty
-category,
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(Y)
is a triangulated category. The category
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(Y)
is often referred to as the derived category of quasi-coherent sheaves on
Y
.

Let
X
be a (classical) Cohen–Macaulay scheme, and let
\mathscr{F}
be a discrete finite type quasi-coherent sheaf on
X
of homological dimension
\leq 1
and rank
\delta\geq 1
.
Assume furthermore that condition (

b

) of §
7.3
is satisfied (so that
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})\simeq\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{cl}}(\mathscr{F})
), then it follows immediate from Proposition
6.19
of last section that, for any
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, the functor

\Psi_{i}=\mathrm{pr}^{*}(\underline{\hphantom{A}})\otimes\mathscr{O}(i)\colon\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(X)\to\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}))

is fully faithful, and for any given integer
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, the essential images of
\Psi_{i}
induced from the relative exceptional sequence
\mathscr{O}(k+1),\ldots,\mathscr{O}(k+\delta)
of
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
over
X
,

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(X)\otimes\mathscr{O}(k+1),\cdots,\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(X)\otimes\mathscr{O}(k+\delta),

form a semiorthogonal sequence which spans a full triangulated subcategory of
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}))
.

(1)

(Orlov’s theorem) If
\mathscr{F}
is locally free of rank
\delta\geq 1
, then a theorem of Orlov
[
O92
]
implies that the above semiorthogonal sequence spans the whole category
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}))
. In other words, there is a semiorthogonal decomposition

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}))=\big\langle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(X)\otimes\mathscr{O}(k+1),\cdots,\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(X)\otimes\mathscr{O}(k+\delta)\big\rangle.

(See also
[
BS
, Theorem 6.7]
,
[
Kh20
, Theorem 3.3]
,
[
J21
, Theorem B.3]
.)

(2)

(The projectivization formula) In general, if
\mathscr{F}
is not locally free, the above semiorthogonal sequence no longer spans the whole category
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}))
. In
[
JL18
]
, we show that the “correction” is precisely provided by the category of another projectivization
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{cl}}(\mathscr{E}\kern-1.0ptxt^{1}_{\mathscr{O}_{X}}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{O}_{X}))
, whose support is the non-locally-free locus of
\mathscr{F}
. More precisely, under the above condition (

b

) (so that
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})\simeq\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{cl}}(\mathscr{F})
and
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}\kern-1.0ptxt^{1}_{\mathscr{O}_{X}}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{O}_{X}))\simeq\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{cl}}(\mathscr{E}\kern-1.0ptxt^{1}_{\mathscr{O}_{X}}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{O}_{X}))
), there is a semiorthogonal decomposition

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}))=\big\langle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{E}\kern-1.0ptxt^{1}_{\mathscr{O}_{X}}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{O}_{X}))),~\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(X)\otimes\mathscr{O}(1),\cdots,\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{qc}}(X)\otimes\mathscr{O}(\delta)\big\rangle.

Remark 7.1
.

The projectivization formula in the above form can be found in
[
JL18
, Theorem 3.4]
,
[
J21
, Theorem 6.16]
; see also
[
Ku07
, Theorem 5.5]
and
[
Tod19
, Theorem 4.6.11]
.

In this section, we prove a generalization of the above projectivization formula (Theorem
7.5
), which completely describes the
\infty
-category
\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}))
for a quasi-coherent sheaf
\mathscr{F}
of perfect-amplitude contained in
[0,1]
and rank
\delta\geq 0
over a prestack
X
, in terms of the
\infty
-categories
\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
and
\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}(\Sigma\mathscr{F}^{\vee}))
, where
\Sigma\mathscr{F}^{\vee}
is the “shifted dual” of
\mathscr{F}
.

Theorem
7.5
has many new applications even in the classical situation, and we consider such examples in §
7.3
. For the purpose of applications, in §
7.3
, we first provide criteria for the derived projectivizations and derived symmetric products to be classical (Lemmata
7.16
and
7.18
). Then we apply the results in each situation; we only the list lower dimensional examples in each situation, but all these results have analogues in higher dimensions.

7.1.
The Fourier–Mukai functors and semiorthogonal decompositions

In this subsection, we present the main result of this section

Notation 7.2
.

Let
X
be a prestack, and let
\mathscr{F}
be a quasi-coherent sheaf of perfect-amplitude contained in
[0,1]
on
X
of rank
\delta\geq 0
. We consider the following derived projectivizations

\mathrm{pr}_{+}\colon\mathbb{P}_{+}=\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})\to X\qquad\mathrm{pr}_{-}\colon\mathbb{P}_{-}=\mathbb{P}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{F}^{\vee})\to X.

We let
\rho_{+}\colon\mathrm{pr}_{+}^{*}\mathscr{F}\to\mathscr{O}_{+}(1)
and
\rho_{-}\colon\mathrm{pr}_{-}^{*}(\Sigma\mathscr{F}^{\vee})\to\mathscr{O}_{-}(1)
denote the tautological quotient maps on
\mathbb{P}_{+}
and
\mathbb{P}_{-}
, respectively. We denote the (derived) fiber product of
\mathbb{P}_{\pm}
over
X
by

Z=\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})\times_{X}\mathbb{P}(\Sigma\mathscr{F}^{\vee})

and let
\mathrm{pr}_{Z,+}\colon Z\to\mathbb{P}_{+}
and
\mathrm{pr}_{Z,-}\colon Z\to\mathbb{P}_{-}
denote the respective projections. For a pair of quasi-coherent sheaves
\mathscr{M}_{+}\in\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}_{+})
and
\mathscr{M}_{-}\in\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}_{-})
, we let
\mathscr{M}_{+}\boxtimes_{X}\mathscr{M}_{-}
denote the external tensor product
\mathrm{pr}_{Z,+}^{*}\mathscr{M}_{+}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{Z}}\mathrm{pr}_{Z,-}^{*}\mathscr{M}_{-}\in\operatorname{QCoh}(Z)
.

Lemma 7.3
.

In the situation of Notation
7.2
, the following constructions of a pair
(\widehat{Z},\iota_{\widehat{Z}})
, where
\widehat{Z}
is a prestack and
\iota_{\widehat{Z}}\colon\widehat{Z}\to Z
is a quasi-smooth closed immersion, are equivalent:

(1)

We let
\widehat{Z}
denote the derived projectivization
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{P}_{+}}(\Sigma\,\operatorname{fib}(\rho_{+})^{\vee})
, and let
\iota_{\widehat{Z}}\colon\widehat{Z}\to Z
denote the closed immersion induced by the surjective map
\Sigma(\mathrm{pr}_{+}^{*}\mathscr{F})^{\vee}\to\Sigma\,\operatorname{fib}(\rho_{+})^{\vee}
on
\mathbb{P}_{+}
(Corollary
4.32
), where
Z
is canonically identified with
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{P}_{+}}(\Sigma(\mathrm{pr}_{+}^{*}\mathscr{F})^{\vee})
.

(2)

We let
\widehat{Z}
denote the derived projectivization
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{P}_{-}}(\Sigma\,\operatorname{fib}(\rho_{-})^{\vee})
, and let
\iota_{\widehat{Z}}\colon\widehat{Z}\to Z
denote the closed immersion induced by the surjective map
\mathrm{pr}_{-}^{*}\mathscr{F}\to\Sigma\,\operatorname{fib}(\rho_{-})^{\vee}
on
\mathbb{P}_{-}
(Corollary
4.32
), where
Z
is canonically identified with
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{P}_{-}}(\mathrm{pr}_{-}^{*}\mathscr{F})
.

(3)

We let
\widehat{Z}
together with the closed immersion
\iota_{\widehat{Z}}\colon\widehat{Z}\to Z
be defined by the following pullback square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{V}_{Z}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{O}_{+}(-1)\boxtimes_{X}\mathscr{O}_{-}(-1)),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\widehat{Z}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\widehat{Z}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\mathbf{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\mathbf{0}}}

where
i_{\mathbf{0}}
is the section of
\mathbb{V}_{Z}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{O}_{+}(-1)\boxtimes_{X}\mathscr{O}_{-}(-1))\to Z
that classifies the zero map, and
i_{\mathbf{1}}
is the section of
\mathbb{V}_{Z}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{O}_{+}(-1)\boxtimes_{X}\mathscr{O}_{-}(-1))\to Z
that classifies the composition

\Sigma\,\mathscr{O}_{+}(-1)\boxtimes_{X}\mathscr{O}_{-}(-1)\xrightarrow{\Sigma\,\rho_{+}^{\vee}\boxtimes\rho_{-}^{\vee}}\mathrm{pr}_{+}^{*}(\mathscr{F}^{\vee})\boxtimes_{X}\mathrm{pr}_{-}^{*}(\mathscr{F})\simeq(\mathrm{pr}_{Z}^{*}\mathscr{F})^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{Z}}(\mathrm{pr}_{Z}^{*}\mathscr{F})\xrightarrow{\rm ev}\mathscr{O}_{Z}

(here,
\mathrm{pr}_{Z}\colon Z\to X
is the natural projection, and
{\rm ev}
is the evaluation map).

Proof.

Notice that there are canonical fiber sequences

\Sigma\mathscr{O}_{+}(-1)\to\Sigma(\mathrm{pr}_{+}^{*}\mathscr{F})^{\vee}\to\Sigma\,\operatorname{fib}(\rho_{+})^{\vee}\quad\text{and}\quad\Sigma\mathscr{O}_{-}(-1)\to\mathrm{pr}_{-}^{*}\mathscr{F}\to\Sigma\,\operatorname{fib}(\rho_{-})^{\vee},

on
\mathbb{P}_{+}=\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
and
\mathbb{P}_{-}=\mathbb{P}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{F}^{\vee})
, respectively. Therefore, both the equivalences of the constructions “
(1)\iff(3)
” and “
(2)\iff(3)
” follow from Proposition
4.33
and the following general fact: let
\mathcal{C}
be a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category with unit
1\in\mathcal{C}
. Let
C,D\in\mathcal{C}
be dualizable objects with (right) duals
C^{\vee}
and
D^{\vee}
, and evaluation maps
e_{C}\colon C^{\vee}\otimes C\to 1
and
e_{D}\colon D^{\vee}\otimes D\to 1
, respectively. Let
f\colon C\to D
be a map in
\mathcal{C}
, and let
f^{\vee}\colon D^{\vee}\to C^{\vee}
be the dual map. Then the following two composite maps

\displaystyle D^{\vee}\otimes C\xrightarrow{f^{\vee}\otimes\operatorname{id}}C^{\vee}\otimes C\xrightarrow{e_{C}}1\quad\text{and}\quad D^{\vee}\otimes C\xrightarrow{\operatorname{id}\otimes f}D^{\vee}\otimes D\xrightarrow{e_{D}}1

are canonically homotopy equivalent to each other.
∎

Notation 7.4
.

In the situation of Notation
7.2
, we let
(\widehat{Z},\iota_{\widehat{Z}}\colon\widehat{Z}\hookrightarrow Z)
denote the closed sub-prestack of
Z
constructed in Lemma
7.3
, and refer to is as the
universal incidence locus
for
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
and
\mathbb{P}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{F}^{\vee})
over
X
. We let
r_{+}\colon\widehat{Z}\to\mathbb{P}_{+}=\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
and
r_{-}\colon\widehat{Z}\to\mathbb{P}_{-}=\mathbb{P}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{F}^{\vee})
denote the respective projections, and we will refer to the following commutative diagram

(7.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}_{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}_{-}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathrm{pr}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{-}}

as the
incidence diagram
(for the derived projectivizations
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
and
\mathbb{P}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{F}^{\vee})
over
X
).

The main result of this section is the following:

Theorem 7.5
.

Let
X
be a prestack and
\mathscr{F}
a quasi-coherent sheaf of perfect-amplitude contained in
[0,1]
on
X
of rank
\delta\geq 0
, and we let everything be defined as in Notations
7.2
and
7.4
. Then the exact functor induced by the incidence diagram (
7.1
),

\Phi=r_{+,*}\,r_{-}^{*}\colon\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{F}^{\vee}))\to\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})),

and its restriction to the subcategory of perfect objects,

\Phi|\mathrm{Perf}(\mathbb{P}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{F}^{\vee}))\colon\mathrm{Perf}(\mathbb{P}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{F}^{\vee}))\to\mathrm{Perf}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})),

are fully faithful. If
\delta=0
, then
\Phi
and
\Phi|\mathrm{Perf}(\mathbb{P}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{F}^{\vee}))
induce equivalences of
\infty
-categories

\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{F}^{\vee}))\simeq\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}))\qquad\mathrm{Perf}(\mathbb{P}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{F}^{\vee}))\simeq\mathrm{Perf}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})).

If
\delta\geq 1
, then for any
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, the functor

\Psi_{i}=\mathrm{pr}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}^{*}(\underline{\hphantom{A}})\otimes\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}(i)\colon\operatorname{QCoh}(X)\to\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}))

and its restriction to the subcategory of perfect object,

\Psi_{i}|\mathrm{Perf}(X)=\mathrm{pr}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}^{*}(\underline{\hphantom{A}})\otimes\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})}(i)\colon\mathrm{Perf}(X)\to\mathrm{Perf}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})),

are fully faithful. Furthermore, there are induced semiorthogonal decompositions

\displaystyle\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}))

\displaystyle=\big\langle\Phi(\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{F}^{\vee}))),~\Psi_{1}(\operatorname{QCoh}(X)),\ldots,\Psi_{\delta}(\operatorname{QCoh}(X))\big\rangle

\displaystyle\mathrm{Perf}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}))

\displaystyle=\big\langle\Phi(\mathrm{Perf}(\mathbb{P}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{F}^{\vee}))),~\Psi_{1}(\mathrm{Perf}(X)),\ldots,\Psi_{\delta}(\mathrm{Perf}(X))\big\rangle.

Remark 7.6
.

By virtue of Theorem
3.7
(
2
) and (
3
), the functors
\Phi
and
\Psi_{i}
of Theorem
7.5
admit both left and right adjoints, and they and their left and right adjoints preserve perfect objects, almost perfect objects, and locally bounded almost perfect objects. Consequently, there are also induced similar semiorthogonal decompositions for the subcategories of almost perfect objects, and locally bounded almost perfect objects.

Remark 7.7

(Additive invariants)
.

As a consequence of Theorem
7.5
, if
\mathbb{E}
is any additive invariant (in the sense of
[
BGT
, Definition 6.1]
, from the
\infty
-category of stable
\infty
-categories, with exact functors between them, to a stable presentable
\infty
-category
\mathcal{D}
), then there is a canonical equivalence

\mathbb{E}(\mathrm{Perf}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})))\simeq\mathbb{E}(\mathrm{Perf}(\mathbb{P}(\Sigma\mathscr{F}^{\vee})))\oplus\big(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{\delta}\nolimits\mathbb{E}(\mathrm{Perf}(X))\big).

For example, we could take
\mathbb{E}
to be algebraic
K-theory, topological Hochschild homology, or topological cyclic homology; see
[
BGT
]
for more details about additive invariants.

Remark 7.8

(Derived blowups)
.

In the situation of Theorem
7.5
, if
\operatorname{rank}\mathscr{F}=1
, then the derived projectivization
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
could be regarded as the
derived blowup
(in the sense of
[
KR18
,
He21
]
) of
X
along the derived first degeneracy locus of
\mathscr{F}
. This point of view is justified in the classical situation (
[
JL18
, §3.1.2]
,
[
J20
, Lemma 5.1]
, and
[
J21
, Lemma 2.25]
), and we expect similar results to be true in the derived context.

Proof of Theorem
7.5
.

We let
\Phi_{0}=\Phi
and
\Phi_{i}=\Psi_{i}
where
1\leq i\leq\delta
, let
\Phi_{i}^{L}
and
\Phi_{i}^{R}
denote the left and right adjoint functors of
\Phi_{i}
, respectively. Then to prove Theorem
7.5
, it suffices to prove the following assertions:

(1)

(Fully-faithfulness) The cofiber of the unit
\operatorname{id}\to\Phi_{i}^{R}\circ\Phi_{i}
is equivalent to zero for all
i
.

(2)

(Semiorthogonality) The composite map
\Phi_{i}^{R}\circ\Phi_{j}
is equivalent to zero for all
i>j
.

(3)

(Generation) For every object
\mathscr{E}\in\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}))
, the cofiber of the canonical map from
\mathscr{E}
to its left mutation passing through
\{\operatorname{Im}\Phi_{i}\}_{0\leq i\leq\delta}
is equivalent to zero.

The formations of the above assertions
(1)
through
(3)
, regarded as conditions on the pair
(X,\mathscr{F})
, commute with base change and are local on
X
with respect to Zariski topology; The analogous statement is also true for the subcategories of perfect objects. Therefore, to prove Theorem
7.5
, we might reduce to the local case where
X=\operatorname{Spec}R
,
R\in\operatorname{CAlg}^{\Delta}
, and
\mathscr{F}
is represented by the cofiber of a map of vector bundles
\sigma\colon\mathscr{W}\to\mathscr{V}
on
X
. The proof of Theorem
7.5
in the local situation is given in the next subsection.
∎

Remark 7.9
.

The above conditions
(1)
through
(3)
of the pair
(X,\mathscr{F})
are local on
X
even with respect to
flat
topology. Consequently, the formation of the semiorthogonal decomposition of Theorem
7.5
commutes with arbitrary base change and satisfies flat descent. See also
[
Ku11
]
and
[
AE
,
BS
]
for general results on base change and flat descent of semiorthogonal decompositions.

7.2.
The local situation

In this subsection, we present the proof of Theorem
7.5
in the local situation. Assume that we are in the situation of Notations
7.2
and
7.4
, and assume furthermore that
X=\operatorname{Spec}R
,
R\in\operatorname{CAlg}^{\Delta}
, and
\mathscr{F}
is represented by the cofiber of a map of vector bundles
\sigma\colon\mathscr{W}\to\mathscr{V}
on
X
, where
n=\operatorname{rank}\mathscr{V}
,
m=\operatorname{rank}\mathscr{W}
,
\delta=n-m\geq 0
. By virtue of Proposition
4.33
, there are closed immersions

\iota_{+}\colon\mathbb{P}_{+}=\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})\qquad\iota_{-}\colon\mathbb{P}_{-}=\mathbb{P}(\Sigma\mathscr{F}^{\vee})\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{W}^{\vee}).

By virtue of Corollary
4.32
, the projections
r_{\pm}\colon\widehat{Z}\to\mathbb{P}_{\pm}
of diagram (
7.1
) are quasi-smooth, and factorize as the respective compositions

(7.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}_{-}\times_{X}\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}_{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{-}}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}_{+}\times_{X}\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{W}^{\vee})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{+}}

Furthermore, by virtue of Proposition
4.33
, there are respective pullback squares:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}_{-}\times_{X}\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}_{-}\times_{X}\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{P}_{-}\times_{X}\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(\iota_{-}^{*}\mathscr{R}_{-}^{\vee}\boxtimes_{X}\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(-1)),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{-}}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}_{+}\times_{X}\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{W}^{\vee})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}_{+}\times_{X}\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{W}^{\vee})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{P}_{+}\times_{X}\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{W}^{\vee})}(\iota_{+}^{*}\mathscr{R}_{+}^{\vee}\boxtimes_{X}\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{W}^{\vee})}(-1)).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{+}}

Definition 7.10
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a stable
\infty
-category. We say a collection
\mathcal{E}
of objects of
\mathcal{C}

generates

\mathcal{C}
if it satisfies the following condition: let
C\in\mathcal{C}
be an object having the property that
\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{C}}^{n}(E,C)\simeq 0
for all
E\in\mathcal{E}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, then
C\simeq 0
. Consequently, let
\mathcal{C}_{0}
be a stable subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
for which the inclusion
i\colon\mathcal{C}_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}
admits a right adjoint. If
\mathcal{C}_{0}
contains a collection
\mathcal{E}
of objects which generates
\mathcal{C}
in the above sense, then
\mathcal{C}_{0}=\mathcal{C}
.

Lemma 7.11
.

In the above situation, we fix any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, and let
\mathscr{R}_{+}=\Omega_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})/X}(1)
and
\mathscr{R}_{-}=\Omega_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{W}^{\vee})/X}(1)
denote the twisted relative cotangent bundles of
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})
and
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{W}^{\vee})
, respectively.

(1)

\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}_{+})
is generated by any of the following collections of vector bundles:

\{\mathscr{O}_{+}(i)\}_{k\leq i\leq k+n-1},\qquad\{\iota_{+}^{*}(\bigwedge\nolimits^{i}\mathscr{R}_{+})\}_{0\leq i\leq n-1},\qquad\text{or}\qquad\{\iota_{+}^{*}(\bigwedge\nolimits^{i}\mathscr{R}_{+}^{\vee})\}_{0\leq i\leq n-1}.

(2)

\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}_{-})
is generated by any of the following collections of vector bundles:

\{\mathscr{O}_{-}(i)\}_{k\leq i\leq k+n-1},\qquad\{\iota_{-}^{*}(\bigwedge\nolimits^{i}\mathscr{R}_{-})\}_{0\leq i\leq m-1},\qquad\text{or}\qquad\{\iota_{-}^{*}(\bigwedge\nolimits^{i}\mathscr{R}_{-}^{\vee})\}_{0\leq i\leq m-1}.

Proof.

This follows from the fact that
\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
is generated by
\mathscr{O}_{X}
in the case where
X=\operatorname{Spec}R
(
[
HA
, Theorem 7.1.2.1]
), the Beilinson’s relative exceptional sequences of Proposition
6.19
are full in the case of projective bundles (which follows from the usual Orlov’s theorem; see also
[
J21
, Proposition B.3]
), and that the morphisms
\iota_{\pm}
are affine.
∎

The following is the analogue of the key lemma
[
J21
, Lemma 5.6]
in the case of projectivizations (Here, we only present half of all the cases, for these we will need in our proof).

Lemma 7.12
.

In the above situation (where
\delta=n-m\geq 0
):

(1)

Let
\Phi=r_{+\,*}\,r_{-}^{*}\colon\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}_{-})\to\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}_{+})
. Then for any
\mathscr{E}\in\mathrm{Perf}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{W}^{\vee}))
, there is a series of fiber sequences in
\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}_{+})
for all
0\leq i\leq n-1
:

\mathscr{G}_{i-1}(\mathscr{E})\to\mathscr{G}_{i}(\mathscr{E})\to\iota_{+}^{*}(\bigwedge\nolimits^{i}\mathscr{R}_{+}^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{+}}}\pi_{+}^{*}(\mathscr{M}_{i}(\mathscr{E})),

where
\mathscr{G}_{i}(\mathscr{E})\in\mathrm{Perf}(\mathbb{P}_{+})
,
\mathscr{G}_{-1}(\mathscr{E})=0
,
\mathscr{G}_{n-1}(\mathscr{E})\simeq\Phi(\iota_{-}^{*}\mathscr{E})
, and

\mathscr{M}_{i}(\mathscr{E})=\Sigma^{i}\,\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{W}^{\vee})}(i),\mathscr{E})\in\mathrm{Perf}(X).

(2)

Let
\Phi^{L}=r_{-\,!}\,r_{+}^{*}\colon\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}_{+})\to\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}_{-})
denote the left adjoint of
\Phi
. Then for any
\mathscr{E}\in\mathrm{Perf}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}))
, there is a series of fiber sequences in
\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}_{-})
for all
0\leq i\leq m-1
:

\iota_{-}^{*}(\bigwedge\nolimits^{i}\mathscr{R}_{-})\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{-}}}\pi_{-}^{*}(\mathscr{N}^{i}(\mathscr{E}))\to\mathscr{G}^{i}(\mathscr{E})\to\mathscr{G}^{i-1}(\mathscr{E}),

where
\mathscr{G}^{i}(\mathscr{E})\in\mathrm{Perf}(\mathbb{P}_{-})
,
\mathscr{G}^{-1}(\mathscr{E})=0
,
\mathscr{G}^{m-1}(\mathscr{E})\simeq\Phi^{L}(\iota_{+}^{*}\mathscr{E})
, and

\mathscr{N}^{i}(\mathscr{E})=\Omega^{i}(\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(i),\mathscr{E}^{\vee})^{\vee})\in\mathrm{Perf}(X).

In particular:

(i)

If we set
\mathscr{E}=\bigwedge^{i}\mathscr{R}_{+}^{\vee}
,
0\leq i\leq n-1
, then there are canonical equivalences

\Phi^{L}(\iota_{+}^{*}(\bigwedge^{i}\nolimits\mathscr{R}_{+}^{\vee}))\simeq\begin{cases}\iota_{-}^{*}(\bigwedge\nolimits^{i}\mathscr{R}_{-})&\text{if}\quad 0\leq i\leq m-1;\\
0,&\text{if}\quad m\leq i\leq n-1.\end{cases}

(ii)

If an object
\mathscr{E}\in\mathrm{Perf}(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V}))
having the property that

\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(j),\mathscr{E}^{\vee})\simeq 0\quad\text{for}\quad j=0,\ldots,m-1.

Then
\Phi^{L}(\iota_{+}^{*}\mathscr{E})\simeq 0
.

Proof.

We will only prove assertion (
1
); the proof of assertion (
2
) is similar (and simpler). There are commutative diagrams:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}_{+}\times_{X}\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{W}^{\vee})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{+}}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}_{+}\times_{X}\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{W}^{\vee})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}_{-}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{W}^{\vee}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{-}}

where
p_{+}\colon\mathbb{P}_{+}\times_{X}\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{W}^{\vee})\to\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{W}^{\vee})
denotes the natural projection. Therefore, we obtain

\Phi(\iota_{-}^{*}\mathscr{E})=r_{+\,*}r_{-}^{*}\mathscr{E}\simeq q_{+\,*}j_{+\,*}j_{+}^{*}(\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{+}}\boxtimes_{X}\mathscr{E}).

Apply Lemma
4.16
to the quasi-smooth immersion
j_{+}
(which corresponds to a cosection of
\iota_{+}^{*}\mathscr{R}_{+}^{\vee}\boxtimes_{X}\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{W}^{\vee})}(-1)
), we obtain a sequence of fiber sequences on
\mathbb{P}_{+}\times_{X}\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{W}^{\vee})
,
0\leq i\leq n-1
:

\overline{\mathscr{G}}_{i-1}(\mathscr{E})\to\overline{\mathscr{G}}_{i}(\mathscr{E})\to\Sigma^{i}\,\iota_{+}^{*}(\bigwedge\nolimits^{i}\mathscr{R}_{+}^{\vee})\boxtimes_{X}(\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{W}^{\vee})}(-i)\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{W}^{\vee})}}\mathscr{E})

for which
\overline{\mathscr{G}}_{-1}(\mathscr{E})\simeq 0
and
\overline{\mathscr{G}}_{n-1}(\mathscr{E})\simeq j_{+\,*}\,j_{+}^{*}(\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{+}}\boxtimes_{X}\mathscr{E})
. From the pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}_{+}\times_{X}\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{W}^{\vee})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{W}^{\vee})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}_{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{W}^{\vee})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{+}}

we obtain that there are canonical equivalences

\displaystyle q_{+*}\big(\Sigma^{i}\,\iota_{+}^{*}(\bigwedge\nolimits^{i}\mathscr{R}_{+}^{\vee})\boxtimes_{X}(\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{W}^{\vee})}(-i)\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{W}^{\vee})}}\mathscr{E})\big)

\displaystyle\simeq\Sigma^{i}\iota_{+}^{*}(\bigwedge\nolimits^{i}\mathscr{R}_{+}^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{+}}}\pi_{+}^{*}\big(\mathrm{pr}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{W}^{\vee})\,*}(\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{W}^{\vee})}(-i)\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{W}^{\vee})}}\mathscr{E})\big)

\displaystyle\simeq\iota_{+}^{*}(\bigwedge\nolimits^{i}\mathscr{R}_{+}^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{+}}}\pi_{+}^{*}\big(\Sigma^{i}\,\underline{\mathrm{Map}}_{X}(\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{W}^{\vee})}(i),\mathscr{E})\big).

Therefore, by pushing-forward the above fiber sequence along
q_{+}
and setting
\mathscr{G}_{i}(\mathscr{E})=q_{+\,*}\overline{\mathscr{G}}_{i}(\mathscr{E})\in\mathrm{Perf}(\mathbb{P}_{+})
, we obtain the desired fiber sequences of assertion (
1
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
7.5
in the local situation.

We first show that the functor
\Phi
is fully faithful. By virtue of Lemma
7.11
, it suffices to show that the natural transformation

\Phi^{L}\circ\Phi(\mathscr{E}_{i})\to\mathscr{E}_{i}

is an equivalence for all
\mathscr{E}_{i}=\iota_{-}^{*}(\bigwedge^{i}\mathscr{R}_{-})
, where
0\leq i\leq m-1
. By virtue of Lemma
7.12
(
1
) (applied to
\mathscr{E}=\bigwedge^{i}\mathscr{R}_{-}
) we obtain canonical equivalences
\mathscr{G}_{n-1}(\bigwedge^{i}\mathscr{R}_{-})\simeq\Phi(\mathscr{E}_{i})
and

\displaystyle\mathscr{G}_{0}(\bigwedge\nolimits^{i}\mathscr{R}_{-})\simeq\ldots\simeq\mathscr{G}_{i-1}(\bigwedge\nolimits^{i}\mathscr{R}_{-})\simeq 0;

\displaystyle\mathscr{G}_{i}(\bigwedge\nolimits^{i}\mathscr{R}_{-})\simeq\ldots\simeq\mathscr{G}_{m-1}(\bigwedge\nolimits^{i}\mathscr{R}_{-})\simeq\iota_{+}^{*}(\bigwedge\nolimits^{i}\mathscr{R}_{+}^{\vee}).

Furthermore, for
0\leq j\leq n-m-1
, there are fiber sequences in
\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}_{+})
:

\mathscr{G}_{m-1+j}(\bigwedge\nolimits^{i}\mathscr{R}_{-})\to\mathscr{G}_{m+j}(\bigwedge\nolimits^{i}\mathscr{R}_{-})\to\iota_{+}^{*}(\bigwedge\nolimits^{m+j}\mathscr{R}_{+}^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{+}}}\pi_{+}^{*}(\mathscr{M}_{m+j}(\bigwedge\nolimits^{i}\mathscr{R}_{-})).

By virtue of Lemma
7.12
(
2i
) (applied to the case where
\mathscr{E}=\bigwedge\nolimits^{m+j}\mathscr{R}_{+}^{\vee}
), we obtain

\Phi^{L}\big(\iota_{+}^{*}(\bigwedge\nolimits^{m+j}\mathscr{R}_{+}^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{+}}}\pi_{+}^{*}(\mathscr{M}_{m+j}(\bigwedge\nolimits^{i}\mathscr{R}_{-}))\big)\simeq 0\quad\text{for}\quad 0\leq j\leq n-m-1.

Therefore, we obtain canonical equilvalences

\Phi^{L}\circ\Phi(\mathscr{E}_{i})=\Phi^{L}(\mathscr{G}_{n-1}(\bigwedge^{i}\mathscr{R}_{-}))\simeq\ldots\simeq\Phi^{L}(\mathscr{G}_{m-1}(\bigwedge^{i}\mathscr{R}_{-}))\simeq\Phi^{L}(\iota_{+}^{*}(\bigwedge\nolimits^{i}\mathscr{R}_{+}^{\vee}))\simeq\mathscr{E}_{i},

where the last equivalence follows from Lemma
7.12
(
2i
). This proves that
\Phi
is fully faithful.

Next, we prove that the essential images of
\Phi
and
\Psi_{j}
are semiorthogonal, where
1\leq j\leq\delta
. For all
\mathscr{A}\in\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
and
\mathscr{B}\in\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}_{-})
, we wish to show that the mapping space

\mathrm{Map}_{\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}_{+})}(\Psi_{j}(\mathscr{A}),\Phi(\mathscr{B}))\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}_{-})}(\Phi^{L}(\pi_{+}^{*}(\mathscr{A})\otimes\mathscr{O}_{+}(j)),\mathscr{B})

is contractible. Since
\Phi^{L}(\pi_{+}^{*}(\mathscr{A})\otimes\mathscr{O}_{+}(j)\simeq\pi_{+}^{*}(\mathscr{A})\otimes\Phi^{L}(\iota_{+}^{*}(\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(j)))
, it suffices to show

\Phi^{L}(\iota_{+}^{*}(\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(j)))\simeq 0\qquad\text{for all}\qquad 1\leq j\leq n-m.

This follows directly from Lemma
7.12
(
2ii
).

Finally, let
\mathcal{C}
denote the smallest stable subcategory of
\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}_{+})
that contains the essential images
\operatorname{Im}\Phi
and
\{\operatorname{Im}\Psi_{j}\}_{1\leq j\leq\delta}
. We wish to show that
\mathcal{C}=\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}_{+})
. By virtue of Lemma
7.11
, it suffices to show that for each
0\leq i\leq n-1
,
\iota_{+}^{*}(\bigwedge^{i}\mathscr{R}_{+}^{\vee})\in\mathcal{C}
. Since
\mathscr{R}_{+}
is a vector bundle on
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})
of rank
(n-1)
, we obtain that for all
0\leq j\leq\delta-1
,

\bigwedge\nolimits^{m+j}\mathscr{R}_{+}^{\vee}\simeq(\bigwedge\nolimits^{\delta-1-j}\mathscr{R}_{+})\otimes\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(1)\otimes(\mathrm{pr}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}^{*}\det\mathscr{V}).

By virtue of Proposition
6.19
, the sequence of vector bundles

\bigwedge\nolimits^{\delta-1}\mathscr{R}_{+},\ldots,\mathscr{R}_{+},\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}

is left dual to the relative exceptional sequence
\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})},\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(1),\ldots,\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(\delta-1)
. Therefore, by virtue of Remark
6.18
, each
\bigwedge\nolimits^{m+j}\mathscr{R}_{+}^{\vee}
is an iterated extensions of objects of the form
\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(s)\otimes(\mathrm{pr}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}^{*}\mathscr{F}_{s})
, where
1\leq s\leq\delta
and
\mathscr{F}_{s}\in\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
. In particular, we obtain that for all
\mathscr{M}\in\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
,

\iota_{+}^{*}(\bigwedge\nolimits^{m+j}\mathscr{R}_{+}^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{+}}}(\pi_{+}^{*}\mathscr{M})\in\mathcal{C}\quad\text{for}\quad 0\leq j\leq\delta-1.

On the other hand, if we consider
\Phi(\mathscr{E}_{i})
, where
\mathscr{E}_{i}=\iota_{-}^{*}(\bigwedge^{i}\mathscr{R}_{-})
, then
\mathscr{G}_{n-1}(\bigwedge^{i}\mathscr{R}_{-})\simeq\Phi(\mathscr{E}_{i})\in\mathcal{C}
by construction. By inverse induction and the fact that
\mathcal{C}
is stable under the formation of fibers, we obtain that
\mathscr{G}_{m+k-1}(\bigwedge^{i}\mathscr{R}_{-})\in\mathcal{C}
for all
0\leq k\leq\delta
. In particular, we obtain

\iota_{+}^{*}(\bigwedge\nolimits^{i}\mathscr{R}_{+}^{\vee})\simeq\mathscr{G}_{m-1}(\bigwedge\nolimits^{i}\mathscr{R}_{-})\in\mathcal{C}

for all
0\leq i\leq m-1
. This proves
\mathcal{C}=\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{P}_{+})
. Combined with the proof of the preceding subsection, this completes the proof of Theorem
7.5
.
∎

7.3.
Applications to classical examples

In this subsection, we apply Theorem
7.5
to classical examples. We only list examples in lower dimensions in each situation; all these results have direct analogues in higher dimensions.

7.3.1.
Criteria for derived projectivizations and symmetric powers being classical

We first give criteria for the derived projectivizations and symmetric powers being classical. For simplicity of exposition, we make the following assumption throughout this subsection:

(\star)

We let
X
be an integral Cohen–Macaulay scheme, let
\mathscr{W}
and
\mathscr{V}
be vector bundles on
X
of rank
m
and
n
where
1\leq m\leq n
, and let
\sigma\colon\mathscr{W}\to\mathscr{V}
be a map of
\mathscr{O}_{X}
-modules which is injective at the generic point of
X
. Let
\mathscr{F}=\operatorname{cofib}(\mathscr{W}\xrightarrow{\sigma}\mathscr{V})\in\mathrm{Perf}(X)^{\mathrm{cn}}
.

Under assumption (

⋆

‣
7.3.1
),
\Sigma\mathscr{F}^{\vee}
is represented by the shifted dual complex

\Sigma\mathscr{F}^{\vee}\simeq\operatorname{cofib}(\mathscr{V}^{\vee}\xrightarrow{\sigma^{\vee}}\mathscr{W}^{\vee})\in\mathrm{Perf}(X)^{\mathrm{cn}}.

(Thus by possibly replacing
\mathscr{F}
with
\Sigma\mathscr{F}^{\vee}
, there is no loss of generality by assuming
m\leq n
.)

Remark 7.13
.

The assumption (

⋆

‣
7.3.1
) is
not
necessary: all the results of this section remain true for a general scheme
X
and a discrete finite type quasi-coherent sheaf
\mathscr{F}
on
X
of homological dimension
\leq 1
, as long as one replaces the conditions on codimensions of degeneracy loci with conditions on polynomial depths of the corresponding Fitting ideals; see
[
J21
, §2]
.

Notation 7.14
.

Under assumption (

⋆

‣
7.3.1
), for each integer
i\geq 0
, we let
X_{i}\subseteq X
denote the closed subscheme of
X
defined by all
(m+1-i)\times(m+1-i)
-minors of the map
\sigma
; Equivalently,
X_{i}
is
i
-th degeneracy locus
of the discrete sheaf
\operatorname{Coker}(\sigma)
, characterized by the following property: for each point
x\in X_{i}
(closed or not),
\operatorname{rank}(\operatorname{Coker}(\sigma)|_{x})\geq n-m+i
. Therefore we have a sequence of closed subschemes of
X
:

\emptyset=X_{m+1}\subseteq X_{m}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq X_{i}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq X_{1}\subseteq X.

For a nonempty closed subscheme
Z\subseteq X
, we let
\operatorname{codim}_{Z}(X)
denote the minimal of the codimensions of irreducible components of
Z
inside
X
(since
X
is Noetherian and equidimensional,
\operatorname{codim}_{Z}(X)
is well-defined). In particular, for an integer
d
,
\operatorname{codim}_{X}(Z)\geq d
holds if and only if
\operatorname{codim}_{X}(Z_{j})\geq d
holds for each irreducible component
Z_{j}
of
Z
.

Remark 7.15

(Expected codimensions)
.

In the situation of Notation
7.14
, a theorem of Macaulay–Eagon–Northcott implies that for any irreducible component
Z
of
X_{i}
,

\operatorname{codim}_{X}(Z)\leq(n-m+i)i\qquad i=1,2,\ldots,m.

Moreover, a theorem of Hochster–Eagon implies that, if
\operatorname{codim}_{X}(X_{i})
achieves the maximal possible value
(n-m+i)i
(that is,
X_{i}
is equidimensional and each irreducible component of
X_{i}
has codimension
(n-m+i)i
in
X
), then
X_{i}
is a Cohen-Macaulay subscheme (see, for example,
[
Ei
, Exercise 10.9, §18.5, & Theorem 18.18]
for these statements for commutative rings, and
[
J21
, §2.2]
for a summary in the geometric situation). The maximal possible value
(n-m+i)i
for
\operatorname{codim}_{X}(X_{i})
is often referred to as the
expected codimension of
i
-th degneracy locus
X_{i}
in
X
; it is achieved in the situation when
\sigma
is a “generic” map.

First, we give criteria for the derived projectivizations to be classical.

Lemma 7.16
.

Under assumption (

⋆

‣
7.3.1
) and let
X_{i}
be defined as in Notation
7.14
. Then:

(1)

\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
is classical if and only if the following condition is satisfied:

(
a
)

\operatorname{codim}_{X}(X_{i})\geq i\quad\text{for}\quad i=1,2,\ldots,m.

If
\operatorname{rk}\mathscr{F}=n-m\geq 1
, then
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
is classical and irreducible if and only if the following condition is satisfied:

(
a^{\prime}
)

\operatorname{codim}_{X}(X_{i})\geq i+1\quad\text{for}\quad i=1,2,\ldots,m.

(2)

\mathbb{P}(\Sigma\mathscr{F}^{\vee})
is classical if and only if the following condition is satisfied:

(
b
)

\operatorname{codim}_{X}(X_{i})\geq n-m+i\quad\text{for}\quad i=1,2,\ldots,m.

\mathbb{P}(\Sigma\mathscr{F}^{\vee})
is classical and irreducible if and only if the following condition is satisfied:

(
b^{\prime}
)

\operatorname{codim}_{X}(X_{1})=n-m+1\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{codim}_{X}(X_{i})\geq n-m+i+1\quad\text{for}\quad i=2,\ldots,m.

(3)

Let
\widehat{Z}
be the universal incidence locus of
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
and
\mathbb{P}(\Sigma\mathscr{F}^{\vee})
over
X
of Notation
7.4
. Then
\widehat{Z}
is classical if and only if the following condition is satisfied:

(
c
)

\operatorname{codim}_{X}(X_{i})\geq n-m+2i-1\quad\text{for}\quad i=1,2,\ldots,m.

\widehat{Z}
is classical and irreducible if and only if the following condition is satisfied:

(
c^{\prime}
)

\operatorname{codim}_{X}(X_{1})=n-m+1\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{codim}_{X}(X_{i})\geq n-m+2i\quad\text{for}\quad i=2,\ldots,m.

(Notice that if
\widehat{Z}
is classical, then
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
and
\mathbb{P}(\Sigma\mathscr{F}^{\vee})
are classical, and
\widehat{Z}
is canonically equivalent to the classical fiber product
(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})\times_{X}\mathbb{P}(\Sigma\mathscr{F}^{\vee}))_{\mathrm{cl}}
.)

Proof.

We first prove the assertions about
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
. By virtue of Proposition
4.33
, the closed immersion
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})
is the derived zero locus of a section
s
of the vector bundle
\mathscr{W}^{\vee}\otimes\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(1)
. Since
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})
is an integral Cohen–Macaulay scheme, therefore for each irreducible component
Z
of
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{cl}}(\mathscr{F})
,
\operatorname{codim}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(Z)\leq m
. Moreover,
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
is classical if and only if
s
is a regular section, if and only if
\operatorname{codim}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{cl}}(\mathscr{F}))\geq m
.
Since the restriction of the projection from the classical projectivization
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{cl}}(\mathscr{F})\to X
is smooth with fiber
\mathbb{P}^{n-m+i-1}
over the subscheme
X_{i}\backslash X_{i+1}
(Remark
4.22
), it follows that
\operatorname{codim}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})}(\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{cl}}(\mathscr{F}))\geq m
if and only if condition (

a

) is satisfied. If we assume
n-m\geq 1
and
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
is classical, then
\mathrm{pr}^{-1}(X\backslash X_{1})
is a
\mathbb{P}^{n-m-1}
-bundle over
X\backslash X_{1}
. Hence
\mathrm{pr}^{-1}(X\backslash X_{1})
has codimension
m
inside
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})
. Therefore, in this case,
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
is irreducible if and only if (

a
′

) is satisfied. The same argument proves the assertions about
\mathbb{P}(\Sigma\mathscr{F}^{\vee})
. By virtue of Lemma
7.3
, the assertions about
\widehat{Z}
can be proved in a similar manner.
∎

Remark 7.17
.

In the situation of Lemma
7.16
:

•

If
\operatorname{rk}\mathscr{F}=n-m\geq 1
, then we have the following implications:

\eqref{eqn:condition:PF}\impliedby\eqref{eqn:condition:irr:PF}\impliedby\eqref{eqn:condition:PK}\impliedby\eqref{eqn:condition:irr:PK}\impliedby\eqref{eqn:condition:hatZ}\impliedby\eqref{eqn:condition:irr:hatZ}.

In general, each of the above implications “
\impliedby
” is
strict
(for example, if
\operatorname{rk}\mathscr{F}
and
m
are large enough, say, if
\operatorname{rk}\mathscr{F}\geq 2
and
m\geq 3
).

•

If
\operatorname{rk}\mathscr{F}=n-m=0
, then we have the following implications:

\eqref{eqn:condition:PF}\iff\eqref{eqn:condition:PK}\impliedby\eqref{eqn:condition:irr:PK}\impliedby\eqref{eqn:condition:hatZ}\impliedby\eqref{eqn:condition:irr:hatZ}.

In general, each of the above implications “
\impliedby
” is
strict
(for example, if
m\geq 3
).

It is easy to construct numerous counter-examples for the inverse of eahc of the above implications “
\impliedby
”, and we will consider some of such examples in the next subsection.

Next, we give criteria for the derived symmetric powers to be classical.

Lemma 7.18
.

Under assumption (

⋆

‣
7.3.1
) and let
X_{i}
be defined as in Notation
7.14
. Then:

(1)

Let
d\geq 0
be an integer. Then the canonical map

\operatorname{Sym}^{d}_{X}(\mathscr{F})\to\pi_{0}(\operatorname{Sym}^{d}_{X}(\mathscr{F}))\simeq S^{d}_{X}(\operatorname{Coker}(\sigma))

is an equivalence if and only if the following condition is satisfied:

\operatorname{codim}_{X}(X_{i})\geq i\quad\text{for}\quad i=1,2,\ldots,\min\{m,d\}.

(2)

Let
d\geq 0
be an integer.

(i)

If
d\geq n-m+1
. Then the canonical map

\operatorname{Sym}^{d}_{X}(\Sigma\mathscr{F}^{\vee})\to\pi_{0}(\operatorname{Sym}^{d}_{X}(\Sigma\mathscr{F}^{\vee}))\simeq S^{d}_{X}(\operatorname{Coker}(\sigma^{\vee}))

is an equivalence if and only if the following condition is satisfied:

\operatorname{codim}_{X}(X_{i})\geq n-m+i\quad\text{for}\quad i=1,2,\ldots,\min\{m,d\}.

(ii)

If
0\leq d\leq n-m
. Assume that the following condition is satisfied:

\operatorname{codim}_{X}(X_{1})=n-m+1.

Then there are canonical equivalences

\pi_{i}(\operatorname{Sym}^{d}_{X}(\Sigma\mathscr{F}^{\vee}))\simeq\begin{cases}\operatorname{Sym}^{d}_{\mathrm{cl}}(\operatorname{Coker}(\sigma^{\vee}))&i=0.\\
\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-m-d}_{\mathrm{cl}}(\operatorname{Coker}(\sigma))\otimes(\det\mathscr{F})^{\vee}&i=d.\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Proof.

We first prove assertion (
1
). We will assume
n>m
, since the case
n=m
can be reduced to assertion (
2
). By virtue of Corollary
2.30
,
\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{d}(\mathscr{F})
is canonically represented by the complex of vector bundles
\mathbf{S}^{d}(X,\mathscr{W}\xrightarrow{\sigma}\mathscr{V})
(defined by formula (
2.2
)). For each
0\leq i\leq\min\{m,d\}
, we let
F_{i}
denote the
i
th term of the complex
\mathbf{S}^{d}(X,\sigma)
, let
d_{i}\colon F_{i}\to F_{i-1}
denote the differential of
\mathbf{S}^{d}(X,\sigma)
, and let
r_{i}=\sum_{j=i}^{d}(-1)^{j-i}\operatorname{rank}F_{j}
be the “expected rank” of
d_{i}
. Let
\mathscr{I}_{X_{i}}
denote the ideal of
X_{i}\subseteq X
, and let
I_{r_{i}}(d_{i})
denote the ideal generated by
(r_{i}\times r_{i})
-minors of the differential
d_{i}
(see
[
Ei
, §20.2]
). From the same argument as the proof of
[
Ei
, Theorem A2.1]
, we know that

\sqrt{I_{r_{i}}(d_{i})}=\sqrt{\mathscr{I}_{X_{i}}}\quad\text{for}\quad i=1,2,\ldots,\min\{m,d\}.

(This can also be deduced from the general result
[
AT19
, Theorem 6.1
(b)
]
). In particular,

0pt(I_{r_{i}}(d_{i}))=0pt(\mathscr{I}_{X_{i}})=\operatorname{codim}_{X}(X_{i})

since
X
is Cohen–Macaulay. On the other hand, from Buchsbaum–Eisenbud’s acyclicity criterion (
[
Ei
, Theorem 20.9]
),
\mathbf{S}^{d}(X,\sigma)
is a resolution of
S^{d}_{X}(\operatorname{Coker}(\sigma))
if and only if

0pt(I_{r_{i}}(d_{i}))\geq i\quad\text{for}\quad i=1,2,\ldots,\min\{m,d\}.

This proves assertion (
1
) in the case where
n>m
.

We next prove assertion (
2i
).
By virtue of Corollary
2.30
,
\operatorname{Sym}_{X}^{d}(\Sigma\mathscr{F}^{\vee})
is canonically represented by
\mathbf{S}^{d}(X,\mathscr{V}^{\vee}\xrightarrow{\sigma^{\vee}}\mathscr{V}^{\vee})
. For each
0\leq i\leq\min\{m,d\}
, we let
F_{i}^{\prime}
denote the
i
th term of the complex
\mathbf{S}^{d}(X,\sigma^{\vee})
, let
d_{i}^{\prime}\colon F_{i}^{\prime}\to F_{i-1}^{\prime}
denote the differential, and let
r_{i}^{\prime}=\sum_{j=i}^{d}(-1)^{j-i}\operatorname{rank}F_{j}^{\prime}
. From the proof of
[
Ei
, Theorem A2.1]
, we know that

\sqrt{I_{r_{i}^{\prime}}(d_{i}^{\prime})}=\begin{cases}\sqrt{\mathscr{I}_{X_{1}}}&\text{if}\quad 1\leq i\leq n-m+1\\
\sqrt{\mathscr{I}_{X_{i-n+m}}}&\text{if}\quad n-m+2\leq i\leq\min\{m,d\}.\end{cases}

(This can also be deduced from
[
AT19
, Theorem 6.1
(a)
&
(b)
]
.) Therefore,

0pt(I_{r_{i}^{\prime}}(d_{i}^{\prime}))=\begin{cases}\operatorname{codim}_{X}(X_{1})&\text{if}\quad 1\leq i\leq n-m+1\\
\operatorname{codim}_{X}(X_{i-n+m})&\text{if}\quad n-m+2\leq i\leq\min\{m,d\},\end{cases}

since
X
is Cohen–Macaulay. By Buchsbaum–Eisenbud’s acyclicity criterion again, we know that
\mathbf{S}^{d}(X,\sigma^{\vee})
is a resolution of
S_{X}^{d}(\operatorname{Coker}(\sigma^{\vee}))
if and only if

0pt(I_{r_{i}^{\prime}}(d_{i}^{\prime}))\geq i\quad\text{for}\quad i=1,2,\ldots,\min\{m,d\}.

This proves assertion (
2i
) (and assertion (
1
) in the case where
n=m
).

Finally, to prove assertion (
2ii
), we consider the Eagon–Northcott complex
\mathbf{EN}_{d}(X,\sigma)
of Remark
5.9
. Similarly as above, the desired assertions follow from
[
Ei
, Theorem A2.1]
(where the complex
\mathcal{C}^{i}
there is our
\mathbf{EN}_{i}(X,\sigma)
) and Buchsbaum–Eisenbud’s acyclicity criterion.
∎

7.3.2.
Examples violating (

a

)

By virtue of Lemma
7.16
, as long as the pair
(X,\mathscr{F})
in the situation of (

⋆

‣
7.3.1
) violates the condition (

a

),
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
is not classical. For example:

Example 7.19
.

Let
X=\mathbb{A}^{1}=\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}[x]
, and
\mathscr{F}=\mathscr{O}_{\mathbf{0}}^{\oplus k}
, where
\mathbf{0}\in\mathbb{A}^{1}
is the closed point define by the equation
x=0
,
k\geq 1
is an integer, and
\mathscr{O}_{\mathbf{0}}
is the structure sheaf of
\mathbf{0}
regarded as a discrete
\mathscr{O}_{X}
-module. In this case,

(X_{1})_{{\operatorname{red}}}=\cdots=(X_{k-1})_{{\operatorname{red}}}=X_{k}=\{\mathbf{0}\}.

Therefore, by virtue of Lemma
7.16
and Lemma
7.18
, we obtain:

•

If
k=1
, then

\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{O}_{\mathbf{0}})\simeq\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{cl}}(\mathscr{O}_{\mathbf{0}})\simeq\{\mathbf{0}\}\subseteq\mathbb{A}^{1}

is classical, and there is a canonical isomorphism

\operatorname{Sym}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}^{d}(\mathscr{O}_{\mathbf{0}})\simeq\operatorname{Sym}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathbb{A}^{1}}^{d}(\mathscr{O}_{\mathbf{0}})

for all integers
d\geq 0
;

•

If
k\geq 2
, then
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{O}_{\mathbf{0}})
is not classical, and the canonical map

\operatorname{Sym}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}^{d}(\mathscr{O}_{\mathbf{0}}^{\oplus k})\to\operatorname{Sym}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathbb{A}^{1}}^{d}(\mathscr{O}_{\mathbf{0}}^{\oplus k})

is not an equivalence as long as
d\geq 2
.

More concretely, we can identify the classical projectivization with a projective space

\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{cl}}(\mathscr{O}_{\mathbf{0}}^{\oplus k})\simeq\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{k-1}\to\{\mathbf{0}\}\subseteq\mathbb{A}^{1}.

On the other hand, by virtue of Proposition
4.33
, we can identify the derived projectivization
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{O}_{\mathbf{0}}^{\oplus k})
as the derived zero locus of
\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{k-1}=\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}[x]\times\operatorname{Proj}\mathbb{Z}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{k}]
defined by the
k
equations
xX_{1}=xX_{2}=\cdots=xX_{k}=0
. This derived zero locus has underlying classical scheme
\{\mathbf{0}\}\times\mathbb{P}^{k-1}
defined by the equation
x=0
; but as long as
k\geq 2
, it is equipped with a non-trivial derived structure.

7.3.3.
Examples satisfying (

a

) but violating (

a
′

) and (

b

)

By virtue of Lemma
7.16
, as long as the pair
(X,\mathscr{F})
in the situation of (

⋆

‣
7.3.1
) satisfies condition (

a

) but violate condition (

a
′

) and (

b

),
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
is classical but reducible, and
\mathbb{P}(\Sigma\mathscr{F}^{\vee})
is not classical. There are numerous examples.

Example 7.20
.

Let
X=\mathbb{A}^{1}=\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}[x]
, and let
\mathscr{F}
denote the cokernel of the map

\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}\xrightarrow{(x,x)^{T}}\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}^{\oplus 2}.

•

By virtue of Lemma
7.16
,
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
is classical but
\mathbb{P}(\Sigma\mathscr{F}^{\vee})
is not.

•

By virtue of Lemma
7.18
, the canonical map

\operatorname{Sym}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}^{d}(\mathscr{F})\to\operatorname{Sym}_{\mathrm{cl},\mathbb{A}^{1}}^{d}(\mathscr{F})

is an equivalence for every
d\geq 0
, but the canonical map
\operatorname{Sym}^{d}(\Sigma\mathscr{F}^{\vee})\to\operatorname{Sym}_{\mathrm{cl}}^{d}(\mathscr{O}_{\mathbf{0}})
is
not
an equivalence as long as
d\geq 1
(the case
d\geq 2
is Lemma
7.18
(
2i
) and the case
d=1
is obvious).

More concretely,
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})\simeq\mathbb{A}^{1}\coprod_{\rm pt}\mathbb{P}^{1}
is a nodal curve which is the union of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
and
\mathbb{P}^{1}
along a point, and
\mathbb{P}(\Sigma\mathscr{F}^{\vee})
is isomorphic the derived zero locus in
\mathbb{A}^{1}
which is “cut out by the equation
(x=0)

twice
” (Proposition
4.33
). By virtue of Example
4.35
,
\mathbb{P}(\Sigma\mathscr{F}^{\vee})\simeq\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}[\varepsilon]
, where
\mathbb{Z}[\varepsilon]=\operatorname{Sym}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{*}(\Sigma\,\mathbb{Z})\simeq\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\varepsilon
is the ring of
derived
dual numbers (where we informally think of
\varepsilon
as the generator in degree
1
; beware that
\mathbb{Z}[\varepsilon]
is
not
isomorphic to the classical dual number
\mathbb{Z}[x]/(x^{2})
). In particular,
\mathbb{P}(\Sigma\mathscr{F}^{\vee})
has underlying classical scheme
\{\mathbf{0}\}=\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}\simeq\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}[x]/(x)
, but with a non-trivial derived structure given by the extra equation
(x=0)
.

Moreover, by virtue of Theorem
7.5
, we obtain semiorthogonal decompositions

\displaystyle\operatorname{QCoh}\big(\mathbb{A}^{1}\coprod\nolimits_{\rm pt}\mathbb{P}^{1}\big)

\displaystyle=\big\langle\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{A}^{1}),~\operatorname{QCoh}(\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}[\varepsilon])\big\rangle,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Perf}\big(\mathbb{A}^{1}\coprod\nolimits_{\rm pt}\mathbb{P}^{1}\big)

\displaystyle=\big\langle\mathrm{Perf}(\mathbb{A}^{1}),~\mathrm{Perf}(\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}[\varepsilon])\big\rangle.

We could consider the following global version of Example
7.20
:

Example 7.21

(Reducible schemes)
.

Let
X
be a scheme, let
D\subseteq X
be an effective Cartier divisor, and let
f\in\operatorname{H}^{0}(X,\mathscr{O}(D))
be the section determined by
D
. Let
\mathscr{F}=\mathscr{O}_{X}\oplus\mathscr{O}_{D}
. Then:

•

By virtue of Proposition
4.33
,
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})\subseteq X\times\mathbb{P}^{1}=X\times\operatorname{Proj}\mathbb{Z}[X_{0},X_{1}]
is the divisor defined by the equation
(X_{1}f=0)
. Spelling this out, we see that
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
is classical and is union of
X
and
\mathbb{P}_{D}^{1}=D\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
along the divisor
D
.

•

By virtue of Example
4.35
, we obtain
\mathbb{P}(\Sigma\mathscr{F}^{\vee})\simeq\mathbb{V}_{D}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{O}_{D}(-D))
is not classical (and is equipped with a nontrivial derived structure everywhere).
More concretely, in any open subset
U\subseteq X
for which
\mathscr{O}_{X}(-D)|_{U}
is a trivial line bundle, there is an equivalence
\mathbb{P}_{U}(\Sigma\mathscr{F}^{\vee})\simeq(D\cap U)\times\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}[\varepsilon]
, where
\mathbb{Z}[\varepsilon]=\operatorname{Sym}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{*}(\Sigma\mathbb{Z})
is the ring of derived dual numbers.
More informally, we could think of
\mathbb{V}_{D}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{O}_{D}(-D))
as the total space
{\rm Tot}(\mathcal{N}_{D/X}[-1])
of
(-1)
-shifted normal bundle of
D
in
X
, in the sense
[
ACH
,
PTVV
]
.

By virtue of Theorem
7.5
, we obtain semiorthogonal decompositions

\displaystyle\operatorname{QCoh}\big(X\coprod\nolimits_{D}\mathbb{P}_{D}^{1}\big)

\displaystyle=\big\langle\operatorname{QCoh}(X),~\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathbb{V}_{D}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{O}_{D}(-D)))\big\rangle,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Perf}\big(X\coprod\nolimits_{D}\mathbb{P}_{D}^{1}\big)

\displaystyle=\big\langle\mathrm{Perf}(X),~\mathrm{Perf}(\mathbb{V}_{D}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{O}_{D}(-D)))\big\rangle.

Example 7.22

(Attaching
\mathbb{P}^{1}
’s to smooth points of curves)
.

In the above Example
7.21
, if we let
X=C
be a reduced curve over a field
\kappa
(that is, a reduced separated finite type scheme over
\kappa
whose irreducible components all have dimension
1
), and let
p\in C(\kappa)
be a non-singular rational point, and let
\mathscr{F}=\mathscr{O}_{C}\oplus\mathscr{O}_{p}
, then we obtain semiorthogonal decompositions

\displaystyle\operatorname{QCoh}\big(C\coprod\nolimits_{p}\mathbb{P}^{1}\big)

\displaystyle=\big\langle\operatorname{QCoh}(C),~\operatorname{QCoh}(\operatorname{Spec}\kappa[\varepsilon])\big\rangle,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Perf}\big(C\coprod\nolimits_{p}\mathbb{P}^{1}\big)

\displaystyle=\big\langle\mathrm{Perf}(C),~\mathrm{Perf}(\operatorname{Spec}\kappa[\varepsilon])\big\rangle,

where
\kappa[\varepsilon]=\operatorname{Sym}_{\kappa}(\Sigma\,\kappa)=\mathbb{Z}[\varepsilon]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\kappa
is the ring of derived dual numbers over
\kappa
; These semiorthogonal decompositions agree with the results in
[
Ku21
, Example 5.2, Theorem 5.12]
.

We could also repeat the above process: set
C_{0}=C
,
p_{0}=p
, and let
C_{i}
be a curve obtained by attaching a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
to a nonsingular
\kappa
-point
p_{i-1}\in C_{i-1}(\kappa)
of
C_{i-1}
. Then for an integer
n\geq 1
,
C_{n}
is the union of
C
with finite number of trees of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
’s for which the total number of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
is
n
. We therefore obtain semiorthogonal decompositions:

\displaystyle\operatorname{QCoh}(C_{n})

\displaystyle=\big\langle\operatorname{QCoh}(C),~\text{$n$-copies of $\operatorname{QCoh}(\operatorname{Spec}\kappa[\varepsilon])$}\big\rangle,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Perf}(C_{n})

\displaystyle=\big\langle\mathrm{Perf}(C),~\text{$n$-copies of $\mathrm{Perf}(\operatorname{Spec}\kappa[\varepsilon])$}\big\rangle.

However, the morphism spaces of the latter components depend on the configurations of the trees of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
’s. For example, in the case where
C_{n}
is the union of
C
and a chain
\Gamma_{n}
of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
’s, the endomorphism algebra of the latter component (of a choice of tilting bundle on
\Gamma_{n}
) is described explicitly in
[
Bur
, Theorem 2.1]
. In general, the endomorphism algebras of “the contributions” of the new attached trees of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
’s can be described similarly; see
[
KPS21
, Theorem 4.9]
.

We could also consider the following variant
5
5

5

I thank Michel van Garrel for asking about the relationship between the above example and the schemes in the construction of deformation to normal cones.
of Example
7.21
:

Variant 7.23

(Deformation to normal cones)
.

Similar to Example
7.21
, we let
X
be a scheme and let
D\subseteq X
be an effective Cartier divisor. Now we consider
\mathscr{F}^{\prime}=\mathscr{O}_{X}\oplus\mathscr{O}_{D}(D)
instead of
\mathscr{F}=\mathscr{O}_{X}\oplus\mathscr{O}_{D}
. Then
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}^{\prime})=X\coprod_{D}\mathbb{P}_{D}(\mathcal{N}\oplus\mathscr{O}_{D})
, where
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}_{D/X}
is the normal bundle, and
\mathbb{P}(\Sigma\,\mathscr{F}^{\prime\vee})=D\times\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}[\varepsilon]
. Therefore, Theorem
7.5
implies there are semiorthogonal decompositions

\displaystyle\operatorname{QCoh}\big(X\coprod\nolimits_{D}\mathbb{P}_{D}(\mathcal{N}\oplus\mathscr{O}_{D})\big)

\displaystyle=\big\langle\operatorname{QCoh}(X),~\operatorname{QCoh}(D)\otimes\operatorname{QCoh}(\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}[\varepsilon])\big\rangle,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Perf}\big(X\coprod\nolimits_{D}\mathbb{P}_{D}(\mathcal{N}\oplus\mathscr{O}_{D})\big)

\displaystyle=\big\langle\mathrm{Perf}(X),~\mathrm{Perf}(D)\otimes\mathrm{Perf}(\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}[\varepsilon])\big\rangle.

Notice that the reducible scheme
X\coprod_{D}\mathbb{P}_{D}(\mathcal{N}\oplus\mathscr{O}_{D})
in the above formulae is precisely the special fiber appearing in the construction of deformation to normal cones applied to
D\subseteq X
. More concretely, let
M=\operatorname{Bl}_{D\times\{\infty\}}(X\times\mathbb{P}^{1})
be the total space in the construction of deformation to normal cones of
[
Ful
, §5.1]
, and let
\rho\colon M\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
be the natural projection. Then for
t\neq\infty\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
,
\rho^{-1}(t)\simeq X
, and
\rho^{-1}(\infty)\simeq X\coprod_{D}\mathbb{P}_{D}(\mathcal{N}\oplus\mathscr{O}_{D})\simeq\mathbb{P}_{X}(\mathscr{F}^{\prime})
.

Remark 7.24

(Derived blowups)
.

In view of Remark
7.8
, we could view the semiorthogonal decompositions in Examples
7.21
and
7.23
as
blowup formulae
, where
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})=X\coprod\nolimits_{D}\mathbb{P}_{D}^{1}
and
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F}^{\prime})=X\coprod\nolimits_{D}\mathbb{P}_{D}(\mathcal{N}\oplus\mathscr{O}_{D})
are the derived blowups (in the sense of
[
KR18
,
He21
]
) of
X
along the virtual codimension two closed derived subschemes
\mathbb{P}(\Sigma\mathscr{F}^{\vee})={\rm Tot}(\mathcal{N}_{D/X}[-1])\subseteq X
and
\mathbb{P}(\Sigma\mathscr{F}^{\prime\vee})=D\times\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}[\varepsilon]\subseteq X
, respectively.

If a variety
X
over a field
\kappa
has locally complete intersection singularities, then the cotangent complex
L_{X/\kappa}
has Tor-amplitude
\leq 1
. In many situations, the pair
(X,\mathscr{E}=L_{X/\kappa})
satisfies (

a

). For example, we could consider the cases of nodal curves:

Example 7.25

(Contracting rational bridges)
.

Let
\kappa
be a field, and let
C\subseteq\mathbb{A}_{\kappa}^{2}=\operatorname{Spec}\kappa[x,y]
be the nodal curve defined by the equation
xy=0
. Then the cotangent complex
\mathscr{F}=L_{C/\kappa}\simeq\Omega_{C/\kappa}^{1}
is represented by the complex of locally free sheaves:

\left[\mathscr{O}_{C}\xrightarrow{(y,x)^{T}}\mathscr{O}_{C}{\rm d}x\oplus\mathscr{O}_{C}{\rm d}y\right].

In this case, by virtue of Proposition
4.33
,
\mathbb{P}(L_{C/\kappa})
is the classical reducible scheme defined the equation
yX_{0}+xX_{1}=0
in
C\times\operatorname{Proj}\kappa[X_{0},X_{1}]
.
In particular,
\widetilde{C}=\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{C/\kappa}^{1})
is the total transform of
C\subseteq\mathbb{A}^{2}
along the blowup
\operatorname{Bl}_{0}\mathbb{A}^{2}\to\mathbb{A}^{2}
, where
o=(0,0)
is the origin. Then projection morphism
\pi\colon\widetilde{C}\to C
is an isomorphism over
C\backslash\{o\}
and the exceptional locus
E=\pi^{-1}(\o)

is a projective line
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\kappa}
(called the rational bridge) with multiplicity
two
6
6

6

I thank Martin Kalck for bringing my attention to the multiplicity issue.
. The underlying morphism of reduced schemes
\widetilde{C}^{\rm red}=\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{C/\kappa}^{1})^{\rm red}\to C
is the contraction of the rational bridge
\mathbb{P}_{\kappa}^{1}
.
On the other hand,
\mathbb{P}(\Sigma L_{C/\kappa}^{\vee})
is the derived zero locus in
\mathbb{A}^{2}
cut out by the three equations
x=0,y=0
and
xy=0
, hence we obtain
\mathbb{P}(\Sigma L_{C/\kappa}^{\vee})\simeq\operatorname{Spec}(\kappa[\varepsilon]\otimes_{\kappa}\kappa[\delta])
, where
\kappa[\varepsilon]=\operatorname{Sym}_{\kappa}(\Sigma\,\kappa)=\mathbb{Z}[\varepsilon]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\kappa
is the ring of derived dual numbers over
\kappa
and
\kappa[\delta]=\kappa[\delta]/(\delta^{2})
denote the ring of usual dual numbers. Theorem
7.5
implies semiorthogonal decompositions

\displaystyle\operatorname{QCoh}(\widetilde{C})

\displaystyle=\big\langle\pi^{*}\operatorname{QCoh}(C),~\operatorname{QCoh}\big(\operatorname{Spec}(\kappa[\varepsilon]\otimes_{\kappa}\kappa[\delta])\big)\big\rangle,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Perf}(\widetilde{C})

\displaystyle=\big\langle\pi^{*}\mathrm{Perf}(C),~\mathrm{Perf}\big(\operatorname{Spec}(\kappa[\varepsilon]\otimes_{\kappa}\kappa[\delta])\big)\big\rangle.

One can generalize this example by considering any nodal curve
C/\kappa
(or a family of nodal curves
\mathscr{C}/S
) and the projectivization
\widetilde{C}:=\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{C/\kappa}^{1})\to C
(or
\widetilde{\mathscr{C}}:=\mathbb{P}(L_{\mathscr{C}/S})\to\mathscr{C}
).

Remark 7.26

(Stabilization maps)
.

We could regard the formulae of Examples
7.20
and
7.22
as describing the behavior of the derived categories under “contracting rational tails” (
[
Sta
,
Tag 0E3G
]
).
On the other hand, the formula of Example
7.25
describes the behavior of the derived categories under “contracting non-reduced rational bridges” (see
[
Sta
,
Tag 0E7M
]
for more about the contraction of rational bridges). Notice that a recent result of Kuznetsov and Shinder
[
KS22b
, Corollary 6.8]
implies that there are no semiorthogonal decompositions for the underlying
reduced
scheme
\widetilde{C}^{\rm red}
in Example
7.25
(if
C
is projective).
These results altogether provide a comprehensive picture of the behavior of derived categories of nodal curves under stabilization maps, since the general stabilization maps of nodal curves are composed of operations of contracting rational tails and rational bridges (see, for example,
[
Sta
,
Tag 0E7Q
]
).

We can also consider higher-dimensional variants of Example
7.20
. For example:

Example 7.27
.

Let
X=\mathbb{A}^{2}=\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}[x,y]
, and let
\mathscr{F}
denote the cokernel of the map

\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{2}}^{\oplus 2}\xrightarrow{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}x&0\\
0&y\\
x&y\end{smallmatrix}\big)}\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{2}}^{\oplus 3}.

Then
\operatorname{rank}\mathscr{F}=1
,
\operatorname{codim}_{X}(X_{1})=1
,
\operatorname{codim}_{X}(X_{2})=2
, so by virtue of Lemma
7.16
,
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
is classical but
\mathbb{P}(\Sigma\mathscr{F}^{\vee})
is not. More concretely:

•

\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})\subseteq\mathbb{A}^{2}\times\mathbb{P}^{2}=\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}[x,y]\times\operatorname{Proj}\mathbb{Z}[X,Y,Z]
is the classical closed subscheme defined by the equations
xX+xZ=0
and
yY+yZ=0
. Unwinding the definition, we see that
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})
has four irreducible components:

\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})=\mathbb{A}^{2}\cup(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{x}\times\mathbb{P}^{1})\cup(\{(0,0)\}\times\mathbb{P}^{2})\cup(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{y}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}).

•

\mathbb{P}(\Sigma^{\vee}\mathscr{F})\subseteq\mathbb{A}^{2}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}=\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}[x,y]\times\operatorname{Proj}\mathbb{Z}[U,V]
is the derived closed subscheme defined by the quations
xU=0
,
yV=0
, and
xU+yV=0
. Unwinding the definition, we see that the underlying classical scheme of
\mathbb{P}(\Sigma^{\vee}\mathscr{F})
is the blowup of the nodal curve defined by
(xy=0)
along the nodal point, that is,

\mathbb{P}(\Sigma^{\vee}\mathscr{F})_{\mathrm{cl}}=\mathbb{A}^{1}\coprod_{\rm pt}\mathbb{P}^{1}\coprod_{\rm pt}\mathbb{A}^{1}.

But over each closed point,
\mathbb{P}(\Sigma^{\vee}\mathscr{F})
is equipped with a non-trivial derived structure which is isomorphic to that of the ring of derived dual numbers
\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}[\varepsilon]
:

\mathbb{P}(\Sigma^{\vee}\mathscr{F})\simeq(\mathbb{A}^{1}\coprod_{\rm pt}\mathbb{P}^{1}\coprod_{\rm pt}\mathbb{A}^{1})\times\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}[\varepsilon].

Theorem
7.5
implies there are semiorthogonal decompositions

\displaystyle\operatorname{QCoh}\big(\mathbb{A}^{2}\cup(\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1})\cup(\{(0,0)\}\times\mathbb{P}^{2})\cup(\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1})\big)
